01

What we know so far

This archive begins with the people named by the family and the published history of a Salvadoran aviator whose life intersected with the April 1944 uprising.

Family materials identify José Gavidia and Carlota Castro de Gavidia as the parents of a ten-child family associated with Santa Ana; the 1944 prison registry names Carlos’s parents as José Gavidia and Carlota Castro. Whether Carlota was the same person as Carlota Antonia Castro Bernal remains unconfirmed. Carlos became a military pilot, a husband and father of four—and, at 28, one of the officers executed after the failed uprising against the Hernández Martínez regime.

10children of José and Carlota
4children of Carlos and María Teresa
1937military pilot title earned
1944uprising and execution
02

Why this matters — April 1944

This was not an isolated family tragedy. Carlos and José Antonio were caught in one of the defining confrontations of twentieth-century Salvadoran history.

On Palm Sunday, April 2, 1944, military officers rose against General Maximiliano Hernández Martínez after thirteen years of dictatorship. The failed uprising and the executions that followed helped ignite the nonviolent general strike that forced the dictator from power five weeks later. Documented

How big was it?

The uprising is known as the Palm Sunday Coup or rebelión del 2 de abril de 1944. It failed within days; its leaders were hunted down and tried by Consejos de Guerra. Scholarship based on the certified court-martial file counts 44 death sentences, with 14 carried out—13 military men and one civilian. Scholarship

The reprisals outraged the country. In May, the massive Huelga de Brazos Caídos forced Hernández Martínez to resign on May 9. The event remains one of the best-known episodes in Salvadoran history, and the novel’s epilogue recounts that May strike. DocumentedNovel

The reprisals: a corroborating roster

A later eyewitness recollection by someone who was eight years old in Villa El Rosario in 1944 independently names both brothers—“capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro” and “teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro”—among those shot. It also names Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche; Tenientes Alfonso Marín, Marcelino Calvo, Miguel Ángel Linares, Ricardo Mancía, Oscar Armando Cristales and Edgardo Chacón; Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas; Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo; Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa; and General Alfonso Marroquín. The key names align with the court-martial scholarship. Later eyewitness account

The account also names civilian Víctor Marín, described as “un santo laico,” as shot one April morning; Tenientes Mario Villacorta and Héctor Cárdenas as killed by military patrols; and journalist Jorge Pinto as machine-gunned in his cell. Later eyewitness account

How the strike brought the dictatorship down

AGEUS began the university strike on April 28; factories and rail service stopped on May 2; banks and shops closed May 3; and public employees joined May 4. On May 7, security agent Juan Arnoldo Reyes Baires killed José Wright Alcaine, prompting a protest from U.S. Ambassador Walter Thurston. On May 9, Martínez handed power to Vice President Andrés Ignacio Menéndez and fled to Guatemala. Later eyewitness account

On May 15, 1966, Hernández Martínez was assassinated at his Jamastrán farm in Honduras by his own aide, Cipriano Morales. Later eyewitness account

Look up the event

Use these English and Spanish search terms:

Palm Sunday Couprebelión del 2 de abril de 1944Huelga de Brazos Caídosfusilados de abril de 1944 El Salvador

04

A life in time

Verified history, family records and the novel are deliberately labeled so they can be read together without confusing one for another.

1915

Born November 24

Carlos Gavidia Castro is born in Santa Ana, the second of ten children of José Gavidia and Carlota Castro de Gavidia. Documented

1931

Enters the Escuela Militar

He enters as a cadet on February 13 at age 15. Documented

1933

Graduates subteniente

He is commissioned on October 20 as part of the IV Promoción, the “Cuarta Tanda.” Documented

1936

Selected for aviation

He is chosen for the Aviation School at Ilopango and promoted to teniente on September 29. His aircraft include the WACO CTO, Curtiss-Wright CW-14 “Osprey” and Caproni AP-1. (links show reference photos of each aircraft type)Fue elegido para la Escuela de Aviación de Ilopango y ascendido a teniente el 29 de septiembre. Entre sus aeronaves estaban el WACO CTO, el Curtiss-Wright CW-14 “Osprey” y el Caproni AP-1. (los enlaces muestran fotos de referencia de cada tipo de aeronave) Documented

Dec 2

José and Carlota’s silver anniversary

A family photograph marks their 25th wedding anniversary and names eight of the ten siblings in its caption. Family record

c. 1937

Pilot and civil marriagePiloto y matrimonio civil

Soon after earning his military pilot title, Carlos and María Teresa married civilly. Their first three children were born during this marriage. Her book says they postponed a church wedding because his parents opposed the marriage.Poco después de que Carlos obtuviera su título de piloto militar, él y María Teresa se casaron por lo civil. Sus primeros tres hijos nacieron durante este matrimonio. El libro de ella dice que pospusieron la boda religiosa porque los padres de Carlos se oponían al matrimonio. BookLibro

1938

His uncle is exiled

José Cipriano Castro Bernal is exiled to Guatemala after protesting Hernández Martínez’s reelection. Documented

1939

Forced out of aviation

Amid regime suspicion, Carlos leaves military aviation and manages Haciendas El Ángel and El Carmen in San Juan Opico / San Pablo Tacachico, La Libertad. Documented

1942–43

Returns and becomes captain

He rejoins military aviation in late 1942 and is promoted to capitán on November 12, 1943. Documented

Apr 2

Joins the uprising

After José Antonio persuades him, Carlos joins the revolt against President Maximiliano Hernández Martínez. Blocked from reaching Ilopango, he fights on the ground at Candelaria and Cuartel San Carlos, then retreats west toward Santa Ana. Biography

Apr 10

José Antonio is executed

Teniente José Antonio Gavidia Castro is executed by firing squad with ten other officers. Scholarship / Biography

Apr 1944

Catholic marriage in prisonMatrimonio católico en la prisión

On the morning he was to be executed, Carlos asked to marry María Teresa in the Catholic Church because he did not want to die without that sacrament. The Central Penitentiary registry records the in articulo mortis ceremony at 5:15 a.m. This was their second ceremony—not the beginning of their family.La mañana en que iba a ser ejecutado, Carlos pidió casarse con María Teresa por la Iglesia católica porque no quería morir sin ese sacramento. El registro de la Penitenciaría Central consigna la ceremonia in articulo mortis a las 5:15 a. m. Esta fue su segunda ceremonia, no el comienzo de su familia. Registry

Apr 23

Sentenced to death

The court-martial study names Carlos among those sentenced to death on April 23. Scholarship

Apr 23

Farewell letter

He writes to María Teresa, who is three months pregnant with their fourth child. Biography

“Cuida a nuestros hijos, los tres y el que viene.”

Apr 24

Executed in San Salvador

The family-assisted biography places his execution by firing squad at the Cementerio General at 6:30 a.m. The registry’s separate 5:15 a.m. entry is the marriage ceremony time, not the execution time; some popular summaries say April 26, so the archive preserves the date conflict. Biography / Registry

Apr 28

University strike begins

AGEUS begins the strike; factories and rail service stop May 2, banks and shops close May 3, and public employees join May 4. Later eyewitness account

May 7

José Wright Alcaine is killed

The account names security agent Juan Arnoldo Reyes Baires as the shooter and says U.S. Ambassador Walter Thurston protested. Later eyewitness account

May 9

The Martinato falls

After the Huelga de Brazos Caídos, Hernández Martínez hands power to Vice President Andrés Ignacio Menéndez and flees to Guatemala. Later eyewitness account

1966

Hernández Martínez is assassinated

On May 15, his own aide Cipriano Morales kills him at his Jamastrán farm in Honduras; the book’s epilogue frames it with “el que a hierro mata, a hierro muere.” Later eyewitness account

1987

Pugna a un dictador

María Teresa González publishes her novel-memoir of the uprising, loss and aftermath. Documented

After 1944

The story continues as fiction

The novel follows María’s grief, the fourth child’s birth, a later marriage to Coronel Roberto Ocampo, their children, María’s intervention for condemned officers, years in the United States and a silver anniversary. It closes about thirty years after Carlos’s death. Novel

05

People and their stories

Each person’s dates, role and story are kept together. Source labels separate documented history, family material, literary passages and leads still awaiting proof.

Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro

1915–1944 · military pilot · grandfather
BornNov. 24, 1915 · Santa Ana Biography
ParentsJosé Gavidia + Carlota Castro Registry
CommissionIV Promoción · 1933 Documented
DiedApr. 24, 1944 · San Salvador Biography

Why there are two marriage datesPor qué aparecen dos fechas de matrimonio

Carlos and María Teresa were the same couple throughout. The two dates refer to two different ceremonies: a civil marriage first, then a Catholic marriage seven years later.Carlos y María Teresa fueron la misma pareja en todo momento. Las dos fechas corresponden a dos ceremonias distintas: primero un matrimonio civil y, siete años después, un matrimonio católico.

1 · Around 19371 · Alrededor de 1937
Civil marriageMatrimonio civil

They married civilly shortly after Carlos graduated as a military pilot. Their first three children were born during this marriage. In Pugna a un dictador, María Teresa says the church wedding was postponed because Carlos’s parents opposed the marriage.Se casaron por lo civil poco después de que Carlos se graduara como piloto militar. Sus primeros tres hijos nacieron durante este matrimonio. En Pugna a un dictador, María Teresa cuenta que pospusieron la boda religiosa porque los padres de Carlos se oponían al matrimonio. BookLibro

2 · Morning of his execution, 19442 · Mañana de su ejecución, 1944
Catholic marriage in prisonMatrimonio católico en la prisión

Inside the Central Penitentiary, they married in articulo mortis—at the point of death. The prison registry records the ceremony at 5:15 a.m. Carlos requested it himself because he did not want to die without being married in the Church.Dentro de la Penitenciaría Central, se casaron in articulo mortis, es decir, en peligro de muerte. El registro penitenciario consigna la ceremonia a las 5:15 a. m. Carlos la pidió porque no quería morir sin estar casado por la Iglesia. Registry

Early life and aviation. Born the second of ten children, Carlos entered the Escuela Militar on February 13, 1931, at age 15; became subteniente on October 20, 1933, with the IV Promoción; entered the Aviation School at Ilopango in 1936; and became teniente on September 29. He earned his pilot title January 5, 1937, and flew the WACO CTO, Curtiss-Wright CW-14 “Osprey” and Caproni AP-1. Biography

Family and career. Carlos and María Teresa married civilly around 1937. Their first three children were born during this marriage, and they were expecting a fourth in April 1944. Suspicion surrounding the family led to his forced “voluntary” discharge from aviation in late 1939; he managed Haciendas El Ángel and El Carmen, rejoined aviation in late 1942 flying the Fairchild PT-19 and Vultee BT-13A, and became captain on November 12, 1943. Biography

What he did in April 1944. Persuaded by José Antonio, Carlos joined the uprising. Loyalist roadblocks kept him from Ilopango, so he joined Subteniente Juan Ramón Merino’s armored ground unit and fought around Candelaria and Cuartel San Carlos; the biography finds no evidence that he flew a bombing mission. Retreating toward Santa Ana, his vehicle ran out of fuel near San Andrés. He escaped through cane fields, reached Hacienda El Ángel for clothes and money, and tried to reach Honduras, but his paid guide betrayed him to a National Guard patrol near the border. He was returned to the Central Penitentiary. Biography

Marriage, sentence and execution. On the morning he was to be executed, Carlos asked to marry María Teresa in the Catholic Church because he did not want to die without that sacrament. With special archdiocesan permission, they contracted an in articulo mortis marriage inside the prison. The registry’s 5:15 a.m. notation is the ceremony time, not an execution time. Registry The biography reports an April 22 sentence; the court-martial study lists him among those condemned on April 23. BiographyScholarship The family-assisted biography places his firing-squad execution at the Cementerio General on April 24 at 6:30 a.m.; some popular summaries say April 26, while the novel includes April 10. BiographyNovel

“Perdono a todos mis compañeros que me condenaron a muerte…”“I forgive all my fellow officers who condemned me to death…” · Farewell letter, April 23, preserved by the family Biography

In the novel. The family reads “Capitán Romo” as Carlos’s literary counterpart. Family interpretation

Want to see more? Carlos’s personal effects were reported on display at the Museo Nacional de Aviación in Ilopango in 2021; whether they remain there has not been verified. Biography

Carlos Gavidia II

Son · father · grandfather

First marriage. Carlos married Claudette. Their children are Alicia, Carlos and Susan.

Alicia is the mother of Scotty Peterson. Susan is the mother of Sena Gavidia and Angel Gavidia.

Second marriage. Carlos later married Gloria. Their children are Janet, Carlos Gavidia III and Richard Gavidia; Janet is the older sister of Carlos and Richard.

Janet, Carlos Gavidia III and Richard are the half-siblings of Alicia, Carlos and Susan.

Teniente / Subteniente José Antonio Gavidia Castro

Younger brother · “Rey Antonio” in the family
Family name“Rey Antonio” Family record
RankTeniente / subteniente BiographyScholarship
CapturedAt El Zapote Scholarship
ExecutedApr. 10, 1944 Scholarship

José Antonio persuaded Carlos to join the April 2 uprising. Biography He was captured with Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas while attempting to obtain the surrender of the El Zapote artillery position. Scholarship

He was condemned in the first court-martial and executed by firing squad on April 10, 1944, with ten other officers. The biography and family material call him teniente; the scholarly appendix lists him as subteniente. A later eyewitness roster also names “teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro.” ScholarshipBiographyLater eyewitness account

María Teresa González

Grandmother · wife · author
ParentsSantiago González + Susana Jirón Registry
OriginSan Salvador Registry
ResidenceSan José Guayabal Registry
BookPugna a un dictador · 1987 Documented

The penitentiary registry identifies María Teresa as the legitimate daughter of Santiago González and Susana Jirón, born in San Salvador and resident in San José Guayabal. Registry She married Carlos in 1937 and contracted an in articulo mortis Catholic marriage with him in the Central Penitentiary before his execution. BiographyRegistry

She pleaded for Carlos’s life with help from her cousin Coronel Jorge Tenorio. Novel She was widowed with three children while pregnant with their fourth. Biography She later married again and had Gloria Gavidia Ayala and Herbert Ayala. Family record

In 1987 she published Pugna a un dictador, a novel-memoir of the uprising, loss and aftermath. Its changed names and compressed dates are treated as literature rather than as a court record. DocumentedNovel

José Gavidia + Carlota Castro de Gavidia

Named by family materials · Carlota’s full identity under review
HomeSanta Ana Family record
ChildrenTen Biography
AnniversaryDec. 2, 1936 Family record
Novel names“Don José” + “Doña Carmela” Family interpretation

The 1944 marriage registry names Carlos’s parents as José Gavidia and Carlota Castro; the family portrait identifies them as José and Carlota de Gavidia. Whether she was the same person as “Carlota Antonia Castro Bernal” (1893–1938) remains unconfirmed. Their silver-anniversary portrait is dated December 2, 1936; its family caption identifies eight children and says Carlos is absent. Registry / Family record

The family reads the novel’s “Don José” and “Doña Carmela” as José and Carlota, but that pseudonym mapping is an interpretation, not proof. Family interpretation

UNVERIFIED LEAD. A user-created FamilySearch tree names “Carlota Antonia Castro Bernal” (1893–1938) as José Cipriano Castro Bernal’s sister and may represent Carlota’s full identity. It remains unverified pending civil or church birth and marriage records. The same tree proposes Cipriano Castañeda and Isabel del Carmen Castaneda Bernal as the previous generation, but the surname pattern looks inconsistent and should be treated skeptically. Unverified lead

José Cipriano Castro Bernal

1895–1958 · uncle
Born1895 Documented
Died1958 Documented
Exiled1938 Biography
WorkEconomist Biography

Educated in North America, José Cipriano was an economist and an early friend and collaborator of Hernández Martínez who later opposed reelection. He was exiled in 1938, first to Guatemala and then Mexico. Biography

The family reads the novel’s “Don Juan” as José Cipriano, but the book’s changed names make that a family interpretation rather than documented identification. Family interpretation

UNVERIFIED LEAD. A La Zebra interview identifies Salvadoran anthropologist, writer and linguist Carlo Antonio Castro Guevara as José Cipriano’s son and says the family emigrated to Mexico in 1938 under pressure from the dictatorship. His exact connection to this branch—and therefore whether he was Carlos’s cousin—still needs primary records. Unverified lead

Eldest brother · identity and dates disputed
Family information names him José Gavidia Castro and says he died in 2016 at age 105. Separate legal notices use Juan José Gavidia Castro and give September 19, 2016 at age 104 or July 30, 2017. A civil record is still needed to resolve the full name, age and date. Conflicting sources

Dr. Ricardo Gavidia Castro

Possible sibling · unconfirmed
A researcher with this name earned a chemistry doctorate in 1957 and served as a dean at the Universidad de El Salvador. His relationship to this family has not been confirmed. Unverified lead

Pugna a un dictador

The Spanish transcription is complete: front matter, all 176 numbered pages and the epilogue. The earlier 157-page count was an undercount.La transcripción en español está completa: páginas preliminares, las 176 páginas numeradas y el epílogo. El recuento anterior de 157 páginas fue insuficiente.

176 of 176 numbered pages176 de 176 páginas numeradasSpanish complete · English translation and audiobook completeEspañol completo · traducción y audiolibro en inglés completos

A witness to 1944Un testigo de 1944

Relámpagos de libertad

Mariano Castro Morán · two volumesMariano Castro Morán · dos tomos

Castro Morán was a Salvadoran officer who took part in the April 2, 1944 military uprising against General Maximiliano Hernández Martínez. He was captured and condemned to death, then survived to write this first-hand account of the revolt, its defeat, the prisons and courts-martial, and the repression that followed. That participant’s perspective makes the work an important companion to later scholarship and to María Teresa González’s family-centered Pugna a un dictador.Castro Morán fue un oficial salvadoreño que participó en el levantamiento militar del 2 de abril de 1944 contra el general Maximiliano Hernández Martínez. Fue capturado y condenado a muerte, pero sobrevivió para escribir este relato de primera mano sobre la rebelión, su derrota, las cárceles, los consejos de guerra y la represión posterior. Esa perspectiva de participante convierte la obra en un complemento importante de los estudios posteriores y de Pugna a un dictador, el relato familiar de María Teresa González.

Why it matters to the Gavidias.Por qué importa para los Gavidia. Eighteen searchable pages mention Gavidia. José wrote that, from a nearby cell at 3 a.m. on April 10, he heard the condemned young officers praying and confessing. The book transcribes that morning’s sentence and includes Antonio among those condemned. It calls Antonio both teniente and subteniente, a rank discrepancy still to resolve. Castro Morán twice calls Antonio “mi hermano,” most likely meaning brother-in-arms rather than a literal sibling; a more distant Castro-family connection remains possible but unproven.Dieciocho páginas consultables mencionan a Gavidia. José escribió que, desde una celda cercana a las 3 a. m. del 10 de abril, oyó a los jóvenes condenados rezar y confesarse. El libro transcribe la sentencia de esa mañana e incluye a Antonio entre los condenados. Llama a Antonio tanto teniente como subteniente, una diferencia de rango que aún debe resolverse. Castro Morán llama dos veces a Antonio “mi hermano”, probablemente en el sentido de hermano de armas y no de hermano literal; un parentesco más lejano por la familia Castro sigue siendo posible, pero no está comprobado.

Audiolibro / Audiobook

The complete English and Spanish recordings of the family’s copy of Pugna a un dictador, each divided into ten parts for easier listening.Las grabaciones completas en inglés y español del ejemplar familiar de Pugna a un dictador, cada una dividida en diez partes para facilitar la escucha.

Listen here or download any part for offline listening. Together, the ten English parts run about 9 hours and 10 minutes.

English edition
Recorded from the complete English translation of all 176 numbered pages and the epilogue.

About the summaries: They follow the book’s novelized narrative, not established history. Romo family = the Gavidias; Paco = the Antonio figure; Roberto = the Coronel Roberto Ocampo figure. Part boundaries are approximate.Sobre los resúmenes: Siguen la narración novelada del libro, no hechos históricos establecidos. Familia Romo = los Gavidia; Paco = la figura de Antonio; Roberto = la figura del coronel Roberto Ocampo. Los límites de cada parte son aproximados.

Part 1

English · 55 min

Summary / Resumen

Approx. Author's note, Part One pp. 1–15. The book opens with the author's dedication to her husband and the young officers executed after the April 1944 uprising. The novel begins: 15-year-old María, poor and working in a store, turns down wealthy young Roberto's proposal before he enters military school. At a Christmas party she meets Carlos, a handsome cadet, and falls desperately in love dancing the Alejandra Waltz.

Novelized memoir · names are pseudonyms · part boundaries are approximate.

Summary / Resumen

Approx. Part One pp. 16–32. At the Romo family's New Year's party, Marta — the employer's daughter, the "chosen bride" — also wants Carlos; when the lights go out at midnight, Carlos kisses María by mistake and their secret romance begins, sealed with a music box playing "Alejandra." Marta exposes them to Carlos's proud parents, who threaten disinheritance, and gets María fired. María finds bookstore work, Roberto enters the Military School, and Carlos vows nothing will separate them but death.

María's father dies, they move to the capital, Carlos makes lieutenant with María pinning his stars, and he graduates best pilot of his class.

Roberto, hardened and hostile, breaks with María; she is expecting her fourth child when Carlos gets a mysterious order to report to Aviation — the revolution is beginning.

María is interrogated; Paco is tried by the first Council of War and executed April 10. Betrayed near the border, Carlos is captured to face the second Council of War.

Part 7

English · 55 min

Summary / Resumen

Approx. Part Two pp. 105–121. María visits Carlos in prison, finding his hands bruised from torture; Roberto, secretly on the war council, helps her — arranging the visit and giving her a petition for Carlos's life. At the second Council of War Carlos defends himself proudly; the jury votes four for death, three for exile (Roberto voted exile), but the President signs the sentence. In the penitentiary Carlos asks one last favor — to marry María by the Church — and at dawn she is brought in for the wedding and farewell.

The student strike erupts into the nationwide "huelga de brazos caídos" and Hernández Martínez flees to Guatemala. María mourns daily at the cemetery, her hair turned white. Two years later, widowed Roberto courts María, and at Betina's wedding she agrees to marry him — on her conditions.

Summary / Resumen

Approx. Part Three pp. 158–176. As minister Roberto hardens — but when two colonels face execution in a new plot, María defies him, pleads with the President herself, and saves their lives. She spends a year in the US with her children, returns, and gets the President to pardon the exiles. A sixth child is born; a new president takes office and Roberto resigns to quiet life. Thirty years after Carlos's death, María still brings fresh flowers to his tomb.

08

La novela

María Teresa wrote a novel-memoir, not a court record. The author says that protagonists’ names and the names of the war council were changed.

How the family reads the names

“Doña Carmela”
Read by the family as Carlota Castro de Gavidia, Carlos’s mother. Family interpretation
“Don José”
Read as José Gavidia, Carlos’s father. Family interpretation
“Don Juan”
Read as Carlos’s maternal uncle, probably José Cipriano Castro Bernal, exiled in 1938. Family interpretation
The “Romo” family
Read as the Gavidia family; the text calls him “Capitán Romo.” Family interpretation
“María”
Read as the author’s fictional alter ego, María Teresa González. Family interpretation
“Roberto”
Coronel Roberto Ocampo in the novel; no real-world counterpart has been confirmed. Novel
“Alejandra”
The couple’s waltz and Carlos’s music-box gift, also named in his real farewell letter. Novel / Biography

Two war-council rosters

The narrative on page 94 and the epilogue give different membership lists, sharing only Joaquín Fernández. Both still need comparison with the real 1944 trial file. Novel

The 1936 portrait

The December 2 silver-anniversary photograph names José Antonio, Alba Marina, Juan Carlos, Estela, Juan José, Dagoberto, Rodolfo Eduardo and Ricardo. Carlos is absent. Family record

The penitentiary registry

It records the in articulo mortis marriage at 5:15 a.m.; Carlos, 28, of Santa Ana, son of José Gavidia and Carlota Castro; and María Teresa, 27, of San Salvador, daughter of Santiago González and Susana Jirón. A separate entry names Teniente Alfonso Marín, 26, as executed that morning. Registry

09

Properties & unclaimed assets

A careful place to log confirmed property records and registry matches. Nothing is treated as family property without supporting documentation.

Unclaimed assets

Research queue

No confirmed registry match has been added to the archive. Future checks of U.S. state and El Salvador unclaimed-property records can be documented here with the exact registry and result date.

Haciendas El Ángel & El Carmen

Historical connection

Carlos managed these haciendas in the San Juan Opico / San Pablo Tacachico area after leaving military aviation in 1939. Management does not establish family ownership.

José & Carlota

No record found yet

No records have been located for property owned by José Gavidia and Carlota Castro de Gavidia. This remains an open research question.

Can you help?

Family knowledge matters. Copy these questions or download a simple text file, then send answers and scans to the relative coordinating the archive.

The Flotilla Aérea article “Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (1915–1944)”, with photos by Ricardo Gavidia, answered several long-standing questions. Contested details stay clearly marked, and Ricardo and the family can still help with the remaining blanks.El artículo de Flotilla Aérea “Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (1915–1944)”, con fotos de Ricardo Gavidia, respondió varias preguntas antiguas. Los datos discutidos siguen marcados con claridad, y Ricardo y la familia aún pueden ayudar a completar los vacíos.

Family overviewResumen familiar The family story — cliff notesLa historia familiar — resumen rápido

The whole saga in six short pieces—for relatives who want the shape of the story before exploring the records.Toda la saga en seis partes breves, para los familiares que quieren entender la historia antes de explorar los documentos.

The coreEl núcleo

Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, a military pilot, joined the April 2, 1944 uprising against dictator Maximiliano Hernández Martínez. It failed. He was court-martialed and executed, along with his brothers José Antonio—“Rey Antonio”—and Juan Carlos. The morning Carlos died, he and María Teresa González married in articulo mortis inside the Central Penitentiary.El capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, piloto militar, participó en el levantamiento del 2 de abril de 1944 contra el dictador Maximiliano Hernández Martínez. El intento fracasó. Fue sometido a consejo de guerra y ejecutado, junto con sus hermanos José Antonio—«Rey Antonio»—y Juan Carlos. La mañana en que Carlos murió, él y María Teresa González se casaron in articulo mortis dentro de la Penitenciaría Central.

The bookEl libro

María Teresa told the story in Pugna a un dictador (1987). She said plainly that she changed the names of living protagonists, while printing the real execution lists in the front matter and sometimes letting real names surface in quoted speech. The code is now partly cracked: Romo = Gavidia, Gutiérrez = Calvo, and about 25 identities have been mapped; see the pseudonym key below.María Teresa contó la historia en Pugna a un dictador (1987). Explicó claramente que cambió los nombres de los protagonistas vivos, mientras imprimía las listas reales de ejecutados en las páginas preliminares y a veces dejaba aparecer nombres verdaderos en el diálogo citado. El código ya está parcialmente descifrado: Romo = Gavidia, Gutiérrez = Calvo y se han trazado unas 25 identidades; véase la clave de seudónimos más abajo.

The networkLa red

This was not a lone rebellion. Military families were intertwined through classmates such as Flores Magaña, compadres such as Capitán Erazo—who refused to lead the firing squad—and in-laws such as Erazo’s brother-in-law Marín, who was also executed. Marta Pérez, the bride Carlos’s family wanted him to marry, was widowed that same April when her husband Flores Magaña was executed.No fue una rebelión aislada. Las familias militares estaban entrelazadas por compañeros como Flores Magaña, compadres como el capitán Erazo—quien se negó a dirigir el pelotón de fusilamiento—y parientes políticos como Marín, cuñado de Erazo, también ejecutado. Marta Pérez, la novia con quien la familia quería casar a Carlos, quedó viuda ese mismo abril cuando ejecutaron a su esposo, Flores Magaña.

The survivorsLos sobrevivientes

Dagoberto served as consul in San Francisco, resigned in protest and returned after the dictator fell. Estela became the first woman to earn a doctorate from UES; Ricardo became a dean there; Juan José lived to 104. The survivors rebuilt through education and public service.Dagoberto fue cónsul en San Francisco, renunció en protesta y regresó después de la caída del dictador. Estela se convirtió en la primera mujer en obtener un doctorado en la UES; Ricardo llegó a ser decano allí; Juan José vivió hasta los 104 años. Los sobrevivientes reconstruyeron sus vidas por medio de la educación y el servicio público.

The mysteriesLos misterios

Unverified family theory: was great-grandfather José the unrecognized son of Ángel Guirola Duke, a presidential candidate and leader in the 1932 repression? The surrounding historical details align, but no document yet proves the paternity. Doctor Velásquez—the defense lawyer in the book—also remains unidentified.Teoría familiar sin verificar: ¿fue el bisabuelo José hijo no reconocido de Ángel Guirola Duke, candidato presidencial y dirigente de la represión de 1932? Los detalles históricos que rodean la pista coinciden, pero todavía ningún documento demuestra la paternidad. El doctor Velásquez—el abogado defensor del libro—también sigue sin identificar.

The living threadsLos hilos vivos

The strongest paths forward lead to Francisco José Gavidia Ayala, Juan José’s grandson in San Salvador; the Flores-Pérez children, including a newborn from 1944 who would be about 82 in 2026; and Elena Castro Morán, keeper of the author’s papers.Las pistas más prometedoras conducen a Francisco José Gavidia Ayala, nieto de Juan José en San Salvador; a los hijos Flores-Pérez, entre ellos un recién nacido de 1944 que tendría unos 82 años en 2026; y a Elena Castro Morán, custodia de los papeles del autor.

Under investigation · October 2026En investigación · octubre de 2026 Was José Gavidia born out of wedlock?¿Nació José Gavidia fuera del matrimonio?

A cousin shared a family account that great-grandfather José Gavidia may have been a hijo natural of Carmen Gaviria and Ángel Guirola Duke, without legal recognition from his father. The surrounding history is unusually specific, and every checkable detail is documented, but the paternity itself is not.Un primo compartió un relato familiar según el cual el bisabuelo José Gavidia pudo haber sido hijo natural de Carmen Gaviria y Ángel Guirola Duke, sin reconocimiento legal de su padre. La historia que rodea la pista es inusualmente específica y todos sus detalles comprobables están documentados, pero la paternidad en sí no lo está.

The Guirola family storyLa historia de la familia Guirola

The Guirolas were coffee-era royalty—among the richest families in El Salvador and owners of large parts of Santa Tecla. Rafael María Guirola was a younger son from Santander, Spain, cut out of the inheritance by custom. He emigrated and rebuilt from nothing, then married Gertrudis de la Cotera y González—also from a Santander family—in Zacatecoluca in 1808.Los Guirola eran la realeza de la época cafetalera: una de las familias más ricas de El Salvador y propietaria de grandes extensiones de Santa Tecla. Rafael María Guirola era un hijo menor de Santander, España, excluido de la herencia por costumbre. Emigró y reconstruyó su fortuna desde cero; en 1808 se casó en Zacatecoluca con Gertrudis de la Cotera y González, también de una familia santanderina.

Their son Ángel Guirola (1826–1910) served as acting president of El Salvador in 1884 and founded the Banco Internacional. He married Cordelia Duke Alexander of Kingston, Jamaica, and all their children carried the compound surname Guirola Duke.Su hijo Ángel Guirola (1826–1910) ejerció como presidente interino de El Salvador en 1884 y fundó el Banco Internacional. Se casó con Cordelia Duke Alexander, de Kingston, Jamaica, y todos sus hijos llevaron el apellido compuesto Guirola Duke.

One of those sons, Ángel Guirola Duke, ran for president in 1919. On the night of January 22, 1932, he co-founded the council that organized the repression of the peasant uprising—the Matanza—and became a leader in Hernández Martínez’s own party.Uno de esos hijos, Ángel Guirola Duke, se postuló a la presidencia en 1919. La noche del 22 de enero de 1932 cofundó el consejo que organizó la represión del levantamiento campesino—la Matanza—y llegó a ser dirigente del propio partido de Hernández Martínez.

The historical irony: if the cousin’s claim is true, the captain’s biological grandfather was a pillar of the Martínez regime—the same regime that executed the captain in 1944 for rebelling against it.La ironía histórica: si la afirmación del primo es cierta, el abuelo biológico del capitán fue un pilar del régimen de Martínez, el mismo régimen que ejecutó al capitán en 1944 por rebelarse contra él.

Status: every checkable detail around the story is documented, but there is still no paper connecting Ángel Guirola Duke to Carmen Gaviria or to José. The claim remains unverified. The two documents that could settle it are José’s baptism record from about 1880–1885 and his marriage record from about 1911, both in the Santa Ana parish books.Estado: todos los detalles comprobables que rodean la historia están documentados, pero todavía no existe ningún documento que vincule a Ángel Guirola Duke con Carmen Gaviria o con José. La afirmación sigue sin verificarse. Los dos documentos que podrían resolverla son la partida de bautismo de José, de aproximadamente 1880–1885, y su partida de matrimonio, de alrededor de 1911, ambas en los libros parroquiales de Santa Ana.

What checks outLo que sí cuadra

  1. The Guirolas were one of El Salvador’s wealthiest coffee-era families. Ángel Guirola (1826–1910) was briefly acting president from April to August 1884 and founded the Banco Internacional.Los Guirola fueron una de las familias más ricas de la época cafetalera de El Salvador. Ángel Guirola (1826–1910) fue presidente interino de abril a agosto de 1884 y fundó el Banco Internacional.
  2. Family patriarch Rafael María Guirola was documented as a “segundón oriundo de Santander”—a younger son from Santander, Spain, who emigrated after being cut out of the inheritance. This confirms the cousin’s Santander detail, with one correction: Rafael María was Ángel Guirola Duke’s grandfather, not his father.El patriarca Rafael María Guirola fue descrito documentalmente como “segundón oriundo de Santander”: un hijo menor de Santander, España, que emigró después de quedar fuera de la herencia. Esto confirma el detalle de Santander aportado por el primo, con una corrección: Rafael María era abuelo de Ángel Guirola Duke, no su padre.
  3. Guirola Duke is the exact compound surname carried by Ángel Guirola’s children with his Jamaican wife, Cordelia Duke Alexander. Their children included Ángel Guirola Duke, who co-founded the Instituto Moderno de Santa Tecla and was active in the right period.Guirola Duke es el apellido compuesto exacto que llevaron los hijos de Ángel Guirola con su esposa jamaicana, Cordelia Duke Alexander. Entre ellos estuvo Ángel Guirola Duke, cofundador del Instituto Moderno de Santa Tecla y activo en la época indicada.
  4. The timeline is possible: Ángel Jr., born around the 1860s, could have fathered José, born around 1880–1885, while in his twenties.La cronología es posible: Ángel hijo, nacido alrededor de la década de 1860, pudo haber sido padre de José, nacido aproximadamente entre 1880 y 1885, cuando tenía unos veinte años.
  5. “Helguerola,” the name entered on FamilySearch, may be a misreading of Guirola. It could also point to Helguero or Helguera, real surnames from Cantabria—the region around Santander. Either reading circles back to Santander.“Helguerola”, el nombre registrado en FamilySearch, podría ser una lectura errónea de Guirola. También podría apuntar a Helguero o Helguera, apellidos reales de Cantabria, la región de Santander. Cualquiera de las lecturas vuelve a apuntar hacia Santander.

What remains unverifiedLo que sigue sin verificarse

  • October 2026 searches found no record of a Carmen Gaviria in El Salvador; Gaviria is overwhelmingly a Colombian surname.Las búsquedas de octubre de 2026 no encontraron registro de una Carmen Gaviria en El Salvador; Gaviria es un apellido predominantemente colombiano.
  • No birth or baptism record for José Gavidia was found online.No se encontró en línea un acta de nacimiento o bautismo de José Gavidia.
  • No paper currently connects Ángel Guirola Duke to Carmen Gaviria or to José.Hasta ahora, ningún documento vincula a Ángel Guirola Duke con Carmen Gaviria ni con José.

Bottom line: the family background around the story is documented, but the core paternity claim remains an unverified lead—not a family fact.Conclusión: el contexto familiar que rodea la historia está documentado, pero la afirmación central sobre la paternidad sigue siendo una pista no verificada, no un hecho familiar.

Next stepsPróximos pasos

  1. Inspect the actual FamilySearch “Helguerola” entry and its original image.Revisar la entrada original de FamilySearch donde aparece “Helguerola” y la imagen del documento.
  2. Search Santa Ana parish baptisms from about 1880–1885.Buscar en los bautismos parroquiales de Santa Ana de aproximadamente 1880–1885.
  3. Check Archivo General de la Nación indices for “Gaviria” and “Gavidia” in Santa Ana.Revisar los índices del Archivo General de la Nación para “Gaviria” y “Gavidia” en Santa Ana.
Under investigation · May 1944En investigación · mayo de 1944 Dagoberto’s resignation — May 1944La renuncia de Dagoberto — mayo de 1944

Dagoberto Gavidia Castro, the captain’s brother, was Consul General of El Salvador in San Francisco in 1944. Weeks after his brothers Carlos and José Antonio were executed for the April 2 uprising, he resigned his post in protest.Dagoberto Gavidia Castro, hermano del capitán, era cónsul general de El Salvador en San Francisco en 1944. Semanas después de que sus hermanos Carlos y José Antonio fueran ejecutados por el levantamiento del 2 de abril, renunció a su cargo en protesta.

What the newspaper record showsLo que demuestra el registro periodístico

The San Bernardino Sun of May 17, 1944 carried this United Press wire report: “SAN FRANCISCO, May 16 (UP) Dagoberto Gavidia said today he had withdrawn his resignation as consul general of El Salvador.”El San Bernardino Sun del 17 de mayo de 1944 publicó este despacho de United Press: «SAN FRANCISCO, 16 de mayo (UP). Dagoberto Gavidia dijo hoy que había retirado su renuncia como cónsul general de El Salvador». DocumentedDocumentado

Reading the timeline: what first looks like a retraction tells a different story when placed against the fall of the regime. Hernández Martínez left power on May 9, 1944. Dagoberto withdrew his resignation on May 16—one week after the dictator was gone. The timing supports the interpretation that he did not cave to pressure: he resigned in protest over his brothers’ executions and returned to duty once the regime had fallen.Lectura de la cronología: lo que a primera vista parece una retractación cuenta otra historia al situarlo frente a la caída del régimen. Hernández Martínez dejó el poder el 9 de mayo de 1944. Dagoberto retiró su renuncia el 16 de mayo, una semana después de la salida del dictador. La cronología respalda la interpretación de que no cedió a la presión: renunció en protesta por las ejecuciones de sus hermanos y volvió al cargo una vez caído el régimen. AnalysisAnálisis

The missing letter: Dagoberto’s original resignation letter has not been found online. It is likely to be in the foreign ministry files at the Archivo General de la Nación and should be requested during the El Salvador trip.La carta pendiente: la carta original de renuncia de Dagoberto no se ha encontrado en línea. Es probable que se conserve en los archivos del Ministerio de Relaciones Exteriores en el Archivo General de la Nación y conviene solicitarla durante el viaje a El Salvador. Dagoberto’s son, Carlos Dagoberto Gavidia Ventura, is documented as a lawyer active in San Salvador court records in the 2000s.Carlos Dagoberto Gavidia Ventura, hijo de Dagoberto, aparece documentado como abogado activo en expedientes judiciales de San Salvador durante la década de 2000. Research key · October 2026Clave de investigación · octubre de 2026 The pseudonym key — decoding the book’s changed namesLa clave de seudónimos — descifrando los nombres cambiados del libro

María Teresa González tells readers that she changed the names of the Council of War members and every protagonist. Her own front matter—and the way real names surface inside dialogue—makes it possible to begin reversing those changes.María Teresa González advierte al lector que cambió los nombres de los miembros del Consejo de Guerra y de todos los protagonistas. Su propio prólogo—y la manera en que los nombres reales aparecen dentro del diálogo—permite comenzar a revertir esos cambios.

How the key was madeCómo se construyó la clave

«En este relato, todos los nombres de los que integraron el fatídico Consejo de Guerra, están cambiados, lo mismo que el de todos los protagonistas de mi Historia.»

“In this account, all the names of those who made up the fateful Council of War are changed, as are those of all the protagonists of my Story.” Yet in the same front matter, María Teresa prints the real newspaper execution lists. Those lists became the Rosetta Stone: narrative names can be checked against real people who held the same rank, appeared on the same date and played the same role.«En este relato, todos los nombres de los que integraron el fatídico Consejo de Guerra, están cambiados, lo mismo que el de todos los protagonistas de mi Historia». Sin embargo, en esas mismas páginas preliminares, María Teresa reproduce las listas periodísticas reales de los ejecutados. Esas listas se convirtieron en la piedra de Rosetta: los nombres narrativos pueden compararse con personas reales del mismo rango, fecha y función.

The tell is direct speech. When a character speaks in a quoted passage, the book sometimes uses real names—for example, Erazo says “Teniente Marín… el Capitán Gavidia Castro”—while the narration surrounding that quotation calls them “Marinelo” and “Romo.” Quoted speech is therefore one of the strongest clues for future decoding.La pista está en el discurso directo. Cuando un personaje habla dentro de una cita, el libro a veces conserva los nombres reales—por ejemplo, Erazo dice «Teniente Marín… el Capitán Gavidia Castro»—mientras la narración que rodea esa cita los llama «Marinelo» y «Romo». Por eso, el diálogo citado es una de las pistas más sólidas para seguir descifrando el libro.

Flags and puzzlesAlertas y enigmas

  • Flores Magaña is absent from María Teresa’s own printed newspaper execution list, even though the narrative says he died on April 10. This remains unresolved.Flores Magaña no aparece en la lista periodística de ejecutados que María Teresa reproduce, aunque la narración afirma que murió el 10 de abril. Sigue sin resolverse.
  • The “three brothers” device is literary fiction: Marroquín, Calvo and Sosa were not brothers.El recurso de los «tres hermanos» es ficción literaria: Marroquín, Calvo y Sosa no eran hermanos.

How to use this keyCómo usar esta clave

  • Start with the real names in quoted speech.Comenzar con los nombres reales dentro del diálogo citado.
  • Match rank, date, place and role against the printed execution lists.Comparar rango, fecha, lugar y función con las listas impresas de ejecutados.
  • Keep composite characters and shifted ranks possible; do not force a one-to-one match.Mantener abierta la posibilidad de personajes compuestos y rangos alterados; no forzar una correspondencia uno a uno.

Her methodSu método

In 1987, mid-civil-war, María Teresa González printed the truth inside the fiction. The living got false names so no one could come after them or their families—but the dead got their real names, set in print forever. She hid the key in plain sight: the real execution lists in the front matter, and real names slipping out whenever someone speaks in quoted speech. Anyone careful enough to compare the two gets the whole code. Not just caution—craft.En 1987, en plena guerra civil, María Teresa González imprimió la verdad dentro de la ficción. Los vivos recibieron nombres falsos para que nadie pudiera perseguirlos a ellos ni a sus familias; pero los muertos conservaron sus nombres reales, fijados para siempre en letra impresa. Escondió la clave a plena vista: las listas reales de ejecutados en las páginas preliminares y los nombres verdaderos que se escapan cada vez que alguien habla en discurso directo. Quien compare ambas cosas con suficiente cuidado obtiene el código completo. No fue solo cautela: fue oficio.

Research notes · October 2026Notas de investigación · octubre de 2026 The wider circle — new findingsEl círculo más amplio — nuevos hallazgos

New connections around the captain, the book and the April 1944 uprising—kept concise and labeled by evidence.Nuevas conexiones en torno al capitán, el libro y el levantamiento de abril de 1944, resumidas y clasificadas según la evidencia.

Romero

Doctor Arturo Romero López (1911–1965), the book’s “Doctor Córdova,” was a Sorbonne-trained physician at Hospital Rosales and a co-founder of the Colegio Médico. On April 2 he seized radio stations YSP and YSR with a submachine gun, earning the name “El hombre símbolo de la Revolución.” Sentenced to death in absentia, he survived a machete attack while fleeing toward Honduras, lived in exile in Costa Rica and died in 1965. In 1945 he ran for president on the same ballot as José Cipriano Castro Bernal, the captain’s uncle.El doctor Arturo Romero López (1911–1965), el «doctor Córdova» del libro, fue un médico formado en la Sorbona, trabajó en el Hospital Rosales y cofundó el Colegio Médico. El 2 de abril tomó las radios YSP y YSR con una metralleta en la mano, ganándose el nombre de «El hombre símbolo de la Revolución». Condenado a muerte en ausencia, sobrevivió un ataque a machetazos mientras huía hacia Honduras, vivió exiliado en Costa Rica y murió en 1965. En 1945 fue candidato presidencial en la misma papeleta que José Cipriano Castro Bernal, tío del capitán.

DocumentedDocumentado

The Calvo MarroquínLos Calvo Marroquín

Tito Tomás Calvo—Martínez’s own compadre and an officer in the 1932 Matanza—rebelled in 1944. The family is the Calvo Marroquín of Izalco: General Alfonso Marroquín was the maternal half-brother of Tito and Marcelino Calvo. Five relatives were condemned—three executed and two doctors sentenced in absentia—so the book’s “three brothers” was half-right. Merceditas Ros, seven in 1944, later testified that Martínez’s 1966 assassin was the godson of one of the executed and was raised in their home; the family remembers it as vengeance.Tito Tomás Calvo—compadre del propio Martínez y oficial durante la Matanza de 1932—se rebeló en 1944. La familia es la Calvo Marroquín de Izalco: el general Alfonso Marroquín era medio hermano materno de Tito y Marcelino Calvo. Cinco familiares fueron condenados: tres ejecutados y dos médicos sentenciados en ausencia; por eso, los «tres hermanos» del libro estaban a medias en lo cierto. Merceditas Ros, de siete años en 1944, declaró después que el asesino de Martínez en 1966 era ahijado de uno de los ejecutados y se había criado en su casa; la familia lo recuerda como una venganza.

Documented · trial-dossier scholarship and published testimonyDocumentado · estudios del expediente judicial y testimonio publicado

Sosa

Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa, Air Force second-in-command and a pioneer pilot, is likely the book’s “Mayor Pineda,” declared insane but shot anyway on Martínez’s order. A memoir recalls Sosa’s son—about 85 in 2026—as the writer’s schoolmate. One discrepancy remains to verify: some material says Julio Domingo, while the records say Julio Faustino.El mayor Julio Faustino Sosa, segundo al mando de la Fuerza Aérea y piloto pionero, probablemente sea el «mayor Pineda» del libro, declarado demente pero fusilado de todos modos por orden de Martínez. Unas memorias recuerdan al hijo de Sosa—de unos 85 años en 2026—como compañero de escuela del autor. Queda una discrepancia por verificar: una fuente dice Julio Domingo, mientras los registros dicen Julio Faustino.

Documented · name discrepancy to verifyDocumentado · falta verificar la discrepancia del nombre

The hacienda yearsLos años de hacienda

Around 1939, a jealous colleague reported Carlos’s wedding-anniversary dinner as a clandestine meeting. His chief pushed him out of Aviation, remarking that his father “is wealthy and has many properties you can administer.” The hacienda then became an intelligence node: Flores visited and fed Carlos information. After the uprising failed, Carlos fled through the cane fields toward Honduras and was betrayed by a guide.Hacia 1939, un colega celoso denunció la cena de aniversario de bodas de Carlos como una reunión clandestina. Su jefe lo apartó de Aviación, señalando que su padre «es rico y tiene muchas propiedades que usted puede administrar». La hacienda se convirtió luego en un centro de inteligencia: Flores lo visitaba y le proporcionaba información. Tras fracasar el levantamiento, Carlos huyó por los cañaverales rumbo a Honduras y fue traicionado por un guía.

Family recordRegistro familiar

The civiliansLos civiles

Agustín Alfaro Morán, a coffee grower, was later called “the true manager of the dictator’s fall.” Poet Matilde Elena López seized the radio microphone and declared, “¡Ha sonado la hora de la liberación!” Medical student Fabio Castillo tried to rescue the wounded Romero and later became a statesman. They reveal one conspiracy with intertwined military and civilian tracks.Agustín Alfaro Morán, caficultor, fue llamado después «el verdadero gestor de la caída del dictador». La poeta Matilde Elena López tomó el micrófono de la radio y proclamó: «¡Ha sonado la hora de la liberación!». El estudiante de medicina Fabio Castillo intentó rescatar al herido Romero y más tarde se convirtió en estadista. Sus historias revelan una sola conspiración con vías militares y civiles entrelazadas.

DocumentedDocumentado

Velásquez

Two figures belong to two eras. Doctor Velásquez, the brilliant 1944 defense lawyer, appears only in narration and never in quoted speech; by the book’s naming method, his name is likely a pseudonym, and his real identity remains unknown. Coronel Velásquez belongs to the post-1944 era and is named in quoted speech, so the surname is likely real. Any relationship between them remains unknown.Dos figuras pertenecen a dos épocas. El doctor Velásquez, brillante abogado defensor de 1944, aparece solo en la narración y nunca en el diálogo citado; según el método de nombres del libro, probablemente sea un seudónimo y su identidad real sigue desconocida. El coronel Velásquez pertenece a la época posterior a 1944 y aparece nombrado en discurso directo, por lo que el apellido probablemente sea real. Se desconoce si existía parentesco entre ellos.

AnalysisAnálisis

Flores Magaña — name statusFlores Magaña — estado del nombre

“Roberto” appears once, in the quoted verdict; “Manuel” appears in Marta’s quoted speech—“¿Qué te pasa Manuel?”—so, by the book’s method, Manuel is likely his real first name. Flores Magaña is absent from every documented execution list. The leading hypothesis is that the name shields a family still living in 1987; the best real-name candidate is Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas. A marriage record from around the 1930s is the decisive test.«Roberto» aparece una sola vez, en el veredicto citado; «Manuel» aparece en las palabras citadas de Marta—«¿Qué te pasa Manuel?»—por lo que, según el método del libro, Manuel probablemente sea su verdadero nombre de pila. Flores Magaña no figura en ninguna lista documentada de ejecutados. La hipótesis principal es que ese nombre protegía a una familia que aún vivía en 1987; el mejor candidato como identidad real es el capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas. Un acta de matrimonio de alrededor de la década de 1930 sería la prueba decisiva.

AnalysisAnálisisUnverifiedSin verificar
Research notes · October 2026Notas de investigación · octubre de 2026 Connecting the dots — round twoConectando los puntos — segunda ronda

Eight new connections from the book, family notes and historical research—each kept concise and labeled by the strength of its evidence.Ocho conexiones nuevas a partir del libro, las notas familiares y la investigación histórica, cada una resumida y clasificada según la solidez de la evidencia.

The Marín brothersLos hermanos Marín

Alfonso and Víctor Marín were brothers: the book calls Alfonso “hermano del civil que había sido el más torturado.” “Marinelo” is simply “Marín” with -elo added. Alfonso refused the President’s personal phone order to surrender, had his arm destroyed under torture, refused to defend himself at trial and asked for a Church wedding before execution—parallel to Carlos’s in articulo mortis marriage. His last words were: “Queremos morir como verdaderos hombres y militares de honor.” He left a young child who would be about 82–85 today.Alfonso y Víctor Marín eran hermanos: el libro llama a Alfonso «hermano del civil que había sido el más torturado». «Marinelo» es simplemente «Marín» con el sufijo -elo. Alfonso rechazó la orden telefónica personal del Presidente de rendirse, perdió el brazo bajo tortura, se negó a defenderse en el juicio y pidió casarse por la Iglesia antes de ser ejecutado, en paralelo con el matrimonio in articulo mortis de Carlos. Sus últimas palabras fueron: «Queremos morir como verdaderos hombres y militares de honor». Dejó un hijo pequeño que hoy tendría unos 82–85 años.

Documented in the book · living thread unmappedDocumentado en el libro · hilo familiar vivo sin localizar

Flores Magaña unmaskedFlores Magaña desenmascarado

The identification as Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas is now strong: same slot—first in the front-matter roster for April 10—same rank, date and cemetery; “Manuel” is preserved in Marta’s quoted speech. Horacio Castellanos Moya’s novel Tirana memoria independently casts Sánchez Dueñas as the plot’s true organizer who personally recruited the Gavidia brothers; its sources do not include María Teresa’s book. “Flores Magaña” has the plausible compound surname a careful pseudonymist would choose. Darío Flores, the real colonel on the 1944 council, was a different person; the shared surname is coincidence.La identificación como el capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas ya es sólida: ocupa el mismo lugar—primero en la lista preliminar del 10 de abril—y coincide en rango, fecha y cementerio; «Manuel» se conserva en las palabras citadas de Marta. La novela Tirana memoria, de Horacio Castellanos Moya, presenta de forma independiente a Sánchez Dueñas como el verdadero organizador de la conspiración y quien reclutó personalmente a los hermanos Gavidia; sus fuentes no incluyen el libro de María Teresa. «Flores Magaña» suena como el apellido compuesto que elegiría una autora cuidadosa al crear un seudónimo. Darío Flores, coronel real del consejo de 1944, era otra persona; el apellido compartido es coincidencia.

Strong hypothesis · independent corroborationHipótesis sólida · corroboración independiente

Two Robertos — correctionDos Robertos — corrección

“Roberto Ocampo”—Doña Delia’s son and María’s second husband in the book—and “Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña,” Marta’s husband, are different people. Doña Delia is Ocampo’s mother, not Flores’s; Ana Mercedes is Ocampo’s cousin. Earlier notes attached them to the wrong Roberto; this entry corrects that error.«Roberto Ocampo»—hijo de doña Delia y segundo esposo de María en el libro—y «capitán Roberto Flores Magaña», esposo de Marta, son personas distintas. Doña Delia es la madre de Ocampo, no la de Flores; Ana Mercedes es prima de Ocampo. Las notas anteriores las vinculaban al Roberto equivocado; esta entrada corrige ese error.

Correction · book relationshipsCorrección · relaciones según el libro

The real second husbandEl verdadero segundo esposo

“Roberto Ocampo” is a pseudonym. Family testimony now identifies María Teresa’s second husband as Coronel Luis Lovo Castelar — María Teresa herself confessed this to the family when the book was published in 1987, and the cousin who proofread the book confirms only she and Tony Gavidia knew. Lovo Castelar was real and prominent: military school director (1953), exiled by Lemus, economics doctorate in Mexico, agrarian reform pioneer, UN diplomat. The book novelized details — he was never Minister of War (Bolaños and Molina held the post) and was ~22 in 1944. The children’s “Ayala” surname remains unexplained; the ~1946 marriage record must decide it. The earlier Santiago Ayala hypothesis is superseded, though Ayala is now documentarily confirmed as a Council of War member.«Roberto Ocampo» es un seudónimo. El testimonio familiar identifica ahora al segundo esposo de María Teresa como el coronel Luis Lovo Castelar — la propia María Teresa lo confesó a la familia cuando se publicó el libro en 1987, y el primo que corrigió el libro confirma que solo ella y Tony Gavidia lo sabían. Lovo Castelar fue real y prominente: director de la Escuela Militar (1953), exiliado por Lemus, doctorado en economía en México, pionero de la reforma agraria, diplomático en la ONU. El libro noveló detalles — nunca fue ministro de Guerra (Bolaños y Molina ocuparon el cargo) y tenía ~22 años en 1944. El apellido «Ayala» de los hijos sigue sin explicación; el acta de matrimonio de ~1946 debe resolverlo. La hipótesis anterior de Santiago Ayala queda superada, aunque Ayala está ahora documentalmente confirmado como miembro del Consejo de Guerra.

Family-confirmed · details novelizedConfirmado por la familia · detalles novelados

Part Three decodedLa Tercera Parte descifrada

The book’s Third Part spans roughly 1944–1974. Its President is almost certainly Óscar Osorio (1950–56), Carlos’s former classmate. The Muñiz–Velásquez coup episode appears novelized: no matching real-world coup has been found, though Osorio’s government did systematically commute death sentences, making the setting plausible. The widows petitioned for the case files and discovered they had been stolen—an explanation for the missing trial dossier. The book and the Calvo Marroquín family also preserve different versions of Martínez’s 1966 assassination: María Teresa says his own driver killed him, “a stab for each of the executed”; the family identifies a godson raised in their home and remembers vengeance.La Tercera Parte del libro abarca aproximadamente de 1944 a 1974. El Presidente es casi con certeza Óscar Osorio (1950–56), antiguo compañero de Carlos. El episodio del golpe de Muñiz y Velásquez parece novelado: no se ha encontrado un golpe real que coincida, aunque el gobierno de Osorio sí conmutaba sistemáticamente las condenas a muerte, lo que hace plausible el contexto. Las viudas solicitaron los expedientes y descubrieron que habían sido robados, lo que explicaría la ausencia del dossier judicial. El libro y la familia Calvo Marroquín también conservan versiones distintas del asesinato de Martínez en 1966: María Teresa dice que lo mató su propio chofer, «una puñalada por cada fusilado»; la familia identifica a un ahijado criado en su casa y lo recuerda como venganza.

Book analysis · historical setting partly documentedAnálisis del libro · contexto histórico parcialmente documentado

The 1954 plaqueLa placa de 1954

No photograph or transcription has been found online. Photographing the plaque would create a genuinely new record. The open question is whether Carlos—executed April 26 rather than April 10—is named on it.No se ha encontrado en línea ninguna fotografía ni transcripción. Fotografiar la placa crearía un registro genuinamente nuevo. La pregunta abierta es si Carlos—ejecutado el 26 de abril y no el 10—aparece nombrado.

Undocumented · trip taskSin documentar · tarea para el viaje

The CW-14 photographLa fotografía del CW-14

Eight men stand at Ilopango, probably between 1936 and 1939. The short pilot at center, in a leather flight jacket with goggles on his cap, is probably Carlos. The other seven remain unidentified because no labeled reference photographs are available online. Family identification and the Museo Nacional de Aviación are the two best paths.Ocho hombres posan en Ilopango, probablemente entre 1936 y 1939. El piloto de baja estatura en el centro, con chaqueta de vuelo de cuero y gafas sobre la gorra, probablemente sea Carlos. Los otros siete siguen sin identificar porque no existen fotografías de referencia rotuladas en línea. La identificación familiar y el Museo Nacional de Aviación son las dos mejores vías.

Visual analysis · family confirmation neededAnálisis visual · falta confirmación familiar

The tenth siblingEl décimo hermano

The tenth child remains unnamed. The best hypothesis is a child who died young before the December 1936 family portrait. Santa Ana parish baptism and burial books are the path to an answer. One name is now clearer: the eldest brother’s full legal name was Juan José Gavidia Castro; “José” was the familiar form.El décimo hijo sigue sin nombre. La mejor hipótesis es que fuera un niño que murió pequeño antes del retrato familiar de diciembre de 1936. Los libros parroquiales de bautismos y entierros de Santa Ana son la vía para resolverlo. Un nombre ya está más claro: el nombre legal completo del hermano mayor era Juan José Gavidia Castro; «José» era la forma familiar.

Best hypothesis · parish record neededMejor hipótesis · se necesita un registro parroquial
Under investigation · April 1944En investigación · abril de 1944 Marta Pérez — the chosen brideMarta Pérez — la novia elegida

Marta Pérez was the daughter of Don Pedro Pérez, owner of “el Almacén de los Pérez,” where María Teresa González worked—and the bride the Gavidia family wanted for Carlos. The account below comes from the family’s book and has not been independently confirmed.Marta Pérez era hija de don Pedro Pérez, dueño de «el Almacén de los Pérez», donde trabajaba María Teresa González, y la novia que la familia Gavidia quería para Carlos. El relato que sigue proviene del libro de la familia y no ha sido confirmado de manera independiente. Family recordRegistro familiarNovelNovela

Two women, one April tragedyDos mujeres, una tragedia de abril

When Carlos came home glowing, his mother’s first words were, “¿Te comprometes con Marta?” He refused outright: “¡Dios me libre, esa locura no la cometería jamás!” In the book, Marta intrigues against María and feeds the parents’ opposition to the relationship.Cuando Carlos volvió a casa radiante, las primeras palabras de su madre fueron: «¿Te comprometes con Marta?». Él se negó de plano: «¡Dios me libre, esa locura no la cometería jamás!». En el libro, Marta intriga contra María y alimenta la oposición de los padres a la relación. NovelNovela

Then comes the twist: Marta married Capitán (Manuel) Roberto Flores Magaña—Carlos’s own classmate from his first year of military school. The book describes Flores Magaña as a spotless officer who fed Carlos inside information about the regime and became one of the leaders of the April 1944 plot.Luego viene el giro: Marta se casó con el capitán (Manuel) Roberto Flores Magaña, compañero de Carlos desde su primer año en la Escuela Militar. El libro describe a Flores Magaña como un oficial intachable que proporcionaba a Carlos información interna sobre el régimen y que llegó a ser uno de los dirigentes de la conspiración de abril de 1944. Family recordRegistro familiarNovelNovela

Flores Magaña was tortured, tried and executed on the morning of April 10, 1944. Marta was at home praying for him and knew nothing of what was happening. The book says she “went mad with grief.” She then disappears from the narrative, as do her children—including a newborn who would be about 82 today.Flores Magaña fue torturado, juzgado y ejecutado la mañana del 10 de abril de 1944. Marta estaba en casa rezando por él y no sabía nada de lo que ocurría. El libro dice que «enloqueció de dolor». Después desaparece del relato, al igual que sus hijos, entre ellos un recién nacido que hoy tendría alrededor de 82 años. NovelNovela

The parallel: both women—the chosen bride and the rejected one—lost their husbands to the same firing squads in April 1944.El paralelo: ambas mujeres—la novia elegida y la rechazada—perdieron a sus esposos ante los mismos pelotones de fusilamiento en abril de 1944. Family recordRegistro familiarNovelNovela

What remains missing: no online trace has been found of Marta, the Pérez family or the Flores-Pérez children.Lo que sigue faltando: no se ha encontrado en línea ningún rastro de Marta, de la familia Pérez ni de los hijos Flores-Pérez. Research statusEstado de la investigación

  2. Search for the birth records of their children.Buscar las actas de nacimiento de sus hijos.

Can you help find María Teresa’s birth or baptism record?¿Puede ayudar a encontrar el acta de nacimiento o bautismo de María Teresa?The prison registry names her parents as Santiago González and Susana Jirón and her birthplace as San Salvador, but the exact date and original record remain missing.El registro penitenciario nombra a sus padres como Santiago González y Susana Jirón y señala San Salvador como lugar de nacimiento, pero aún faltan la fecha exacta y el acta original.
What was Capitán Erazo’s first name?¿Cuál era el nombre de pila del capitán Erazo?The book identifies him only by rank and surname.El libro lo identifica solo por rango y apellido.

Do you have more family photographs?¿Tiene más fotografías familiares?Especially the Flotilla, the wedding, the captain, or images with original captions and dates.Especialmente de la Flotilla, la boda, el capitán o imágenes con pies de foto y fechas originales.
Where was José Gavidia from, and what was his occupation?¿De dónde era José Gavidia y cuál era su ocupación?Civil, church or employment records may identify his origins and work.Los registros civiles, eclesiásticos o laborales pueden identificar su origen y su trabajo.
Was Carlota Castro de Gavidia the same person as Carlota Antonia Castro Bernal?¿Era Carlota Castro de Gavidia la misma persona que Carlota Antonia Castro Bernal?A birth or marriage record is needed to confirm her full identity and family line.Se necesita un acta de nacimiento o matrimonio para confirmar su identidad completa y su línea familiar.
Who are the other men in the CW-14 airplane photograph?¿Quiénes son los otros hombres en la fotografía del avión CW-14?Names, ranks or memories about the day would help identify them.Los nombres, rangos o recuerdos de ese día ayudarían a identificarlos.

12

Research Action Plan

A practical path for separating family memory from documented fact and finding the records that can close the gaps.

Red flags

  • Unproven: that the family owned Haciendas El Ángel and El Carmen. The current record says Carlos administered them; management is not proof of ownership.
  • Unproven: that El Ángel and El Carmen became the Talcualuya cooperative.
  • Disputed: the biography gives April 24 for Carlos’s execution, while some popular summaries report April 26 and the novel includes conflicting dates.
  • Still checking: the exact execution time. The registry’s 5:15 a.m. notation is the marriage ceremony time; the family biography gives a 6:30 a.m. execution at the Cementerio General.

What you can do

  • Compare the novel’s two war-council rosters with the certified 1944 trial file.
  • Ask older relatives to resolve the portrait’s 1936/1937 date and “our grandfather in the middle” identification, then confirm all ten children.
  • Ask for Capitán Erazo’s first name and the date, place and circumstances of Juan Carlos’s death in 1944.

Order to request documents

  1. CNR: Datos de Índice and Certificación Literal for the hacienda lands.
  2. ISTA: expropriation decrees for the Talcualuya area.
  3. Diario Oficial, 1944: any confiscation decrees issued after the April uprising.
  4. Municipal catastros: San Juan Opico and San Pablo Tacachico.
  5. Santa Ana parish registers: search through FamilySearch.

Items 1–4 require in-person visits in El Salvador.

Court-martial scholarship

Relámpagos de libertad

Es especialmente valioso junto al expediente del consejo de guerra y los relatos familiares porque registra desde dentro la rebelión, la prisión y la represión.

Later Spanish-language eyewitness account

Síntesis del levantamiento cívico-militar, written later by someone who had been eight years old in Villa El Rosario in 1944. The site retains the research label “Later eyewitness account,” while describing it accurately as a later recollection rather than a document written in 1944.

Fuerza Aérea Salvadoreña

Read the source ↗
Family-held primary sources

The 1987 family copy of Pugna a un dictador, the December 2, 1936 anniversary portrait and the photographed 1944 penitentiary registry. Caption-based identifications and hard-to-read handwriting are labeled as such.

Surname note: Gavidia may be Valencian Spanish and an adaptation of the Basque place-name Gabiria, but that origin is uncertain. Spanish marriage records carry the name into the 1600s, including the Granada area. Approximately 435 people bear it in Spain today, while El Salvador has the highest density worldwide. “Castro” is a place-name surname from many unrelated Spanish villages and cannot identify one origin by itself.

This is a growing family archive. Unknowns are left open until a record or firsthand family account fills them.
