The 1954 Martyrs' Plaque

The 1954 martyrs' plaque — Cementerio General de San Salvador

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: The plaque itself is undocumented online — no photo, no transcription exists on the public web. This is an in-person target, as the trip checklist already notes.

What is documented about the executions it commemorates

April 10, 1944 — paredón del Cementerio General [Documented — elsoca.org, Wikipedia "Huelga de brazos caídos", UES journal article "El golpe del 21 de octubre de 1944"]:

  • Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas
  • Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro (the UES article calls him "Capitán" — rank discrepancy)
  • Teniente Marcelino Calvo (elsoca.org calls him "Capitán" — rank discrepancy)
  • Teniente Miguel Ángel Linares
  • Teniente Ricardo Mancía González
  • Teniente Ricardo Edgardo Chacón (Wikipedia says "Teniente Eduardo Chacón")
  • Teniente Oscar Armando Cristales

The same day at the Policía Nacional: General Alfonso Marroquín, Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo, Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa.

April 11 — civil Víctor Manuel Marín, at the cemetery [Documented].

April 26 — Capitanes Carlos Piche Menéndez and Carlos Gavidia Castro, Teniente Alfonso Marín [Documented].

Combat deaths — Tenientes Héctor Cárdenas, Mario Villacorta; civil Luis Antonio Martí [Documented].

The author's front matter prints essentially the same April 10 roster, with Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas named first [Novel/front matter] — consistent with the leading hypothesis that "Flores Magaña" was Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas.

What the book says about the cemetery memorials

  • The Ocampo mausoleum: Coronel Roberto Ocampo built "una capillita lindísima, con una oración adecuada y por último decía 'Recuerdo de su esposa e hijos'" [Novel — tercera-parte-1.md]. This is a family mausoleum, not the martyrs' plaque, but it sits in the same cemetery and may be nearby.
  • May 1944: demonstrators marched to the cemetery to place floral offerings "en la tumba de los caídos" — the graves were known and visited from the start [Novel — segunda-parte-1.md].
  • 1974: the 30th anniversary was marked with a solemn mass; María visited the tomb [Novel — tercera-parte-1.md].
  • The book's epílogo names the full Council of War roster but says nothing about a 1954 plaque [Novel].

The 1954 context [Documented context, plaque itself Unverified]

  • 1954 was the 10th anniversary of the uprising, during the presidency of Óscar Osorio (1950–1956) — a post-Martínez military government that tolerated limited commemoration.
  • For comparison: the 25th anniversary (1969) produced Legislative Decree Nº 285 declaring April 2 a national holiday ("Fiesta Nacional, sin asueto"), initiated by deputies including Roberto Lara Velado [Documented — asamblea.gob.sv].
  • Who placed the 1954 plaque, what it says, and whose names it bears: unknown from online sources. The trip checklist's phrasing ("the 1954 plaque naming Capt. Carlos Gavidia Castro among the martyrs of April 2, 1944") comes from family knowledge, not a published source.

Burial locations

  • The April 10 officers were shot at the cemetery's paredón and buried there [Documented]; the May 1944 demonstrators knew the graves' location [Novel].
  • Calvo Marroquín remains: Tito and Alfonso in San Salvador, Marcelino in Izalco [Family record — Merceditas Ros testimony].
  • Whether the executed share a common grave or individual plots: unresolved. The cemetery's burial records (April 1944 entries) would settle it.

Unresolved

  • Plaque text and full name list — photograph and transcribe in person.
  • Who commissioned it in 1954 (families? veterans? government?).
  • Current state — does it survive? The cemetery has suffered vandalism (e.g., the Francisco Menéndez monument) [Documented — Scribd cemetery guide].
  • Exact location within the cemetery — ask the administration for the "paredón de 1944" or the martyrs' section.
  • Whether Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (executed April 26, not April 10) is named on it — the checklist says he is; verify.

Trip tasks (already on the checklist; refined)

  • Photograph the 1954 plaque; transcribe every name.
  • Locate the Ocampo family mausoleum ("Recuerdo de su esposa e hijos") — photograph; check whether Carlos Gavidia Castro's remains are there.
  • Cemetery administration: April 1944 burial entries — common grave vs. individual plots; map of the martyrs' section.
  • Check the plaque's physical condition; note any damage or missing names.

Evidence-strength summary

Execution rosters (Apr 10/11/26) [Documented] · Front-matter roster naming Sánchez Dueñas [Novel/front matter] · Ocampo mausoleum [Novel] · 1974 anniversary mass [Novel] · 1969 decree [Documented] · 1954 plaque existence/text/names [Unverified lead — family knowledge, in-person only]

Teniente Alfonso Marín

Teniente Alfonso Marín — Research Notes

Identity

  • Teniente Alfonso Marín — Salvadoran Army officer, executed April 26, 1944 at the Cementerio General, San Salvador, alongside Capitán Carlos Piche Menéndez and Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro. [Documented — Wikipedia "Huelga de brazos caídos en El Salvador de 1944"; elsoca.org; joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com]
  • Brother-in-law of Capitán Erazo (Guardia Nacional): married to Erazo's sister. Erazo's own words, quoted in Pugna a un dictador: "El Teniente Marín, es mi cuñado, esposo de mi hermana y el Capitán Gavidia Castro es mi compadre y amigo entrañable de armas." [Family record]
  • Named in the book's front-matter list of those sentenced by the Second Council of War (April 23, 1944): Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche, Teniente Alfonso Marín. [Family record]

The execution group (April 26, 1944)

All sources agree on the trio executed that day at the Cementerio General:

  • Capitán Carlos Piche Menéndez
  • Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro
  • Teniente Alfonso Marín

[Documented]

Note: the elsoca.org account lists additional executions that day (tenientes Héctor Cárdenas, Mario Villacorta, civil Luis Antonio Martí) as killed "en campaña" (in the field), distinguishing them from the three formally executed. Treat the exact April 26 roster with care — sources vary on who counts as "fusilado" vs. killed in combat.

Role in the uprising

  • No online source found detailing Marín's specific unit, posting, or actions on April 2–3, 1944.
  • The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier study (Mitos y palabras, Realidad No. 154, 2019) analyzes the court-martial expediente but its full text was not accessible in this pass; it may name his unit and testimony. [Scholarship — lead for follow-up]
  • His inclusion in the Second Council of War (April 23) — the same proceeding that condemned Gavidia Castro and Piche — suggests he was captured with or implicated alongside them, likely in the Santa Ana or San Salvador actions.

The Marín vs. "Marinelo" question [Unverified lead]

  • The book's narrative section mentions a "Teniente Marinelo", described as brother of the civilian Víctor Marín (captured April 11, tortured, executed nearly a corpse — per the book).
  • Víctor Marín (civilian) was executed April 11, 1944 at the Cementerio General — a different date and a civilian, so he is clearly NOT Alfonso Marín. [Documented]
  • "Marinelo" could be:
  • (a) A diminutive/nickname or variant spelling of Alfonso Marín (same person), or
  • (b) A different officer — the actual brother of Víctor Marín.
  • The book uses "Teniente Marín" (Erazo's quote, April 23 list) and "Teniente Marinelo" (narrative) in different passages. Do not conflate without the marriage record or trial dossier.

Family / descendants

  • Wife: Erazo's sister (first name unknown). Her marriage record to Alfonso Marín (~1930s, civil registry) is the key document — it would name her parents (Erazo's parents), witnesses, and possibly Erazo himself. [Unverified lead — trip task]
  • No children, descendants, or post-1944 family trace found online.
  • Erazo's sister became the widow of an executed officer at a young age; she may have remarried — check marriage indices under both Marín and her maiden name (once known).

What was NOT found

  • Full name beyond "Alfonso Marín" (no second surname).
  • Military unit, academy class (tanda), or service record.
  • Birth date/place.
  • Marriage record (not online; civil registry trip task).
  • Any photograph.
  • Any descendants.

Trip tasks

  • Alfonso Marín's marriage record (~1930s, San Salvador civil registry) — top priority; unlocks Erazo's sister and family.
  • Guardia Nacional / Army personnel files at the AGN for Marín's service record.
  • Diario Oficial officer appointments/promotions 1935–44 for "Alfonso Marín."
  • Trial dossier (expediente del Consejo de Guerra) — the Cáceres Prendes article confirms a copy exists; the AGN or UCA may hold it. It would give Marín's unit, testimony, and full name.
  • Cementerio General — burial records for April 26, 1944; the 1954 martyrs' plaque may list him.

Sources

  • https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huelga_de_brazos_ca%C3%ADdos_en_El_Salvador_de_1944
  • https://mail.elsoca.org/index.php/america-central/movimiento-obrero-y-socialismo-en-centroamerica/4686-el-salvador-9-de-mayo-de-1944-triunfa-huelga-general-que-tumba-al-dictador-maximiliano-hernandez-martinez
  • http://joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com/2026/06/maximiliano-hernandez-martinez-el.html
  • Cáceres Prendes, J. (2019/2020). "Mitos y palabras." Realidad No. 154, 5-52. https://www.camjol.info/index.php/REALIDAD/article/view/10102

Doctor Arturo Romero — the Civilian Leader

Doctor Arturo Romero López — "Doctor Córdova" in the book

Research: October 2026. Evidence labels: [Documented] / [Unverified lead].

Bottom line

Dr. Arturo Romero López (1911–1965) was one of the two civilian leaders of the April 2, 1944 uprising against Hernández Martínez — the other being Agustín Alfaro Morán. A Sorbonne-trained Hospital Rosales physician beloved by the poor, he seized a radio station with a submachine gun in hand on April 2, was sentenced to death in absentia, was machete-attacked fleeing toward Honduras, survived, and spent the rest of his life in Costa Rican exile as a public-health educator. He later ran for president in 1945 — on the same ballot as José Cipriano Castro Bernal, the captain's uncle. In the book he appears as "Doctor Córdova, líder de la revolución."

Biography [Documented]

  • Full name: Arturo Romero López.
  • Born: June 16, 1911, cantón Valle La Puerta, Tacuba, department of Ahuachapán, El Salvador. (One source gives 1904 — flag as variant; 1911 is supported by Wikipedia, La Prensa Gráfica, and UES materials.)
  • Wife: Coralia Ávila de Romero — "le acompañó en su lucha por la democracia." She directed a ballet company.
  • Education: Medicine at the Universidad de La Sorbona (Sorbonne), Paris, France — described as a brilliant student.
  • Medical career: Returned to El Salvador 1941; head of a unit at Hospital Rosales; wrote in the press on social issues. In 1943 co-founded the Colegio Médico de El Salvador (COLMEDES) with 21 other physicians.
  • Politics: Founded the Partido Unión Democrática (PUD) in 1944. Joined Acción Democrática Social on return from Europe (1941).
  • Death: June 29, 1965, Choluteca, Honduras — car accident, age 54. His wife Coralia and several members of her ballet company died with him; they were traveling to a charity performance for Honduran children with polio.
  • Citizenship: Salvadoran by birth; also held Costa Rican citizenship. Declared "Ciudadano de Honor" (Honorary Citizen) by Costa Rica's Legislative Assembly on September 28, 1965 (posthumous).

Role in April 1944 [Documented]

  • The plot was devised by Colonels Tito Tomás Calvo and Alfredo Aguilar; it was "accepted by the two civilian leaders of the movement and staunch opponents of the regime: Dr. Arturo Romero and Agustín Alfaro Morán" (Wikipedia, "Huelga de brazos caídos").
  • Known as "El hombre símbolo de la Revolución" (the symbolic man of the Revolution).
  • On April 2 he led civilians who seized radio stations YSP and YSR, broadcasting messages supporting the revolt. Among those at YSP: Matilde Elena López and María Loucel (two women), plus doctors, journalists, lawyers. One account: "metralleta en mano, se tomó una radio para dar lectura a un pronunciamiento contra la dictadura."
  • Sentenced to death in absentia (April 11 list) along with Agustín Alfaro Morán; both fled.
  • April 5, 1944: captured/machete-attacked at San Luis La Reina, Chalatenango, while fleeing toward Honduras — facial wounds from border guards' bullets and blades. Cared for by the San Miguel medical guild. Dr. Fabio Castillo (then a student) traveled to San Miguel proposing an armed commando to extract and protect him; the plan fell through when Martínez fled the country after the May strike.
  • Photographed with visible facial scars arriving in Mexico City with his wife aboard a Pan American flight, en route to Rochester, Minnesota for treatment.
  • Exiled to Costa Rica.

After 1944 [Documented]

  • 1945 presidential candidate (Partido Unión Demócrata). Fellow candidates included Salvador Castaneda Castro, Coronel Antonio Claramount Lucero, Napoleón Viera Altamirano — and José Cipriano Castro, the captain's uncle (Partido Popular Progresista). The election was fraudulent; Castaneda Castro was declared winner without published results. Romero testified he suffered torture and death threats that forced his Costa Rica exile.
  • Lived in Costa Rica until 1965: public-health educator, ran mobile health units to rural communities; remembered for "corazón noble y altruista."

Legacy [Documented]

  • Ciudadano de Honor, Costa Rica (Legislative Assembly, Sept 28, 1965).
  • Photograph displayed in the auditorium of the Universidad de Costa Rica's Faculty of Education, honoring his work as an educator.
  • UES: Dean José Raymundo Calderón Morán lectured on his life and work at COLMEDES's 70th anniversary (2013); a photographic exhibition in his memory.
  • Press retrospectives: El Independiente ("La Aurora del 2 de abril de 1944," Apr 2022), Diario El Mundo ("El legado del Dr. Arturo Romero López," Jul 2022), La Prensa Gráfica ("El legado altruista del galeno y político," 2025), Joaquín Rivera Larios ("DR. ARTURO ROMERO, EL HOMBRE SÍMBOLO DE LA REVOLUCIÓN," 2020).

The book connection [Confirmed]

  • Pugna a un dictador, primera parte: "El Doctor Córdova, líder de la revolución era persona querida y estimada por la gente pobre, pues en ese sector él hacía caridades como Médico, aliviando el dolor y el hambre."
  • This matches Romero exactly: Hospital Rosales doctor, "de gran arraigo popular," documented civilian leader of the uprising. He also appears under his real name in the author's front-matter April 11 death-sentence list — the book's standard pattern (pseudonym in narrative, real name in the lists).

Dots to the Gavidia story

  • José Cipriano Castro Bernal (the captain's uncle, exiled 1938) ran for president in 1945 on the same ballot as Romero — two opposition figures, both marked men of the Martínez era, both in exile's shadow.
  • Agustín Alfaro Morán, the other civilian leader, is also in the book's April 11 list ("Don Agustín Alfaro Morán") — a future dig target.
  • Matilde Elena López (seized radio YSP with Romero) is a known Salvadoran writer/poet — possible literary connection to explore.
  • Dr. Fabio Castillo, the student who tried to rescue Romero in San Miguel, became a major UES figure — another thread.
  • Romero survived; the Gavidia brothers did not. His exile-to-Costa-Rica path parallels Dagoberto's diplomatic survival — the ones who lived carried the story.

Descendants [Unverified lead]

  • No children mentioned in any source found. His wife Coralia died with him in 1965. No known living descendants traced. The ballet company connection (Coralia's troupe) is an unexplored avenue.

Sources

  • es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arturo_Romero (stub; cites the three articles below)
  • La Prensa Gráfica, "El legado altruista del galeno y político Dr. Arturo Romero López" (2025): https://mediacenter.elgrafico.com/opinion/El-legado-altruista-del-galeno-y-politico-Dr.-Arturo-Romero-Lopez-20250923-0085.html
  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "DR. ARTURO ROMERO, EL HOMBRE SÍMBOLO DE LA REVOLUCIÓN" (Dec 2020): http://joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com/2020/12/drarturo-romero-el-hombre-simbolo-de-la.html
  • Diario El Mundo, "El legado del Dr. Arturo Romero López" (Jul 2022)
  • El Independiente, "La Aurora del 2 de abril de 1944" (Apr 4, 2022)
  • UES "El Búho" (Jul 2013), Dean Calderón Morán lecture: http://periodismo.humanidades.ues.edu.sv/sites/default/files/buho/Buho11 julio2013_0.pdf
  • salvadorenosporelmundo.net, "Salvadoreños que han dejado huella en Costa Rica"
  • es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huelga_de_brazos_caídos_en_El_Salvador_de_1944 (plot leadership)

  • Pugna a un dictador, transcripción primera-parte-2.md:1121

Trip tasks

  • COLMEDES headquarters (San Salvador): founding records 1943, Romero file.
  • UES: Calderón Morán lecture materials; photo exhibition records.
  • Diario Oficial 1944–45: PUD founding, 1945 candidacy.
  • Hospital Rosales archives: his unit, 1941–44.

The Trial Files Found (Cáceres Prendes)

Cáceres Prendes — Full Article Analysis

Date: October 7, 2026

Sources:

  • Jorge Cáceres Prendes, "PALABRAS. La memoria histórica del alzamiento militar salvadoreño del dos de abril de 1944 a la luz del expediente del juicio correspondiente," Diálogos: Revista Electrónica de Historia, Número especial 2008 (Universidad de Costa Rica). Full text: https://revistas.ucr.ac.cr/index.php/dialogos/article/download/31797/31485/93301
  • Jorge Cáceres Prendes, "Mitos y palabras: El expediente del juicio militar contra los alzados del 2 de abril de 1944. Memoria histórica e historia como representación dramática," Realidad: Revista de Ciencias Sociales y Humanidades No. 154 (2019), pp. 5–52. Full text: https://portal.amelica.org/ameli/journal/769/7695245006/7695245006.pdf

Status: Both full texts obtained and read. Key findings below.

---

1. THE ARCHIVE — where the expediente photocopy was to go [CRITICAL]

Biblioteca "P. Florentino Idoate" de la Universidad Centroamericana "José Simeón Cañas" (UCA), San Salvador.

Exact quote from the 2019 article: "Para efectos de constatación y acceso al público, depositaremos una copia de este documento en la biblioteca 'P. Florentino Idoate' de la Universidad Centroamericana 'José Simeón Cañas' en San Salvador. Aunque se trata de una fotocopia, el texto es suficientemente legible en su estado actual."

Caveats:

  • Both articles use future tense — 2008: "me propongo depositar" (I intend to deposit); 2019: "depositaremos" (we will deposit). Neither confirms the deposit actually happened.
  • The 2019 article is signed "Santa Ana, Costa Rica. Mayo de 2009" — written in 2009, published 2019.
  • Trip task: verify at the UCA library whether the photocopy is actually there. Ask for the expediente del Consejo de Guerra, abril 1944.
  • The expediente photocopy is NOT online — not on archive.org, not in any institutional repository found.

---

2. The expediente itself

  • Two pieces. Piece 1: opened April 4, 1944 at 7:00 a.m., closed April 26 — 261 folios. Piece 2: 44 folios, last declaration taken May 3, 1944.
  • 47 defendant testimonies, 64 witness testimonies.
  • First Council of War (constituted night of April 9, sentenced 2:00 a.m. April 10): condemned 25 of 29 to death (23 military, 6 civilian formally indicted at that point). 13 military + 1 civilian actually executed (those present).
  • Second Council (April 22–23): condemned 19 to death (15 military, 4 civilian). 3 military executed.
  • Military prosecutor (Fiscal Militar): Tte. Cnel. Andrés Eulalio Castillo.

---

3. Council of War membership [DOCUMENTED]

Gral. Luis Andreu, Cnel. Santiago Ayala, Cnel. Joaquín Adolfo Fermán.

Chosen by sorteo (lottery) the night of April 9 (fs. 95).

Santiago Ayala's battlefield role (2008 article): Commander of the Artillery barracks ("El Zapote"). His unexpected return foiled the rebel plan — he reversed his second-in-command's (Cnel. Díaz) decision to surrender the barracks and captured two rebel officers who had come as "parliamentarians" to receive the surrender. The author writes this single event "may have been decisive for the future of the whole project."

Relevance: Ayala was Ricardo's original second-husband hypothesis (retired in favor of Lovo Castelar, but the Ayala-on-the-council fact is now documentary).

---

4. Juan Ramón Merino [DOCUMENTED]

  • Subteniente Juan Ramón Merino, declaración at fs. 40.
  • His defense: he was obeying orders — specifically Marroquín's orders, since Marroquín "secundaba todo lo que su hermano Tito decía" (seconded everything his brother Tito said/did).
  • Cited in both articles as the example of junior officers who defended themselves by claiming obedience to superiors.
  • This is the first documentary proof of the tank officer, independent of Ricardo's family story and the Alfaro biography.
  • Still open: Merino's fate after the trial (condemned? amnestied? — he does not appear in the condemned lists reproduced in the 2019 article). The Ciudad Delgado tank episode appears in neither article — it lives only in Relámpagos de Libertad + family memory. The "coronel de la marina" discrepancy is unresolved: the 1944 record has him as an army subteniente.

---

5. The Gavidia brothers in the expediente

Antonio Gavidia Castro:

  • Listed as Subteniente Antonio Gavidia Castro among the April 10 condemned (military).
  • His testimony/declaration at fs. 17.
  • Footnote 72 (2019): "Los oficiales capturados fueron el Capt. Manuel Sánchez Dueñas y el Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro, posteriormente fusilados." — Captured together, both later executed. (Note: "Teniente" here vs. "Subteniente" in the condemned list — rank discrepancy in the source.)

Carlos Gavidia Castro:

  • Listed as Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro among the April 23 condemned (military), alongside Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche Menéndez, Capitán Alfredo Jiménez Barrios, Tte.-Cnel. Francisco Acosta, Capitán Fernando Carmona Dárdano, Teniente Silvio López y López, and others.
  • The 2008 article contains zero mentions of "Gavidia" by name (full-text search) — the brothers appear by name only in the 2019 article's condemned lists and footnotes.

José Gavidia Castro — NEW family witness:

  • Footnote 52 (2019): "La cita aparece en Relámpagos (p. 151) atribuida al señor José Gavidia Castro, uno de los entrevistados de Castro Morán."
  • Also: "Ver también entrevista al Sr. José Gavidia Castro en Relámpagos op. cit. pp. 136 a 140."
  • This is almost certainly Juan José Gavidia Castro (eldest brother, 1912–2016), using "José" as his name (per the pseudonym key). He was interviewed by Mariano Castro Morán for Relámpagos de Libertad — a 4-page interview (pp. 136–140) plus a citation at p. 151. This is a firsthand family witness in print that we had not previously identified.
  • Footnote 62: Castro Morán records José Gavidia Castro's testimony that after making his declaration, Tito Calvo told him Marroquín "había descargado en él (en Tito Calvo) toda la responsabilidad" (had dumped all responsibility on him) but that Calvo himself "acepté totalmente la responsabilidad… tal vez así se salva el pobre viejo" (accepted total responsibility… maybe that way the poor old man saves himself).

---

6. Manuel Sánchez Dueñas

  • Listed as Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas among the April 10 condemned (military).
  • 2008 article: "Sánchez Dueñas admitió su culpabilidad sin acusar a nadie más" — admitted his guilt without accusing anyone else.
  • Captured together with Antonio Gavidia Castro; both executed April 10.

---

7. Tito Calvo and the tanks

  • Footnote 61 (2019): Calvo "se había identificado a sí mismo como líder del alzamiento ante muchas personas" and on April 3 acted "imprudentemente… saliendo del 1er. Regimiento en un tanque en varias oportunidades" — once to contact troops arriving from Santa Ana, and the last time to seek asylum at the American legation, confident it would be granted. It was denied. Captured at the exit with his companions (per testimonies of Tte. Oscar Armando Cristales fs. 10 and Subtte. Ricardo Mancía González fs. 15 v.).
  • The Calvo–Marroquín command dynamic: Marroquín is described as seconding everything Tito said/did; junior officers (including Merino) cited obedience to Marroquín as their defense.
  • The condemned lists confirm the family structure: Cnel. Tito Tomás Calvo, Tte. Marcelino Calvo (military); Doctor Mario Calvo, Doctor Tomás Calvo, Víctor Marín (civilians).

---

8. Civilian condemned (April 10)

Doctor Arturo Romero, Agustín Alfaro Morán, Doctor Francisco Guillermo Pérez, Doctor Mario Calvo, Doctor Tomás Calvo, Víctor Marín.

This independently confirms our civilian-network identifications (Romero, Alfaro Morán) in the trial record.

---

9. What's NOT in the articles

  • No Ciudad Delgado. Neither article mentions Ciudad Delgado or the captain's tank episode there. That detail lives only in Relámpagos + Ricardo's memory.
  • No Coralia/Marta/Lemus. Nothing about the love-triangle thread.
  • No Lovo Castelar. Nothing about the second husband.
  • No "stolen files" resolution. The articles don't address the widows being told the files were stolen — but the expediente's existence in private hands (the author's photocopy) is consistent with the originals being inaccessible.

---

10. Trip targets from this research

  • Biblioteca "P. Florentino Idoate", UCA, San Salvador — ask whether the expediente photocopy was actually deposited. Target folios: fs. 17 (Antonio Gavidia's testimony), fs. 40 (Merino's testimony), fs. 95 (Council sorteo), fs. 103v–106v (April 10 sentence).
  • Relámpagos de Libertad pp. 136–140, 151 — José (Juan José) Gavidia Castro's interview. Ricardo owns this book.
  • The author's 1994 contribution to "Historia de El Salvador" (Ministerio de Educación) also used expediente material — a secondary access point if the UCA deposit didn't happen.

---

Report compiled October 7, 2026. Both article PDFs: full text obtained via open-access journal URLs. No one contacted.

Capitán Erazo — the Officer Who Refused

Capitán Erazo — Research Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: First name still unknown, but major new leads via the brother-in-law connection.

What the book says [Family record]

From the full transcription of Pugna a un dictador (front matter, author's note):

> "No quiero pasar por alto, la valentía del entonces Capitán Erazo, que al sortear, a quien le tocaría llevar la voz de mando en los Segundos Consejos de Guerra y dar el tiro de gracia; tuvo la mala suerte de ser él el elegido en el sorteo... Entonces valientemente, entregó su espada y dijo: Yo no puedo obedecer esa orden, estoy dispuesto a recibir el castigo que el reglamento me imponga."

Key details from the passage:

  • Erazo was a Captain in the Guardia Nacional (National Guard), not the regular army.
  • The Director of the Guardia Nacional questioned him personally: "¿Por qué desobedece usted la órden dada?"
  • Erazo's reply (verbatim): "El Teniente Marín, es mi cuñado, esposo de mi hermana y el Capitán Gavidia Castro es mi compadre y amigo entrañable de armas, así lo he considerado y estimado siempre."
  • The book notes that in that era "reinaba el pánico entre los militares y nadie se atrevía a desobedecer las órdenes de sus superiores."

The brother-in-law: Teniente Alfonso Marín [Documented]

The book's front matter lists those sentenced in the Second Council of War (April 23, 1944): Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche, and Teniente Alfonso Marín.

Multiple independent sources confirm Teniente Alfonso Marín was executed April 26, 1944 alongside the two captains:

  • Wikipedia ("Huelga de brazos caídos en El Salvador de 1944")
  • elsoca.org (May 9, 1944 strike history)
  • joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com (Martínez biography)

So Erazo's sister was the widow of an executed officer. Her marriage record to Alfonso Marín is the single best document for identifying the Erazo family — it would name her parents (Erazo's parents) and possibly witnesses.

The "Marinelo" complication [Family record / Unverified lead]

The book's narrative section also mentions a "Teniente Marinelo", described as brother of the civilian Víctor Marín (captured April 11, tortured, executed nearly a corpse). It is unclear whether "Marinelo" is:

  • A variant spelling/nickname of Alfonso Marín (same person), or
  • A different officer (possibly a relative).

The book uses "Teniente Marín" (Erazo's quote, April 23 list) and "Teniente Marinelo" (narrative) in different passages. Do not conflate without the marriage record.

What was NOT found

  • Erazo's first name — no web source names him. He does not appear in the 44 condemned (consistent with surviving 1944).
  • Post-1944 fate — unknown whether he was punished for refusing, cashiered, or continued in the Guardia Nacional.
  • Descendants — none traced.
  • The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier article ("Mitos y palabras") was not accessible in full text (PDF link 404); it analyzes the condemned, so Erazo likely isn't in it, but he could appear as a witness.

Best next steps (trip tasks)

  • Alfonso Marín's marriage record (civil registry, ~1930s) — names Erazo's sister, her parents, witnesses. This unlocks the Erazo family.
  • Guardia Nacional personnel files at the Archivo General de la Nación — Erazo's service record, rank, assignments.
  • Diario Oficial officer appointments/promotions 1935–1944 — captains of the Guardia Nacional named Erazo.
  • Baptism records — Erazo as godfather (compadre) to Gavidia children; the captain's children's baptisms may name him with full name.

Assessment

The brother-in-law lead is the breakthrough: it converts an unfindable first name into a findable marriage record. Erazo himself left almost no paper trail online, but Alfonso Marín's documented execution gives us a firm anchor — find the marriage, find the sister, find the family.

The Civilian Network

The Civilian Network Around Dr. Arturo Romero — April 2, 1944

Agustín Alfaro Morán [Documented]

  • Who: A coffee grower (cafetalero) from Ahuachapán. In 1930 he founded Beneficio El Carmen in Concepción de Ataco — a coffee estate still operating four generations later (tours, gourmet brand, international buyers).
  • Role: Wikipedia's account of the 1944 coup states the plan was "elaborated by Colonels Tito Calvo and Alfredo Aguilar, being accepted by the two civilian leaders of the movement... Dr. Arturo Romero and Agustín Alfaro Morán." He was one of the two civilian chiefs.
  • Fate: Sentenced to death in absentia (April 11) alongside Romero; fled the country. [Documented]
  • After: A UCA journal (Estudios Centroamericanos) calls him "el liberal y demócrata Agustín Alfaro Morán" — "the true manager and promoter of the fall of the dictator" and a pillar of the class alliances behind the 1950 constitutional pact. He survived to shape the post-Martínez order.
  • Note: Not to be confused with the Gavidia-connected "Hacienda El Carmen" (San Pablo Tacachico) — different property, likely coincidental name.

Matilde Elena López [Documented]

  • Who: Born February 20, 1919, San Salvador. Daughter of María Carlota López and Dr. Humberto A. Fischnaler (Austrian-German). Raised by her grandmother Adela López. Already publishing poetry and essays as a young woman.
  • April 2, 1944: At radio YSP with Dr. Romero when the civilians seized the station. After Romero's introduction she took the microphone and shouted "¡Ha sonado la hora de la liberación!" — then the government cut the power. Her family, listening at home, thought she had been killed.
  • After: A month in hiding ("se nos abrieron puertas imposibles de creer"), then exile — first Guatemala (studied journalism at Universidad de San Carlos), then Ecuador (PhD in Philosophy and Letters, Universidad Central del Ecuador — one of the first Salvadoran women to earn it).
  • Return: Back to El Salvador in 1957; joined the Universidad de El Salvador — director of the Departamento de Letras, director of Extensión Cultural, vicedecana of Humanities. Became one of the great figures of 20th-century Salvadoran letters (poet, essayist, playwright, scholar).
  • Also at the radio: the poet María Loucel seized the microphones alongside her; both paid with forced exile. [Documented — diariolatino]

Dr. Fabio Castillo Figueroa [Documented]

  • Who: Born March 1921 (turned 90 in March 2011). Medical student at UES, 21 years old in 1942; admirer of Morazán.
  • The Comité Secreto: After Holy Week 1944, ~40–50 students met at La Rotonda and declared an indefinite strike; they named a secret committee — Jorge Bustamante (Medicine), Reynaldo Galindo Pohl (Law), and Fabio Castillo — to direct the fight against the dictatorship.
  • The rescue attempt: Romero, captured and slashed across the face with a machete, lay in the Hospital of San Miguel under guard. Castillo — who had already worked with Romero and knew his plans — boarded a train in a borrowed doctor's white coat, walked into the hospital unchallenged, found Romero's room, and proposed a breakout in French (both men spoke it; Romero trained in France). Romero, already condemned to death, accepted — "da igual que lo maten tratando de escapar." Back in San Salvador, the committee realized they had no way to execute it without ending up against the same wall, and aborted the plan. Castillo later called it: "El plan era absurdo."
  • After: Graduated MD 1947 (thesis on malnutrition in San Salvador's working-class barrios). Became a prominent academic and physiologist; a lifelong anti-dictatorship conspirator — in 1960 he sat on the Junta de Gobierno that ousted Lemus, as the civilian representative alongside military officers. Recognized as a member of the clandestine May-1944 strike committee.

How the civilian and military plots connected [Documented]

  • Authorship: The military side (Colonels Tito Calvo and Alfredo Aguilar) elaborated the operational plan; the civilian chiefs (Romero, Alfaro Morán) accepted it. Two tracks, one conspiracy.
  • Intelligence: Mario Sol — who routinely visited his cotton properties and coastal resorts — was tasked with tracking Martínez's movements; the plan hinged on capturing the president when he left San Salvador.
  • The radio arm: At 14:30 on April 2, civilians meeting at the Casino Salvadoreño forced their way into private radio YSP and began broadcasting the uprising — the propaganda strike meant to demoralize the regime. It backfired operationally: the broadcasts told Martínez exactly which military units were compromised.
  • The full roster (diariolatino, cross-checked): civilians Crescencio Castellanos Rivas, Agustín Alfaro Morán, Víctor Manuel Marín, Drs. Arturo Romero, Julio Jiménez Castillo, Francisco Guillermo Pérez; military — Gen. Alfonso Marroquín, Col. Tito Tomás Calvo, Capts. Carlos Gavidia Castro, Fernando Carmona, Guillermo Fuentes Castellanos; Tenientes incl. Mariano Castro Morán; Subteniente Antonio Gavidia Castro; aviation cadet Enrique Aberle.
  • Note the Marín name: "Víctor Manuel Marín" appears among the civilians — alongside the book's executed civilian Víctor Marín (April 11) and Teniente Alfonso Marín (executed April 26, Erazo's brother-in-law). Possible family cluster; unresolved. [Unverified lead]

Dots for the family story

  • Romero's civilian network and the Gavidia military circle were parts of one conspiracy, linked through Calvo/Aguilar (military planners) and Romero/Alfaro Morán (civilian chiefs).
  • Matilde Elena López and María Teresa González are parallel figures: two young women of the same generation pulled into April 1944 — one at the radio microphone, one pleading at the prison gates. López survived to become a national literary figure; worth comparing their trajectories.
  • Fabio Castillo later sat in government (1960) — the student conspirator became a statesman, like Mariano Castro Morán's arc.
  • Trip/research leads: Beneficio El Carmen (Ataco) — still operating, run by Alfaro Morán's descendants; UES archives on López and Castillo; the Casino Salvadoreño; YSP station history.

Coralia's Birth Year Puzzle

Coralia Párraga de Lemus — Birth Year Investigation (Follow-up)

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: The 1927 birth year is SINGLE-SOURCED and uncorroborated. Age problem UNRESOLVED.

---

Bottom line

Every online source that gives Coralia Párraga de Lemus's birth year says 1927 — but all of them trace back to one author: Joaquín Rivera Larios, in two blogspot articles, both writing "(1927-2011)" parenthetically with no cited source. No baptism record, no marriage record, no obituary, and no independent biography corroborates or contradicts 1927. The age problem (8–9 years old when the captain courted the book's adult "Marta" ~1935–36) stands exactly where it was: only her birth record — or the full text of her "La verdad del golpe de los compadres" interview — can settle it.

---

What was checked

The 1927 source, traced
  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "EL INTELECTUAL QUE LLEGÓ A SER PRESIDENTE" (blogspot, Aug 2023): "...la ex Primera Dama de la República, doña Coralia Párraga de Lemus (1927-2011)..."
  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "PRIMERAS DAMAS SALVADOREÑAS MÁS ALLÁ DE NUESTRAS FRONTERAS" (blogspot, Jul 2024): "Doña Coralia Párraga de Lemus (1927-2011), destacada por su belleza y elegancia..."

Same author, same parenthetical, no source cited in either. This is one claim, not two.

Confirmations (identity, not birth year)
  • Wikipedia "Primera dama de El Salvador": Coralia Párraga de Lemus, First Lady Sept 14, 1956 – Oct 26, 1960. [Confirmed]
  • Lemus details from Rivera Larios: IV promoción 1933, primera antigüedad, subteniente Oct 20, 1933; died March 31, 1993, San José, Costa Rica; family refused Cristiani's state-honors burial offer. [Confirmed — matches prior research]
  • The "golpe de los compadres" (Oct 26, 1960): Coralia gave the interview "La verdad del golpe de los compadres" to Mayor Ernesto García, describing the assault on the presidential residence. She donated Lemus's personal effects to the Museo Militar. [Confirmed]
  • A 1959–60 Puerto Rico State Dept. expense report lists an "Agasajo a la Sra. Coralia Párraga de Lemus, Primera Dama de El Salvador" (May 1959). [Confirmed — she was active as First Lady]
  • A UFG thesis on early childhood centers notes the first guarderías were organized ~1950 "a iniciativa del sector femenino del PRUD, presidido por la primera Dama de ese entonces, Señora Coralia Párraga de Lemus." Note: this is chronologically odd (Lemus took office 1956; the "primera dama de ese entonces" in 1950 would have been Esperanza Llerena de Osorio). Possibly a thesis error — or Coralia was already leading PRUD women's organizing before 1956, which would fit an adult woman born ~1915–20 better than a 23-year-old. [Flagged, not decisive]
Not found
  • No baptism record for Coralia Párraga (any year) online.
  • No marriage record for Lemus × Párraga (would list both spouses' ages) online.
  • No obituary (2011) with biographical detail online. US "Lemus" obituaries found are unrelated families.
  • No digitized text of the Ernesto García "La verdad del golpe de los compadres" interview. Rivera Larios says it "aparece en la red" (appears online) but no searchable copy was located.
  • No FamilySearch-accessible civil record (would require login; trip task).

---

Assessment

The 1927 date is weaker than it looked: a single unsourced parenthetical by one blogger, repeated twice. It is entirely possible the date is wrong — bloggers routinely carry forward unchecked dates. It is equally possible it is right and Ricardo's identification is wrong. There is no online evidence either way. This cannot be resolved from the web.

Decisive documents (trip / records tasks)

  • Coralia Párraga's birth/baptism record — the single document that ends the question.
  • Lemus × Párraga marriage record — lists ages of both spouses.
  • Full text of the Ernesto García interview ("La verdad del golpe de los compadres") — may contain biographical details (age at marriage, courtship timeline).
  • 2011 obituary / cemetery record (she died 2011, presumably in Costa Rica where the family lived in exile — or El Salvador).

Publication guidance

Do NOT assert "Marta = Coralia de Lemus" as fact. Current status: intriguing family identification, blocked by a single-sourced birth year. If the birth record shows ~1915–1920, the identification becomes strong (classmate's wife, right era, right name, right social circle). If it shows 1927, Ricardo's identification needs a full rethink.

Sources

  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "EL INTELECTUAL QUE LLEGÓ A SER PRESIDENTE," http://joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com/2023/08/el-intelectual-que-llego-ser-presidente.html
  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "PRIMERAS DAMAS SALVADOREÑAS MÁS ALLÁ DE NUESTRAS FRONTERAS," http://joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com/2024/07/primeras-damas-salvadorenas-mas-alla-de.html
  • Wikipedia (ES), "Primera dama de El Salvador."
  • UFG thesis, "Influencia del juego educativo..." (ri.ufg.edu.sv) — guarderías/PRUD women's sector reference.
  • Inter-American University (Puerto Rico), State Dept. representation expenses 1959–60 (dspace3.cai.sg.inter.edu) — Coralia as First Lady, May 1959.

Coronel Ayala — Superseded Hypothesis

The Real Coronel Ayala — María Teresa's Second Husband

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: Leading hypothesis identified; NOT confirmed. Key contradictions documented.

Correction: The pseudonym key's "Confirmed" mapping of "Coronel Roberto Ocampo" is wrong on two counts: (1) "Roberto Ocampo" is very likely a pseudonym, and (2) the real surname is Ayala (per Carlos's family notes: "María Teresa's second marriage (Ayala)" — children Gloria Gavidia Ayala and Herbert Ayala).

What the book says about him [Family record / Novel]

From the Third Part transcription (tercera-parte-1.md):

  • Young officer in 1944, already in love with María, married to Ana (rumored a government informant).
  • Courts María ~1946 ("two years a widow"); civil wedding at Christmas season, officiated by the Governor.
  • Described as "un hombre alto, arrogante, de mediana edad" (tall, arrogant, middle-aged) ~1946–48.
  • "Próximo a su ascenso a Teniente Coronel y candidato a Ministro de Guerra, Marina y Aviación" ~1950 (line 531).
  • His promotion classmates ("compañeros de tanda") celebrate his appointment as Minister (line 777).
  • Assumes the ministry ~1950; resigns in 1956 when the new president takes office; retires from the Army; administers properties.
  • María separates from him for a year because "he voted for the executions" (he voted to execute people; she couldn't forgive it).
  • His mother: "Doña Delia" (present from Part 1: "Doña Delia, la madre de Roberto").
  • First wife Ana divorces him, marries "un alto funcionario diplomático venezolano" who becomes Ambassador of Venezuela. They have two sons. Their daughter with Roberto debuts in society.
  • His parents alive ~1946 ("Los padres de Roberto se miraban felices").
  • Children with María: Elizabeth (daughter, ~1948–49, cesarean) and a son (early 1950s, "el retrato de su padre").

The "Minister" claim is contradicted [Documented]

The documented Ministers of Defense under Óscar Osorio (1950–56):

  • Óscar Adán Bolaños: 14 Sept 1950 – 9 Dec 1953.
  • Marco Antonio Molina: 9 Dec 1953 – 14 Sept 1956.
  • Source: Lista de Ministros, Ministerio de la Defensa Nacional (es.wikipedia.org).

No Ayala held the Defense portfolio under Osorio. The book's "Ministro de Guerra, Marina y Aviación" / "Ministro de Defensa" claim appears novelized — OR refers to a different senior position María Teresa elevated in the telling, OR the timeline is shifted. The book uses three different titles interchangeably ("Guerra, Marina y Aviación" — the pre-1939 name; "Guerra y Aviación"; "Defensa"), suggesting loose/novelistic usage rather than precision.

The three documented military Ayalas

1. Coronel Santiago Ayala — LEADING HYPOTHESIS [Unverified lead, strong circumstantial]

Why he's the leading candidate:

  • Sat on the April 1944 Council of War as a propietario (with Gen. Luis Andreu and Col. Joaquín Fermán) — the council that sentenced 25 to death on April 10 (including José Antonio Gavidia Castro) and 19 more on April 23 (including Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, Piche, and Marín). Source: Cáceres Prendes, "Mitos y palabras" (2019).
  • The book says María separated from Roberto for a year because "he voted for the executions." If Roberto = Santiago Ayala, this is LITERALLY TRUE — he voted to execute her first husband and his brother. No other Ayala sat on the council. This is the single strongest dot.
  • Commanded the El Zapote artillery barracks (Dec 1948 — absent visiting his sick wife when the 1948 coup took it).
  • Member of the June 30, 1944 military junta (listed as teniente coronel).
  • Rank by 1944: Colonel. If born ~1900–1910, he'd be "mediana edad" (middle-aged) in 1946 — fits the book's physical description.

Contradictions:

  • The book says Roberto was "próximo a su ascenso a Teniente Coronel" ~1950. Santiago was already a Colonel in April 1944. These cannot both be literally true. (But the book is novelized, written 40 years later; ranks may be misremembered or deliberately altered as part of the pseudonymization.)
  • No biography, birth/death dates, or marriage record found online for Santiago Ayala.
  • No documented link between Santiago Ayala and María Teresa beyond the hypothesis.
2. Coronel Felipe Ayala [Documented, weaker candidate]
  • Member of the June 30, 1944 military junta (listed as Colonel).
  • Nothing else found. No council membership, no biography.
3. General José María López Ayala [Documented, poor fit]
  • Teniente Coronel, second-in-command of the Guardia Nacional (1948); convinced to support the 1948 coup.
  • Director General of the Guardia Nacional, 1949–1953 (as General). Created its intelligence section.
  • Doesn't fit: already a General by 1949 (book's Roberto only makes Colonel ~1950); Guardia Nacional track, not Defense Minister; left the directorship in 1953 (book's Roberto resigns 1956).

The "Ana married a Venezuelan Ambassador" lead [Unverified lead]

The book says Roberto's first wife Ana, after divorcing him, married a high Venezuelan diplomatic official who became Ambassador of Venezuela, with whom she had two sons. This is specific and verifiable in principle — Venezuelan diplomatic postings to El Salvador in the late 1940s–1950s. No match found online. Trip/research lead: Venezuelan Foreign Ministry archives, Diario Oficial diplomatic accreditations.

The "Doña Delia" lead [Unverified lead]

The book names Roberto's mother as "Doña Delia" from Part 1 onward. If "Delia" is real (or based on real), the husband's mother was named Delia — i.e., we're looking for a Coronel Ayala, son of Delia. No "Delia Ayala" or "Delia ... de Ayala" found online. The ~1946 marriage record would name his parents.

Children [Family record]

  • Gloria Gavidia Ayala — daughter of the second marriage (Carlos's aunt). No web trace under this name.
  • Herbert Ayala — son of the second marriage (Carlos's uncle, "dad's half-brother"). A "Dr. Herbert Ayala" (urologist, San Salvador, active practice) appears online but is almost certainly a younger namesake, not the ~70-year-old uncle. No confirmed trace.
  • Note: "Herbert" is an unusual name for El Salvador in that era — potentially useful for record searches.

What would settle it (in order)

  • The ~1946 marriage record (Registro Civil, San Salvador) — names the husband in full, his parents, his age and profession. DECISIVE. Trip task.
  • Santiago Ayala's military personnel file (AGN) — full name, birth, marriages, assignments. Would confirm or kill the leading hypothesis.
  • Diario Oficial 1950–56 — cabinet lists, promotions, appointments. Would clarify what position the real husband actually held (and whether the "Minister" claim has any basis).
  • Ask the cousins — Gloria and Herbert's generation may know their father's full name and story directly. (No outreach without Carlos's approval.)
  • Venezuelan diplomatic records — the "Ana married the Ambassador" lead.

Bottom line

Leading hypothesis: Coronel Santiago Ayala — the only Ayala on the April 1944 Council of War, which makes the book's "he voted for the executions" (the cause of their year-long separation) literally true. But the book's "Minister" claim is contradicted by the documented record, the rank timeline doesn't fit, and there is no direct documentation linking Santiago Ayala to María Teresa. The ~1946 marriage record is the decisive document. Confidence: unverified lead, strong circumstantial — not confirmed.

Coronel Jorge Tenorio

Coronel Jorge Tenorio — research findings (2026-10-04)

Target: the "Coronel Jorge Tenorio" María Teresa González names in the dedication of

Pugna a un dictador (July 1987) as her cousin who comforted her when she pleaded for

her husband's life after the April 1944 uprising. Each finding marked CONFIRMED (sourced)

or UNCERTAIN.

CONFIRMED BY SOURCE

1. Military career — Escuela Militar, 4th promotion (cuarta época)
  • Graduated in the fourth promotion of the Escuela Militar "Capitán General Gerardo Barrios",

alongside José María Lemus and Alberto Escamilla.

  • Source: Anuario de Estudios Centroamericanos, Vol. 49 (2023), Juan Manuel Pérez, citing

Castro Morán 1984 p. 53: "De la cuarta: José María Lemus, Jorge Tenorio y Alberto Escamilla"

(Castro Morán, Relámpagos de libertad).

  • URL: https://proxy.paperity.org/download/http%3A%2F%2Fwww.scielo.sa.cr%2Fpdf%2Faec%2Fv49%2F2215-4175-aec-49-215.pdf
  • Note: first promotion graduated 20 Nov 1930, second 27 Dec 1931 (cf. Óscar Adán Bolaños,

"segunda promoción de la escuela militar en su cuarta época"). Fourth promotion therefore

≈ 1932–1933 — this date is an inference, not a sourced fact.

2. Captain, 1944 — junta press/propaganda officer
  • Mid-1944, as captain, commissioned (with captains José María Lemus, Adolfo Rubio Melhado,

Óscar Adán Bolaños and others) by the military junta to edit the pro-army newspapers

"La verdad desnuda" and "El Combatiente" (treasurer, administrator and distributor

in Bolaños's case).

  • Source: es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Óscar_Adán_Bolaños, citing Jorge Arias Gómez, "El golpe de

estado del 21 de octubre de 1944", Revista La Universidad Vol. 19 (1994).

  • URL: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%93scar_Ad%C3%A1n_Bola%C3%B1os
  • Quote: "A mediados de 1944, sería comisionado (junto con los capitanes José María Lemus,

Adolfo Rubio Melhado, Jorge Tenorio y otros) por la junta militar ... para que editaran

algunos periódicos que defendieran al ejército a la luz pública".

  • Also: on the night of 20 October 1944, with captains Ponce, Rubio Melhado, Juan Sánchez

Ponce, Quintanilla, Leiva, Carlos Bermúdez, Velásquez and Lt. Paniagua, he urged Gen.

Salvador Peña Trejo to call a junta meeting to depose provisional president

Andrés Ignacio Menéndez and install Osmín Aguirre y Salinas.

  • Source: same page, citing Castellanos, El Salvador 1930-1960 (2001).
  • Quote: "En la noche del 20 de octubre de 1944 (junto con los capitanes Ponce, Rubio

Melhado, Jorge Tenorio, Juan Sánchez Ponce, Quintanilla, Leiva, Carlos Bermúdez,

Velásquez, y teniente Paniagua) ... urgirían al general Salvador Peña Trejo una reunión

de la junta militar para deponer al presidente al general Andrés Ignacio Menéndez y

colocar al coronel Osmín Aguirre y Salinas."

  • This CONFIRMS the book's claim that in 1944 he was a mid-level officer with "ningún alto

puesto con el Gobierno" — he was a captain aligned with the military junta, not a

high government official.

3. Major, December 1948 — one of the "jóvenes oficiales" of the coup ("Revolución del 48" / golpe de los mayores)
  • As major, one of the young officers who planned and carried out the 14 December 1948

coup deposing Salvador Castaneda Castro.

  • Sources: es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consejo_de_Gobierno_Revolucionario; joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com

(Salvador Castaneda Castro entry); Anuario article above (lists him among the coup officers).

  • URLs: https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consejo_de_Gobierno_Revolucionario ;

http://joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com/2026/07/salvador-castaneda-castro-de-la.html

  • Quote (Anuario): "El grupo de oficiales golpistas, encabezado por ... y los comandantes

Oscar Bolaños, José María Lemus, Humberto Pineda Villalta, Adolfo Rubio Melhado,

Jorge Tenorio, Miguel Ángel Castillo y Jesús R. Rodríguez y el capitán

José Joaquín Chacón..."

4. Colonel — Director del Departamento del Censo (Ministerio de Economía), by ~1950–52
  • "Coronel Jorge Tenorio", director of the Census Department (Ministry of Economy),

worked closely with engineers Prieto and Parker on the 1:40,000 planimetric maps

(based on 1949 Army Map Service aerial photos) — the first accurate map of El Salvador.

  • Source: "Las coordinadas geográficas de las poblaciones de El Salvador", Universidad de

El Salvador repository, dated 1952-02-01.

  • URL: https://repositorio.ues.edu.sv/items/f0ffc151-fedc-4d03-a11d-c9bff5462938
  • Quote: "agradecimientos sinceros a la estrecha colaboración prestada por el señor

director del Departamento del Censo, Coronel Jorge Tenorio, y por los señores

ingenieros Prieto y Parker."

5. Director de la Policía Nacional, 1957
  • In August 1957 he was director of the Policía Nacional. The "ola difamatoria" press

scandal (El Independiente / Jorge Pinto Hijo, about elite youths detained at a party)

began with "una conversación informal entre el coronel Jorge Tenorio, director de la

Policía Nacional, y el periodista Jorge Pinto, donde le comentaba que habían detenido a

un grupo de jóvenes de la élite social sansalvadoreña en casa de una familia

terrateniente del país."

  • Source: La Prensa Gráfica, "Pánico moral: lo LGBTIQ+" by Marvin Aguilar (June 2025),

based on Amaral Arévalo's book Dialogando con el silencio.

  • URL: https://mediacenter.elgrafico.com/opinion/Panico-moral-lo-LGBTIQ-20250620-0073.html
6. Identity consistency / disambiguation
  • All sources describe ONE officer progressing through the ranks: subteniente (4th promotion,

≈1933) → captain (1944) → major (1948) → coronel (by ≈1950–52; Policía director 1957).

The rank progression and the names of his fellow officers (Lemus, Bolaños, Rubio Melhado —

all fellow members of the 1948 coup generation) are consistent with a single person.

This disambiguates him from the well-known Salvadoran Tenorio family figures (e.g.,

Dr. Jorge Eduardo Tenorio, Supreme Court magistrate — a civilian jurist, different person).

UNCERTAIN / NOT FOUND

  • Full name (maternal surname): every source gives only "Jorge Tenorio". No second surname found.
  • Birth date and place: not found. (Inference: born ≈1910–1912 based on military-school

graduation ≈1932–33; NOT confirmed — do not state as fact.)

  • Death date: not found beyond the book's terminus ante quem — already deceased when

Pugna a un dictador was published July 1987 ("los dos ya fallecidos"). No obituary found

in searchable sources.

  • Wife's name: not found (the book mentions only "su esposa" comforting María Teresa).
  • Children: not found.
  • Role in the April 2, 1944 uprising against Martínez: UNCERTAIN. The Wikipedia coup

article notes some 1948 plotters had joined the failed 2 April 1944 revolt, but does not

name Tenorio among them. María Teresa's account places him in San Salvador comforting her

(not among the rebels she condemns), consistent with him being a serving captain aligned

with the post-Martínez junta — but no source directly confirms or denies participation

in the April 1944 events.

Suggested next steps (for parent agent)

  • FamilySearch "El Salvador Registro Civil" (death records, in-person or account login) and

the Diario Oficial archive (appointment/decreto naming his police directorship) could yield

his full name and dates; the full text of Pugna a un dictador may name his wife.

Coronel Merino — the Tank Officer

Coronel Merino and the Tank at Ciudad Delgado

Date: 2026-10-07

Source: Cousin's voice note (family oral history), 2026-10-07

Status: Largely unverified — the account lives in a physical book and family memory, not on the public web

The cousin's account [Family record]

From the voice note (transcribed, with voice-to-text garbling noted):

  • The cousin owns "Relámpagos de Libertad" by Mariano Castro Morán — described as "un libro muy bueno" (a very good book). Castro Morán was "compañero de tío Antonio, de promoción de la escuela militar" (classmate of Uncle Antonio [José Antonio Gavidia Castro], same military school promotion).
  • In the book appears the account of Coronel Merino — "creo que algunos lo conocieron" (I think some [of you] knew him).
  • Merino was "de la marina" (of the navy).
  • Merino was the one who, together with the cousin's grandfather, boarded "el tanque famoso" (the famous tank) during the April 1944 events.
  • Per the book: the grandfather meets Merino around Ciudad Delgado, and they board the tank; the book tells "toda la aventura" (the whole adventure). "Ahí fue donde mi abuelo se involucró con el coronel Merino" (that's where my grandfather got involved with Colonel Merino).
  • "Casualmente el coronel Merino se terminó casando con una hermana de Beto Ayala" (Merino ended up marrying a sister of Beto Ayala).
  • Garbled fragment: "¿Verdad? Que fue el segunda esposa de Irma Materia."

Parsing the garbled fragment [Analysis — hypothesis]

"Irma Materia" is almost certainly voice-to-text garbling of "mi mamá Tere" (my Mama Tere — the family's name for María Teresa González).

Reconstructed: "...una hermana de Beto Ayala, ¿verdad? Que fue el segundo esposo de mi mamá Tere."

("...a sister of Beto Ayala, right? Who was the second husband of my Mama Tere.")

If this parse is correct:

  • "Beto Ayala" is the cousin's name for María Teresa's second husband.
  • "Beto" is the standard nickname for Roberto — the book's pseudonym name ("Roberto Ocampo").
  • "Ayala" is the children's surname (Gloria Gavidia Ayala, Herbert Ayala).
  • So "Beto Ayala" = the cousin's composite name for the second husband, blending the book's "Roberto" with the family "Ayala."
  • Family testimony (2026-10-07) identifies the real man as Coronel Luis Lovo Castelar.
  • Merino married the second husband's sister → Merino would be the brother-in-law of Luis Lovo Castelar.

⚠️ This parse is provisional. The voice note should be re-listened to confirm.

Who was Coronel Merino? [Unverified lead — no public trace]

  • No "Coronel Merino" in a 1944 Salvadoran military context found on the public web (multiple searches, Spanish).
  • Critical anachronism: The cousin calls him "de la marina" (navy), but El Salvador's navy (Fuerza Naval) was not created until October 12, 1952 [Documented — es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuerza_Naval_de_El_Salvador]. In April 1944 there was no Salvadoran navy.
  • Possible explanations:
  • The cousin misremembers the branch (he was army, not navy).
  • Merino later joined the navy after 1952 and the cousin retrojects.
  • "Marina" refers to something else (unlikely).
  • His full name is unknown. No first name, no second surname on the public web.
  • He does not appear in the "Pugna a un dictador" transcription (grep for "merino": zero hits).

The tank at Ciudad Delgado [Unverified lead]

  • Not found on the public web. The episode appears to exist only in "Relámpagos de Libertad" (physical book) and family oral history.
  • What the book "Pugna a un dictador" does say about tanks (for context) [Family record]:
  • The Department of Ulapa (fictionalized department name) had four tanks — "el más fuerte de todos" (the strongest of all).
  • Carlos took command at Cuartel Páramo of troops from Ulapa, with tanks and armored trucks.
  • The rebel tanks ran out of gasoline — a key factor in the defeat at the ambush on the road to San Andrés ("Fue una verdadera carnicería").
  • A group commanded by "el Coronel... hermano del General líder de la Revolución" (Tito Tomás Calvo) arrived at the US Embassy in a tank seeking asylum — denied; the consul surrendered them.
  • Ciudad Delgado (north of San Salvador) would be on a logical axis of rebel movement toward the capital on April 2. The episode is tactically plausible but undocumented outside the book.

"Relámpagos de Libertad" [Documented — the book exists]

  • "Relámpagos de Libertad, Tomo I" — Mariano Castro Morán, Editorial Lis, El Salvador, 2000, 282 pages.
  • "Relámpagos de Libertad II" — 1st ed. December 2020 (cited in press).
  • Castro Morán (1920–2005): 1944 conspirator, condemned to death, survived; later vice minister of defense, historian.
  • Per a UES academic article, in Tomo I he recounts being recruited in mid-March 1944 by Sub Tte. Roberto Morán López, "coordinador del movimiento rebelde para la zona occidental."
  • Not available online — no digital edition found. The cousin's physical copy is currently the only accessible source for the Merino/tank passage.
  • Also by Castro Morán: "Función Política del Ejército Salvadoreño en el Presente Siglo" (UCA Editores, 1984), "Reseña Histórica de las Administraciones Presidenciales y de la Policía Nacional de El Salvador desde 1867 hasta 1994" (2005).

The cousin's grandfather [Open question]

  • "Mi abuelo" (my grandfather) boarded the tank with Merino.
  • The cousin is in the Gavidia family group and refers to "tío Antonio" (José Antonio Gavidia Castro, executed 1944).
  • If the cousin descends from a Gavidia brother, the grandfather could be one of the surviving brothers (Juan José, Dagoberto, Ricardo, etc.) — but which one, and whether he was in El Salvador in April 1944, is unknown.
  • Alternatively "abuelo" could be on another family line. Needs the cousin to clarify.

The Ayala connection [Hypothesis]

If the parse is correct (Merino married the sister of "Beto Ayala" = María Teresa's second husband):

  • This creates a second independent Ayala thread — not just the children's surname, but a marriage alliance between the Merino and Ayala families.
  • It may help explain the "Ayala" children's surname even though the husband was (per family testimony) Luis Lovo Castelar.
  • Or it may indicate the second husband's family really was Ayala-connected in ways the records haven't shown.

What would settle this

  • Re-listen to the voice note — confirm the "Beto Ayala / mi mamá Tere" parse and identify which grandfather.
  • The cousin's copy of "Relámpagos de Libertad" — the Merino/tank passage (page numbers, full names, dates). Ask the cousin to photograph the relevant pages.
  • Merino's full name — from the book passage; then Diario Oficial / AGN for his service record.
  • Marriage record — Merino + [Ayala sister]: names both families completely.
  • UES/Academia de Historia Militar — may hold "Relámpagos de Libertad" for consultation on the trip.

Evidence-strength summary

Cousin's tank/Merino account [Family record] · "Relámpagos" exists, 2000, 282 pp. [Documented] · Merino's identity/branch [Unverified lead] · Navy anachronism (no navy until 1952) [Documented] · Tank episode [Unverified lead — book-only] · "Beto Ayala" = second husband [Hypothesis from garbled audio] · Merino–Ayala marriage [Unverified lead] · Grandfather's identity [Open question]

The CW-14 Airplane Photo

The CW-14 airplane photo — who are the other men?

Date: 2026-10-07

Photo location: ~/workspace/ts-spaces/gavidia-family-history/assets/carlos-gavidia-cw14.jpg

(Site caption: "At Ilopango — With a Curtiss-Wright CW-14 'Osprey'." No identifications.)

Dating [Documented]

  • El Salvador acquired three CW-14 Ospreys (sns 2033, 2034, 2035) on August 16, 1933; they served until 1942.
  • Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro served at Ilopango 1936–1939 (selected for the Aviation School in 1936, promoted teniente September 29, 1936) and returned 1942–1943.
  • Best window: 1936–1939, his first aviation tour, when the Ospreys were still front-line trainers.

The eight men (left to right)

  • Officer, visor cap — tunic with belt, jodhpurs, boots; smiling, round face. [Unidentified]
  • Officer, cap — stand-collar tunic, hands clasped; tall, lean face. [Unidentified]
  • Short pilot in leather flight jacket — flight cap with goggles pushed up on forehead, jodhpurs, tall boots; round youthful face. [Probable] Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro — the face matches both solo portraits on the site (the flight-suit photo and the colorized uniform portrait: round face, full cheeks, wavy dark hair). He is the only man in full flying gear at the center of the group.
  • Light shirt and tie, arms crossed, no cap — civilian dress or officer in shirtsleeves; older-looking, light hair. [Unidentified]
  • Officer, cap — dark tunic with belt; heavy-set, serious face. [Unidentified]
  • White tropical suit, dark tie, white pith helmet — clearly a civilian dignitary, older. Possibly a government official visiting Ilopango. [Unidentified]
  • Officer, cap — dark tunic; young, round face. [Unidentified]
  • Far right, partially cut off — appears to wear flight gear like #3 (leather jacket, flight cap). [Unidentified]

The aircraft: single-engine biplane with radial-engine cowling and propeller — consistent with the CW-14 Osprey.

Candidate roster for the other seven [Documented pool, individual identities Unverified]

Pilots graduated 1930–1940 (per the Fuerza Aérea Salvadoreña history): Francisco A. Ponce, Miguel Hernández, Héctor Castañeda, Raúl Paniagua Araujo, Mario Ernesto Villacorta, Horacio Melara, Manuel Martínez, Manuel Quijano Hernández, Víctor Alfredo Lara.

Senior pilots who could be among the officers:

  • Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa — graduated pilot 1925, later the Air Force's second-in-command and a pioneer pilot; flew in this exact era and place. [Unverified lead]
  • Juan Ramón Munés — appointed Chief of the Salvadoran Aviation Corps December 8, 1931. [Unverified lead]

What would resolve this

  • Family identification — Ricardo Gavidia or the cousins may recognize faces or hold a captioned copy.
  • Museo Nacional de Aviación (Ilopango) — trip task; the museum may hold a captioned print of this same official photo.
  • Cornejo Escobar, Historia de la Fuerza Aérea Salvadoreña (2002) — trip buy; may name Ilopango's 1936–39 pilot roster with photos.
  • AGN personnel files — service photos of Sosa, Munés, and the 1930–40 graduates for visual comparison.

Bottom line

Carlos is almost certainly the young pilot in the leather flight jacket (third from left). The other seven men are unidentified — the candidate pool is the small fraternity of 1930s Ilopango pilots, but no labeled reference photo exists online to match faces to names. This one needs the family or the museum, not the web.

Dagoberto's Resignation

Dagoberto Gavidia Castro — the 1944 resignation

Research: October 7, 2026. No one contacted; research only.

The wire story [Newspaper]

  • San Bernardino Sun, Vol. 50, May 17, 1944, carried a United Press wire datelined "SAN FRANCISCO, May 16":

"...Dagoberto Gavidia said today he had withdrawn his resignation as consul general of El Salvador..."

  • Indexed in Geneanet's newspaper library (user colgnln10116386). The full article text is not available in the index — only this snippet. The complete page would be on newspapers.com or GenealogyBank (both paywalled) or in the San Bernardino Public Library's California Room, which holds the San Bernardino Sun 1894–1998.

The timeline reframes the story [Documented]

The key dates, assembled from the wire story and the historical record:

  • April 1944: Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro and José Antonio Gavidia Castro executed after the failed April 2 uprising.
  • Sometime April–early May 1944: Dagoberto submits his resignation as Consul General in San Francisco — presumably in protest over his brothers' executions. (The resignation itself was never reported in the indexed press; only the withdrawal was.)
  • May 9, 1944: Hernández Martínez resigns and flees; Andrés Ignacio Menéndez becomes provisional president.
  • May 16, 1944: Dagoberto announces he has withdrawn his resignation.

This changes the interpretation. He did not withdraw under pressure from the regime — the regime had already fallen a week earlier. The most plausible reading: he resigned in protest against Martínez, and once the dictator was gone, the protest was moot — so he returned to duty under the new government. A principled resignation, then a return, not a retraction under duress.

The resignation letter itself — NOT found [Unverified lead]

No digitized copy of the letter (or the withdrawal) surfaced. Where it would be:

  • Archivo General de la Nación, San Salvador — Ministerio de Relaciones Exteriores correspondence, 1944. Highest probability. (Trip task.)
  • US National Archives — State Department decimal files for El Salvador, 1944; consular correspondence from San Francisco. The Foreign Relations of the United States volume for 1944 (Vol. VII, The American Republics) covers El Salvador — the Office of the Historian site has it, but no Dagoberto mention appeared in the indexed documents.
  • Diario Oficial de El Salvador, May 1944 — his reappointment or confirmation under the Menéndez government would be published here. Also his original appointment (1943?) would name the date he took the post.
  • Family papers — via Francisco José Gavidia Ayala (Juan José's grandson) or the Ventura line (see below).

The son: Carlos Dagoberto Gavidia Ventura [Documented]

  • A lawyer active in Salvadoran court records in the 2000s.
  • Testified in a 2004 case before the Tribunal Tercero de Sentencia, San Salvador (vehicle theft), as "apoderado de una institución" — legal representative of an institution identified as CAM. He declared details of a stolen Toyota pickup (plates 528-780) taken September 23, 2004.
  • Source: court document on yumpu.com (Tribunal Tercero de Sentencia transcript).
  • No biographical profile, no mention of his father found. The "Ventura" maternal surname is a new lead for tracing this branch.

Context: Salvadoran diplomats in 1944 [Scholarship]

  • The most famous Salvadoran diplomat of the era, José Arturo Castellanos (Consul General in Geneva), was using his post to save thousands of Jews from the Holocaust in 1944 — a reminder that Salvadoran consuls of this period operated with real autonomy.
  • No record was found of other Salvadoran diplomats resigning over the April 1944 executions specifically. Dagoberto's protest appears to have been personal — the brother of the executed — rather than part of a wider diplomatic break.
  • The US had withdrawn support from Martínez by early May 1944 (partly over the accidental killing of Salvadoran-American student José Wright on May 7), and the US Embassy in San Salvador joined the pressure for his resignation.

Dagoberto after 1944 — no trace

  • No diplomatic lists, appointments, or obituaries found for Dagoberto Gavidia after May 1944. He may have continued as consul under subsequent governments, returned to El Salvador, or left public life. The Diario Oficial and AGN are the places to check.

For the October trip checklist

  • [ ] AGN: Ministerio de Relaciones Exteriores, 1944 correspondence — the resignation letter and withdrawal.
  • [ ] Diario Oficial, April–June 1944: Dagoberto's appointment, resignation acceptance (if any), reappointment under Menéndez.
  • [ ] San Bernardino Public Library (California Room) or newspapers.com: full May 17, 1944 Sun article — it may name who he resigned to and quote his reasons.
  • [ ] US National Archives (if accessible remotely): State Dept. files on the SF consulate, 1944.

Bottom line

The letter itself is still out there somewhere — most likely in the AGN's foreign ministry files. But the timeline solves the "why did he withdraw" mystery: Martínez fell on May 9, and Dagoberto un-resigned on May 16. He protested the executions by quitting, then came back once the dictator was gone.

Darío Flores — Clearing a Name

Darío Flores and the Flores Threads — Research Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: Darío Flores documented as a real officer; no documented link to "Flores Magaña." The Sánchez Dueñas identification strengthened significantly.

---

1. Coronel Darío Flores — the real officer [Documented]

On the Council of War. María Teresa's epilogue prints the Second Council of War (April 23, 1944) with real names. Darío Flores appears as a suplente (substitute member), alongside General Francisco Ponce and Coronel José Garay. The proprietary members were General Luis Andreu, Coronel Santiago Ayala, and Coronel Joaquín Fernán. So Darío Flores sat — at least as an alternate — on the tribunal that condemned Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche, and Teniente Alfonso Marín.

Police director, 1945. By June 1945 Darío Flores was Director of the Policía Nacional. On June 10/12, 1945, he stopped a military coup against the newly elected president Salvador Castaneda Castro. In that attempt Capitán Rafael Paniagua Araujo died piloting the plane that strafed the presidential house. The coup was promoted by young majors who had not participated in the April 2, 1944 uprising — among them Óscar Adán Bolaños (detained, discharged) and Óscar Osorio (exiled to Mexico; returned 1948; president 1950–56).

The irony. Darío Flores helped judge the April 1944 rebels, then a year later crushed a coup led by officers who had sat out 1944 — including the man who would become the reformist president of the book's Third Part.

What is NOT documented: his full name beyond "Darío Flores" (no "Magaña" second surname found anywhere), his birth/death dates, his career before 1944, his fate after 1945, or any family relationship to the book's "Capitán Flores."

---

2. Other Flores officers in the 1940s military

None found. No documented Capitán Flores — and no "Flores Magaña" — appears in any April 1944 execution list, the front-matter newspaper lists, Wikipedia's account, the UES journal's council sentence, or any web-indexed military record. The book's "Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña" exists only in the novel's narrative and its quoted verdict.

"Flores Magaña" as a surname is real. Modern Salvadoran records show genuine bearers (e.g., Hugo Besael Flores Magaña, former mayor of Santa Rosa Guachipilín; Teresa María Flores Magaña; Abel Antonio Flores Magaña). It is a plausible, authentic-sounding compound surname — exactly what a careful pseudonymist would choose.

---

3. Does Darío Flores connect to "Flores Magaña"?

No documented connection. Different rank (colonel vs. captain), different role (judge vs. condemned), no shared second surname, no family link found. The shared surname is most likely coincidence — "Flores" is a common Salvadoran surname — or, at most, a name the author had "in the air" from the military world she knew. Coincidence is the leading reading; a deliberate link is unproven.

---

4. Re-examining the identification: "Flores Magaña" = Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas

The case is now strong. Assembled:

  • Same slot. In the book's quoted April 10 verdict, "Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña" occupies the same list position as Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas in every documented April 10 cemetery roster — and Sánchez Dueñas is named first in the author's own front-matter list.
  • Same rank, same date, same place. Both captains, both executed April 10, 1944 at the Cementerio General.
  • "Manuel" is real. Marta's "¿Qué te pasa Manuel?" (primera-parte-2.md:1076) is direct quoted speech — and per the book's demonstrated method (real names surface in quotes: "Teniente Marín," "Capitán Gavidia Castro"), Manuel is his true first name. This matches Manuel Sánchez Dueñas. (Note: this corrects the flores-magana-puzzle.md claim that the quote doesn't exist — it does, verified twice.)
  • Independent literary corroboration. Horacio Castellanos Moya's novel Tirana memoria (2008) — [Novel], not documentation — portrays Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas as the true organizer of the 1944 military plot: a promotion leader, discharged for insubordination ~1942, exiled under an arrest warrant, returned secretly around Christmas 1943 to build the conspiracy, and the man who convinced the Gavidia brothers to join. This mirrors the book's "Flores": "uno de los jefes del complot," Carlos's classmate feeding him intelligence, central to the conspiracy.
  • One discrepancy to note. Castellanos Moya's Sánchez Dueñas was discharged and operating clandestinely; the book's Flores is on active duty under surveillance. If the identification holds, the author altered service details as part of the disguise — consistent with pseudonym practice.

Why "Flores" as the cover? Unknown. Best guesses, in order: (a) a common surname chosen to sound real — and "Flores Magaña" does sound real; (b) the author borrowed a surname she knew from the military milieu (Darío Flores sat on the council); (c) a private association lost to us. No evidence selects among these. The decisive test remains the ~1930s marriage record: a Manuel Sánchez Dueñas–Marta Pérez (or Pérez-surname) marriage corroborates; its absence under "Flores Magaña" confirms the pseudonym.

---

5. Corrections to prior reports

  • flores-magana.md ("Most likely full legal name: Manuel Roberto Flores Magaña") — revise. The evidence now indicates "Roberto Flores Magaña" is a full pseudonym, not a compound of real + false names. The real man is most likely Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, with "Manuel" the one true element smuggled through in quoted speech.
  • flores-magana-puzzle.md ("The book NEVER calls him 'Manuel'") — wrong. The quote exists at primera-parte-2.md:1076. The puzzle's leading hypothesis (pseudonym) still stands, but its textual claim must be corrected.

---

Bottom line

Darío Flores was real — a council alternate in April 1944 and the police chief who stopped the June 1945 coup — but he is almost certainly not the book's "Capitán Flores." That figure maps cleanly onto the documented Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas: same rank, same list slot, same execution, same first name leaking through in quoted speech, and an independent novel casting Sánchez Dueñas as the plot's true organizer who recruited the Gavidia brothers. "Flores Magaña" reads as a well-chosen mask — a real-sounding Salvadoran surname — over a real martyr whose widow and children were alive in 1987.

Open questions / trip tasks

  • Marriage record (~early 1930s, San Salvador): Manuel Sánchez Dueñas + bride — settles the identification and gives Marta's real identity.
  • AGN personnel file: Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas — photo, promotion (was he Carlos's classmate?), discharge record (~1942?).
  • Trial dossier: his deposition — the Cáceres Prendes article analyzes selected depositions; the full file names all 44.
  • Darío Flores: Diario Oficial 1944–46 for his appointments; full name and later fate.
  • Cementerio General burial records, April 10, 1944: who was actually buried — and under what name.
