Flores Magaña — the Puzzle

The Flores Magaña Puzzle — Research Report

The puzzle

The book's narrative says Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña was captured at El Zunzal, tortured, tried, and executed at 8 AM on April 10, 1944 alongside the Gutiérrez/Calvo group. But he is ABSENT from the author's own printed newspaper execution lists in the front matter — and from every documented record found.

What the book actually says [Family record]

Name usage: "Flores Magaña" appears exactly ONCE in the entire transcription — in the quoted verdict (segunda-parte-1.md:198). Everywhere else he is only "Capitán Flores" (21 mentions across primera-parte-2 and segunda-parte-1). The book NEVER calls him "Manuel" — the earlier claim that Marta says "¿Qué te pasa Manuel?" does not exist in the transcription and should be disregarded.

The verdict as quoted (p. 97): "General Tomás Gutiérrez, Coronel Lorenzo Gutiérrez, Teniente Marcelino Gutiérrez, Mayor Pineda, Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña, y siete Tenientes y Subtenientes serán pasados por las armas a las ocho de la mañana de este mismo día..."

The execution (p. 98): "A las 8 de la mañana del 10 de Abril de 1944, fueron ejecutados en el Cuartel de la Policía... los tres Jefes de la Revolución... a la misma hora serían pasados por las armas en el Cementerio General y en un paredón los demás Oficiales condenados a muerte, entre ellos Paco Romo..."

His story arc: Carlos's classmate from year one of military school; served in the Cuartel de Infantería; described as "un militar sin tacha" (a spotless officer) who fed Carlos inside information on the regime's tortures and injustices; married to Marta Pérez with a child and another on the way; under close surveillance; went as parliamentary envoy to El Zunzal where he, Paco Romo, and Teniente López were captured (López released to report); tortured "horriblemente" without betraying anyone; at trial refused defenders and said "no soy asesino, no disparé ni un tiro"; executed April 10.

Book-internal number problem: The narrative's verdict names 12 (5 named + 7 lieutenants), then says 10 officers at the cemetery + 3 at the police = 13. The author's own factual list says 10 total on April 10. The book's numbers do not add up internally.

What the documented records say

Author's front-matter real lists [Documented] (stated as "tomados del periódico"):

  • April 10, Policía Nacional: General Alfonso Marroquín, Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo, Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa.
  • April 10, Cementerio: Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro, Teniente Marcelino Calvo, Teniente Miguel Angel Linares, Teniente Ricardo Mancía González, Teniente Edgardo Chacón, Teniente Oscar Armando Cristales.
  • April 11: 15 more names (military and civilian). No Flores Magaña.
  • Flores Magaña appears in NEITHER list.

Wikipedia [Scholarship]: "Treinta personas, entre ellas los cabecillas Alfonso Marroquín y Tomás Calvo, fueron ejecutadas." 30 executed total. No Flores Magaña named.

Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier article [Scholarship]: 44 death sentences, 14 carried out (13 military + 1 civilian). Full dossier not accessible online; cannot check for Flores Magaña.

Web search: Zero trace of "Flores Magaña" in any 1944 context. Zero trace of "Roberto Flores" as a 1944 executed captain. No photo, no military biography, no memorial.

Analysis: possible resolutions

1. Incomplete newspaper lists [Plausible]. Wikipedia says 30 executed; the front-matter lists name ~25. About 5 are unnamed. Flores Magaña could be among the unnamed — the newspapers may simply not have had complete lists, and the author (writing decades later from memory plus clippings) couldn't fill the gap.

2. "Flores Magaña" is a pseudonym [Plausible — leading hypothesis]. The author's stated method is to change protagonists' names to protect the living. Marta Pérez was alive in 1987 (the book's present). Naming her real executed husband would identify her and her children. The entire Marta/Flores storyline may use false names. If so, the real husband could be:

  • Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas — the only other captain on the April 10 cemetery list, and the only unmapped captain in the narrative. But there is no textual link between the two names, and "Sánchez Dueñas" never appears in the narrative at all (only in front matter), which is consistent with — but does not prove — a name swap.
  • Someone executed on a different date.
  • Someone whose death was never publicly recorded.

Note the complication: if "Flores Magaña" is false, "Marta Pérez," "Don Pedro Pérez," and the "Almacén de los Pérez" may also be false, and the real family is unidentified.

3. The book conflated or dramatized [Possible]. The internal number discrepancies (13 vs 10) show the narrative is not precise. The author may have merged real people, moved dates, or given Marta's husband a more heroic end than the records show.

4. He survived [Unlikely but not disproven]. Nothing supports this; the book is specific and Marta's grief is central. But absence from all lists leaves it technically open.

What would settle it (trip tasks)

  • The trial dossier at the AGN — the definitive document. 44 condemned by real name. If Flores Magaña (or a matching captain) is there, puzzle solved.
  • Diario Oficial, April–May 1944 — complete execution notices; may name people the newspapers missed.
  • Cementerio General burial records, April 1944 — who was actually buried after the April 10 executions.
  • Flores Magaña + Marta Pérez marriage record (~early 1930s) — if the names are real, this exists; if not found, supports the pseudonym hypothesis.
  • AGN military personnel files — a Capitán Flores (any second surname) serving 1944.

Bottom line

Unresolvable from available sources. The book is the sole source for Flores Magaña's existence, and it contradicts its own factual lists by omitting him. He appears in no documented record anywhere. The leading hypothesis is that "Flores Magaña" (and possibly "Marta Pérez") are pseudonyms protecting a living family in 1987 — which would make the real husband one of the documented dead under another name, or someone whose story was never publicly recorded. The trial dossier at the AGN is the one document that can settle it.

Flores Magaña — the Investigation

Capitán (Manuel) Roberto Flores Magaña — Research Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: Identity and role established from the novel; no independent documentation found online.

---

Bottom line

[Family record] Capitán Flores Magaña was Carlos Gavidia Castro's classmate from the first year of military school, the husband of Marta Pérez (the Gavidia family's "chosen bride"), one of the April 2, 1944 plot's leaders, and was executed the morning of April 10, 1944 at the Cementerio General. The book is the only source for him — no web record, photo, or descendant trace exists.

---

The name question — resolved

  • [Family record] The book's official death-sentence text names him "Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña."
  • [Family record] Marta calls him "Manuel" ("¿Qué te pasa Manuel? dijo Marta"). The research notes on the transcription confirm: "Capitán Flores: nombre de pila Manuel" — Manuel is his first (given) name.
  • Most likely full legal name: Manuel Roberto Flores Magaña — "Roberto" used in official/military documents, "Manuel" used intimately.
  • The marriage record (~early 1930s, San Salvador) would settle this definitively.

---

Military career [Family record]

  • Classmate of Carlos Gavidia Castro from the first year of military school.
  • Rank: Capitán by 1944.
  • Described as "un militar sin tacha" (a spotless officer).
  • Fed Carlos inside information about the regime's abuses.
  • When another officer was suspected of being a government spy living beyond his means, Flores was the one who raised the alarm — and Flores himself explicitly rejected being a government plant.

Role in the April 1944 plot [Family record]

  • "Uno de los jefes del complot" — one of the plot's leaders; under strict government surveillance before April 2.
  • Plot structure: groups of three leaders each. Group 3: Jefe Coronel Cardoza, Comandante Coronel Soriano, Auxiliar Capitán Flores. Paco (Teniente Romo) was in Group 1 alongside Capitán Flores.
  • Gave a speech to the conspirators: the revolution had to be "limpia y pura" (clean and pure), avoiding bloodshed, uniting "desde el más humilde campesino hasta el más orgulloso capitalista."
  • Went as parliamentary envoy to El Zunzal with Paco and Teniente López. Flores and Paco were captured and imprisoned; only López was released to carry warning back.
  • Arrested, subjected to "las torturas más crueles" (the cruelest tortures) alongside Paco — without betraying anyone.
  • At the trial he stood with dignity; sentenced to death.

Execution [Family record]

  • Executed the morning of April 10, 1944 at the Cementerio General, at 8:00 AM.
  • Executed alongside: General Tomás Gutiérrez, Coronel Lorenzo Gutiérrez, Teniente Marcelino Gutiérrez, Mayor Pineda, and seven lieutenants and sub-lieutenants.
  • The sentence text: "por traición a su patria y a su gobierno; por el asesinato de Oficiales, tropa y civiles, muertos en combate defendiendo a su gobierno."

Marriage and children [Family record]

  • Married Marta Pérez (daughter of Don Pedro Pérez of "el Almacén de los Pérez") — the woman the Gavidia family had wanted Carlos to marry.
  • They met at the Círculo's graduation party; married "por esa época" (around the early 1930s).
  • Had children; by April 1944 there was "un niño recién nacido" (a newborn).
  • Marta, home praying and knowing nothing of his fate, "enloqueció de dolor y angustia" when told. She vanishes from the narrative after this.

---

What could NOT be documented

  • [Unverified lead] No independent web record of Roberto/Manuel Flores Magaña exists — no Diario Oficial hits, no obituary, no military biography.
  • No photo found.
  • No marriage record to Marta Pérez located online.
  • No birth records of the Flores-Pérez children located; the 1944 newborn would be ~82 — possible living descendants, untraced.
  • The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier article (Realidad No. 154, 2019) confirms 44 death sentences, 14 carried out, but the PDF could not be downloaded to check for his individual testimony.

---

Dots connected

  • Flores Magaña is the hinge between the romance and the tragedy: Marta, the "acceptable" bride Carlos rejected, became the widow of Carlos's executed comrade and classmate.
  • He and Carlos entered military school together (year one), and died twelve days apart in April 1944 — Flores on April 10, Carlos on April 22/24.
  • His speech about a "clean and pure" revolution mirrors the idealism that drove the uprising.
  • The Gutiérrez officers executed alongside him (a general, a colonel, a lieutenant — same surname) suggest a family that rebelled together, like the Gavidias.

---

Open questions / trip tasks

  • Marriage record — Manuel Roberto Flores Magaña + Marta Pérez (~early 1930s, San Salvador civil registry). Settles the full legal name AND gives Marta's full identity.
  • Birth records of the Flores-Pérez children — the 1944 newborn would be ~82; possible living descendants in El Salvador.
  • Military personnel file at the Archivo General de la Nación — career, unit, photo possibly.
  • Trial dossier — his individual testimony (the Cáceres Prendes article analyzes selected depositions).
  • Diario Oficial officer appointments 1930–1944 — promotions, postings.
  • Cementerio General — burial record, April 10, 1944.

Francisco José Gavidia Ayala

Francisco José Gavidia Ayala — Deep Dig

Research: October 6-7, 2026. Research only — no contact made.

Bottom line

Francisco José Gavidia Ayala is the living grandson and heir of Juan José Gavidia Castro (1912–2016), the captain's eldest brother. He exists in exactly two public records — the 2019 Diario Co Latino succession edicts — and has zero social media, professional, or news footprint. He is a private person. The best path to him runs through the notary who handled the succession: René Alirio Ayala, a prominent San Salvador lawyer (once Bukele's counsel, 2025 Supreme Court candidate).

The family line (reconstructed)

Juan José Gavidia Castro (1912–2016) → his son (name unknown, d. July 30, 2017) → Francisco José Gavidia Ayala (grandson, living).

  • Francisco's paternal surname (Gavidia) comes from his father, Juan José's son.
  • His maternal surname (Ayala) comes from his mother, identity unknown.

[Documented] The two edicts

Edict 1 — August 24, 2019 (Diario Co Latino, p. 11)
  • Notary René Alirio Ayala, resolution August 20, 2019.
  • Juan José Gavidia Castro died September 19, 2016, age 104, at Colonia Miramonte, Calle Cerro Verde #34, San Salvador.
  • DUI: 01870574-5. Born in Santa Ana, resident of San Salvador.
  • Francisco José Gavidia Ayala accepted the intestate inheritance as "nieto sobreviviente" (surviving grandson). Granted interim administration.
  • Source: https://www.yumpu.com/es/document/view/62792881/edicion-24-de-agosto-2019/11
Edict 2 — October 4, 2019 (Diario Co Latino, p. 17)
  • Notary René Alirio Ayala, resolution October 2, 2019.
  • Francisco José Gavidia Ayala declared definitive heir (heredero definitivo).
  • References a death on July 30, 2017 at the Hospital General del Seguro Social, San Salvador.
  • Francisco inherits "en su concepto nieto de los derechos hereditarios del causante" — as grandson, of the hereditary rights of the deceased.
  • Source: https://www.yumpu.com/es/document/view/62854460/edicion-04-de-octubre-2019/17
Reading the two deaths

The edicts describe two different deaths: Juan José on Sept 19, 2016 (age 104, at home in Miramonte) and someone on July 30, 2017 (at the Seguro Social hospital). The most coherent interpretation: Juan José's son — Francisco's father, the direct heir — died July 30, 2017, and Francisco stepped into his deceased father's place in the succession. The father's name appears in neither edict.

[Documented] The notary: René Alirio Ayala

  • Office: Tercera Calle Poniente y 79 Avenida Norte, Condominio Las Alquerías #201, Colonia Escalón, San Salvador.
  • Prominent litigator. Was one of Nayib Bukele's lawyers (Apoderados Generales Judiciales) in the López Davidson criminal case (2019).
  • Proposed by the Consejo Nacional de la Judicatura as a Supreme Court substitute magistrate candidate (July 2025).
  • He shares Francisco's maternal surname (Ayala). Whether he is related to Francisco's mother is unproven — but as the family's chosen notary for a 2019 succession, a family connection is plausible.
  • He is the single best contact path to Francisco José.

[Dead ends — honest]

  • No social media: no Facebook, Instagram, or Threads trace of Francisco José Gavidia Ayala (social search returned nothing; Facebook CLI can only search the user's own friends).
  • No professional profile: no LinkedIn, company, or news mention.
  • No obituary for Juan José Gavidia Castro found (a 104-year-old's death in 2016 left no press trace located).
  • Father's name unknown; mother's identity unknown.
  • The July 30, 2017 death was not found in any online obituary or news source.

[Unverified lead]

  • The Ayala–Ayala surname match between Francisco's mother and the notary suggests the notary may be a maternal relative. Do not assert without confirmation.

Next steps (El Salvador trip)

  • Notary's office (Colonia Escalón) — René Alirio Ayala handled the succession and can reach Francisco. This is the direct door.
  • San Salvador civil registry — Francisco's birth record (names both parents); his parents' marriage record; his father's death record (July 30, 2017).
  • Juan José's death certificate (Sept 19, 2016) — may name surviving children.
  • Ask the notary whether he is related to the Ayala side of the family.

Evidence labels used

  • [Documented]: the two Diario Co Latino edicts (via yumpu), the notary's public legal career (jurisprudencia.gob.sv, diariofuentes.com).
  • [Unverified lead]: the Ayala–Ayala family connection hypothesis; the two-succession interpretation of the edicts.

General Valdéz

General Valdéz — Research Report

Bottom line

The book's "General Valdéz" is almost certainly Joaquín Valdez (1906–1957) — the only Salvadoran general-officer of that surname in the era. He was Carlos's former top commander (defense minister 1931–35), and in April 1944 he was the man María knelt before begging for Carlos's life. He refused to intervene but quietly arranged her prison visit. A regime loyalist by position, personally sympathetic but unwilling to risk himself — the book's own verdict: "todos temblaban de puro miedo" (they all trembled with pure fear).

What the book says [Family record]

Three mentions in Pugna a un dictador (pp. 103–104, 163):

  • The plea (pp. 103–104). María goes to the home of "General Valdéz, ex-jefe de Carlos" — Carlos's former commanding officer. She trusts him because the Valdézes "always showed affection to her and esteem for Carlos." His wife is Doña Catalina ("Catita"), who comforts María. High officials are visiting when María arrives.
  • The General's answer: "el Capitán Romo ya no tiene salvación" — the President listens to no recommendations about the rebels; many colleagues have pleaded in vain; only God and the President own the lives of the condemned. He cites "the law that governs us."
  • What he does do: promises to try to get María a visitation permit, calls high officials, and within hours the permit comes through — 2 PM at the Cuartel de la Policía (via Roberto Ocampo, who confirms "el General Valdéz le había ayudado").
  • The reckoning (p. 163). Years later María recalls: "regué con mis lágrimas los pies del General Valdéz… Todo fue inútil, todos temblaban de puro miedo."

Role: regime officer — loyalist by position, not a rebel, not a persecutor. He embodied the regime's respectable face: courteous, sympathetic, immobile.

The identification: Joaquín Valdez [Documented]

  • Joaquín Valdez (San Salvador, 22 Nov 1906 – San Salvador, 30 Aug 1957), Salvadoran military officer and official.
  • Guardia Nacional officer by 1931; delegate of the Guardia Nacional in the Directorio cívico (Dec 2–4, 1931); co-president of the Directorio alongside Osmín Aguirre y Salinas.
  • Minister of National Defense, 1931–1935, under Hernández Martínez — the top military post in the country. As minister he was the ultimate "jefe" of every young officer, including Carlos (who entered aviation in 1936, just after Valdez left the ministry — hence "ex-jefe").
  • During the 1932 peasant uprising he represented the president in a failed negotiation attempt with Communist leaders (Jan 8, 1932); the repression itself was assigned to Interior Minister Tomás Calderón.
  • Post-1944 career: ambassador to Panama (1948), then Ecuador, Venezuela, and Colombia (1952) — he survived the regime change comfortably, consistent with a cautious man who never stuck his neck out.
  • Sources: es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joaquín_Valdez; Grieb (1971); White (1973); Ministerio de la Defensa Nacional (2021) ex-ministers list.

Fit assessment

For the identification:

  • Only Salvadoran "General Valdez/Valdéz" of the era — no competing candidate found.
  • Senior enough to receive "other high officials" at home; the social standing matches a former defense minister.
  • "Ex-jefe de Carlos" fits: as defense minister he commanded the entire armed forces when Carlos was a junior officer.
  • Alive and in El Salvador in April 1944 (diplomatic postings began 1948).
  • Age 37 in 1944 — young for a general, but he was already defense minister at 25.

Caveats [Unverified lead]:

  • Wikipedia says he reached the rank of coronel; the book calls him General. He may have been promoted between 1935 and 1944 — the Diario Oficial would confirm (trip task).
  • Wife "Catalina/Catita" is unconfirmed in public sources.
  • Spelling: the book writes "Valdéz"; Wikipedia writes "Valdez."
  • No documented direct connection to aviation (he was Guardia Nacional) — but as minister this hardly matters.

The contrast the book draws

Valdéz vs. Capitán Erazo: a general who was Carlos's former chief would not even speak to the president for him; a mere Guardia Nacional captain refused to command the firing squad and surrendered his sword. The book uses Valdéz to indict the entire officer corps' cowardice — "todos temblaban de puro miedo."

Connection to the Gavidia family

  • Professional only: former superior officer of Carlos, family friend ("estimación a Carlos," knowledge of the parents as "honorable persons loved by the best society").
  • No blood or marriage tie found. No evidence the families stayed in contact after 1944.

Trip / archive tasks

  • Diario Oficial 1935–1944 — confirm Joaquín Valdez's promotion to general (brigadier) and any 1944 posting.
  • AGN personnel file — full service record; may name wife Catalina and confirm the "Catita" of the book.
  • 1944 social pages (Diario del Salvador, April 1944) — the Valdéz household; corroborate the visit described on pp. 103–104.
  • Marriage record — Joaquín Valdez + Catalina (full name, date) in San Salvador civil registry.

Dr. González Aguilar's Writings

Dr. Salvador González Aguilar — writings and identity hunt

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: Web sources exhausted; identity unrecoverable from public web. Key documents identified for trip/archive work.

What the book says [Family record]

From Pugna a un dictador, NOTA DE LA AUTORA (front matter):

> "Para terminar, quiero dedicar también, mi Historia a mis estimados primos Doctor Salvador González Aguilar y al Coronel Jorge Tenorio, los dos ya fallecidos, a los que siempre les guardé especial cariño y respeto."

> "El Doctor Aguilar me aconsejó y me ayudó a encausar mi libro, puedo decir que él como buen escritor me dió valor, para que lo publicara."

Established facts:

  • Full name: Doctor Salvador González Aguilar
  • María Teresa González's cousin ("primo")
  • Dead by July 1987 (book's print date)
  • Described as "un buen escritor" (a good writer)
  • Advised on the manuscript, helped "channel" (encausar) the book, gave her courage to publish it
  • Title "Doctor" — profession unknown (physician? lawyer? academic?)

Negative findings (honest dead ends)

Searched systematically, all negative:

  • Absent from "Biografías de escritores salvadoreños" (Carlos Flores, full text via archive.org) — zero hits for "González Aguilar" or "Salvador González." Significant: a nationally recognized published author would likely appear here.
  • No web obituary (pre-1987 Salvadoran or otherwise).
  • No indexed publications under his name in any catalog or database reachable via web.
  • No Diario Oficial hits via web search (appointments, licenses, notices).
  • No professional directory listings (medical, legal, academic).
  • No social media, news, or genealogical footprint (Geneanet, FamilySearch-indexed web hits).

Same-name different person (do not conflate)

  • Salvador Guillermo González Aguilar — Mexican lawyer, Licenciatura en Derecho UNAM 1988, later a magistrate (dedication in a UNAM thesis: "Al Magistrado Salvador Guillermo González Aguilar (qepd)"). Ruled out: graduated 1988, but María Teresa's cousin died before July 1987; middle name "Guillermo" also differs. Possible distant relative given the rare compound surname, but unproven and likely coincidental.

Family-position analysis [Analysis]

  • Shared paternal surname "González" with María Teresa + described as "primo" → his father was most likely a brother of Santiago/Rafael González (María Teresa's father).
  • Maternal surname "Aguilar" → his mother was an Aguilar (first name unknown).
  • Generation: María Teresa's cousin → born roughly 1900–1925.
  • Died: sometime before July 1987 (could be 1980s, 1970s, or earlier — no data).

What "un buen escritor" likely means [Analysis]

Given his absence from the writers' biographical dictionary, "buen escritor" probably does NOT mean nationally recognized published author. More likely:

  • Editorial advisor — an educated, articulate man who helped her shape the manuscript (the verb encausar suggests structural/editorial help, not co-authorship).
  • Newspaper/magazine writer — essays or columns in the Salvadoran press, never collected in books (wouldn't appear in the biographical dictionary).
  • Private writer — wrote but never published, or published only privately.
  • Practical publishing helper — the book was 1000 copies from a small press (Imprenta La Cruz del Sur); he may have helped with the business of getting it printed.

The prior research file (gonzalez-jiron-lead.md) already flagged: his descendants, if any, may hold family papers and possibly correspondence with María Teresa about the book.

Best next steps (trip/archive — web is exhausted)

  • FamilySearch — Salvadoran civil birth records ~1900–1925 and death records pre-1987 for "Salvador González Aguilar" (San Salvador). This is the single highest-value step: gives birth date, parents' names (confirming the González-brother-of-Santiago hypothesis), and death date.
  • UES graduate lists — Facultad de Medicina and Facultad de Jurisprudencia archives (he was "Doctor"; UES was the only university for most of the 20th century).
  • Diario Oficial digital archive (imprentanacional.gob.sv) — manual browsing by date for professional appointments, degree registrations, or death notices. Requires knowing approximate dates first.
  • Ask living cousins directly — the prior research already identified this as a people lead: a doctor-writer who died before 1987 may have children holding family papers, photographs, and possibly his own writings.
  • San Salvador civil registry — death certificate pre-1987 (gives age, address, informant — often a child).
  • Biblioteca Nacional catalog (in person) — the prior research flagged this; a small-press or newspaper writer might appear in the card catalog even if absent from web indexes.
  • Cementerio General — González Aguilar family plot (may give dates and reveal wife/children).

Evidence-strength summary

Cousin of María Teresa, dead by July 1987 [Family record] · "Un buen escritor" who advised the manuscript [Family record] · Title "Doctor," profession unknown [Family record] · Absent from Salvadoran writers' biographical dictionary [Documented negative] · Zero web footprint of any kind [Documented negative] · Father likely a brother of Santiago González [Analysis] · Mexican magistrate same-name is a different person [Documented] · All vital records and next steps require archives or family [Analysis]

María Teresa's Family — the González-Jirón Lead

Lead: María Teresa González's family (the grandmother's side)

Date: 2026-10-07

Source of lead: Fresh research thread — this side of the family was never investigated before

Status: First-pass complete; key facts extracted from the book itself, web traces nearly nil

What the book itself says [Family record]

From the front matter of Pugna a un dictador (1st ed., July 1987, 1000 copies, Imprenta La Cruz del Sur):

  • Dedication: "mis estimados primos Doctor Salvador González Aguilar y al Coronel Jorge Tenorio, los dos ya fallecidos" — both cousins were already dead by July 1987.
  • "El Doctor Aguilar me aconsejó y me ayudó a encausar mi libro, puedo decir que él como buen escritor me dió valor, para que lo publicara." — Dr. Salvador González Aguilar was himself a writer; he advised her on the manuscript and gave her the courage to publish it. (New detail: not just a helper — a fellow writer.)
  • "Al Coronel Tenorio, no tengo palabras para agradecer todo lo que hizo por mis hijos y por mí, cuando yo suplicaba por la vida de mi esposo." — confirms Tenorio's 1944 support firsthand.

The parents discrepancy — Rafael/Lola vs. Santiago/Susana [Family record vs. Registry — UNRESOLVED]

  • The novelized memoir names María's parents as "Don Rafael" and "Doña Lola" (dozens of mentions; e.g., "Don Rafael y Doña Lola estaban seguros que ellos se casarían").
  • The 1944 Central Penitentiary registry entry names her parents as Santiago González and Susana Jirón, birthplace San Salvador.
  • Leading reconciliation hypothesis [Unverified lead]: compound names — Rafael Santiago González (went by Rafael) and Susana Dolores Jirón (Lola is the standard diminutive of Dolores). Extremely common in Latin America; would reconcile both sources cleanly.
  • Alternatives: (a) "Rafael"/"Lola" are novel pseudonyms (the book pseudonymizes the Gavidia side as the "Romo" family, so it may do the same for her own parents); (b) the prison registry erred (filed under extreme duress, April 1944).
  • A brother exists: the novel gives María a brother, Rodolfo (González) — "tu hermana", "mi hermano Rodolfo". So Santiago/Rafael and Susana/Lola had at least two children: María Teresa and Rodolfo.

Dr. Salvador González Aguilar [Unverified lead — no trace found]

  • No online trace whatsoever: no obituary, no Diario Oficial mention, no publication record, no medical-directory hit.
  • Known only from the book: a cousin, a medical doctor, "un buen escritor" who midwifed the book into print, dead before July 1987.
  • His descendants (if any) may hold family papers and photographs — ask the cousins directly; this is a people lead, not a records lead.

Coronel Jorge Tenorio — family connection [Partially documented]

  • Military career fully documented separately (see coronel-jorge-tenorio-findings.md): 4th promotion Escuela Militar, captain 1944, major in the 1948 coup, colonel by ~1950, Policía Nacional director 1957, dead before July 1987.
  • Maternal surname never found — every source says only "Jorge Tenorio."
  • The family link (which of María Teresa's parents connects to the Tenorios) is unknown. If her mother was a Jirón, Tenorio is likely a paternal-side (González) cousin — or via a grandmother. Unverified.

Jirón surname in El Salvador [Documented]

  • Very rare: forebears.io counts 1 person with "Jirón" in El Salvador (vs. tens of thousands for the variant "Girón").
  • Sources: https://forebears.io/es/surnames/jirón ; https://esp-apellidos.com/apellido-jiron-significado-origen-y-historia/
  • This is good news: a rare surname is far easier to trace in parish books than González. Any "Jirón" in San Salvador baptism/marriage indices ~1880–1920 is likely family.

María Teresa's death [Unverified lead]

  • No obituary or death record found online. Born ~1917 → would have been ~90 around 2007. Family knowledge needed; check with cousins whether she died in El Salvador or the US, and where she is buried.

Bottom line

The single most promising thread is the birth certificate. María Teresa's birth record (~1917, San Salvador per the registry — or San José Guayabal per family lore) names her parents and her four grandparents in one document, which would resolve the Rafael/Santiago and Lola/Susana questions simultaneously. It is already among the five Simple SV filings — keep it at the top of the list.

Second: Rodolfo González, the brother. A second child of the same parents doubles the chances of finding the family in baptism/marriage indices and corroborates the parents' names.

Third: ask the living cousins about Dr. Salvador González Aguilar — a doctor-writer who died before 1987 may have children with the family papers, possibly including correspondence with María Teresa about the book.

Trip checklist additions (October 12 – November 12)

  • Registro Civil, San Salvador: María Teresa González birth ~1917 (already a Simple SV filing — confirm it names parents/grandparents); marriage of Santiago/Rafael González + Susana/Dolores Jirón ~1910–1916; death of María Teresa González de Ocampo (2000s?).
  • Parroquia San José Guayabal: baptism of María Teresa if the civil record points there (family lore says born there ~1917).
  • Parish baptism indices, San Salvador ~1915–1925: look for Rodolfo González (brother) and any "Jirón" entries — the rare surname makes manual index scanning feasible.
  • Biblioteca Nacional / UES: search catalog for works by "Salvador González Aguilar" (he was "un buen escritor" — he may have published).
  • AGN: military file for Coronel Jorge Tenorio (may give full name with maternal surname → reveals the González/Tenorio link).
  • Cementerio General: look for González/Jirón family plots near the 1954 martyrs' plaque visit.

Evidence-strength summary

Parents' names (book: Rafael/Lola) [Family record] · Parents' names (registry: Santiago/Susana Jirón) [Registry] · Reconciliation as compound names [Unverified lead] · Brother Rodolfo [Family record] · Dr. González Aguilar as writer/cousin [Family record] · His biography/descendants [Unverified lead] · Tenorio family link [Unverified lead] · Jirón rarity in El Salvador [Documented] · María Teresa's death [Unverified lead]

Guirola Duke and the 1932 Matanza

NEW: Ángel Guirola Duke — 1919 Presidential Candidate, 1932 Counterrevolution Leader

Date: 2026-10-07 (evening dig)

Status: NEW biographical detail — not in guirola-duque-lead.md

Source: ecumenico.org, "Tiburones y delfines de la oligarquía salvadoreña"

---

The find

Ángel Guirola Duke — the man the cousin names as great-grandfather José Gavidia's

biological father — was a far more prominent political figure than our file suggested:

  • Ran for president in 1919 as a candidate.
  • **In January 1932, "se pone a la cabeza de la contrarrevolución para reprimir

a los sectores populares"** — he led the counterrevolution to repress the

popular sectors (the Matanza).

  • Described as representing "los intereses de los sectores más reaccionarios

de la oligarquía agroexportadora" through much of the 20th century.

Our existing file called him "the least-documented of the brothers" with "no

birth/death dates or personal biography found." This corrects that: he was a

hardline oligarchic political operator, not an obscure sibling.

---

Why it matters

The irony: If the paternity story is true, the arc is extraordinary —

Ángel Guirola Duke led the 1932 repression that killed ~30,000 peasants;

his alleged grandson (the captain) was executed by the same militarist

order in 1944; and the captain's brother-in-law Tito Calvo went from 1932

enforcer to 1944 rebel to firing squad. Three generations, same machinery.

Credibility assessment: This does not prove or disprove the paternity claim.

It does establish that Ángel Guirola Duke was a real, consequential political

actor in exactly the era and place the family story requires (Santa Tecla

oligarchy, active 1900s–1930s). A man of his station fathering an unrecognized

child with a woman of lower status ~1883 is historically unremarkable.

Caveat: Single source (ecumenico.org, a leftist historical site with an

editorial stance against the oligarchy). The 1919 candidacy and 1932 leadership

claims should be cross-checked against electoral records and Matanza histories

before being asserted as fact. Treat as [Single-source, needs corroboration].

---

What was NOT found (dead ends confirmed)

  • María Teresa González birth record: no baptism, civil record, or obituary

found online under any name variant. The parent-name discrepancy

(Don Rafael/Doña Lola vs. Santiago González/Susana Jirón) cannot be resolved

from web sources. Trip task stands.

  • Dagoberto Gavidia Castro post-1944: nothing beyond the already-documented

May 16, 1944 resignation withdrawal (San Bernardino Sun). No career, death,

or family trace online.

  • Carmen Gaviria (alleged mother of José): still no Salvadoran trace;

all hits remain Colombian parish records.

  • José Gavidia baptism ~1880–1885: still not found online.

The Guirola Duke Lead

Lead: Ángel Guirola Duque as great-grandfather José Gavidia's biological father

Date: 2026-10-07

Source of lead: Cousin (family-history group chat) — voice message, partially garbled

Status: UNVERIFIED — but the surrounding family details check out remarkably well

The claim

The cousin says he heard that great-grandfather José Gavidia (father of Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, 1915–1944) was:

  • the son of an eminent family but born out of wedlock ("hijo natural"), never legally recognized by his father;
  • determined to make his own way ("voy a luchar por mi cuenta") and did make a fortune;
  • biologically the son of Ángel Guirola Duque and Carmen Gaviria;
  • meaning the "Gavidia" surname comes from the mother (Gaviria → Gavidia shift);
  • the biological father being "hijo de un español que vino de Santander" (son of a Spaniard who came from Santander, Spain).

Someone entered "Helguerola" as José's father on FamilySearch; the cousin believes this is actually Ángel Guirola Duque but has not verified it.

What checks out

1. The Guirola family was real, eminent, and wealthy [Documented]

One of the richest and most powerful families of El Salvador's coffee era; owned large parts of Santa Tecla; built the Casa de las Águilas and Mansión Guirola. Ángel Guirola (1826–1910) was briefly acting president (Apr–Aug 1884), founder of the Banco Internacional, deputy, senator.

  • https://lado.mx/noticia/20696812/ (citing El Diario de Hoy and Violetta Dubois Guirola de Parker's documented family history)
  • https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%81ngel_Guirola
2. Rafael María Guirola was from Santander, Spain [Documented]

The lado.mx article states verbatim: Rafael María Guirola was a "segundón" oriundo de Santander who emigrated because inheritance customs excluded younger sons. He married Gertrudis de la Cotera y González — herself "de familia de abolengo también procedente de Santander" — in Zacatecoluca in 1808.

  • https://lado.mx/noticia/20696812/
  • This confirms the cousin's "español que vino de Santander" detail exactly. It is a specific, non-obvious fact he is unlikely to have guessed — the strongest credibility signal for the family story. (Note: he compresses a generation — the Santander Spaniard is Ángel Guirola Duque's grandfather, Rafael María, not his father; common in oral history.)
3. "Guirola Duke" is a real compound surname [Documented]

Ángel Guirola de la Cotera married Cordelia Duke Alexander (b. Kingston, Jamaica 1834, d. Santa Tecla 1920) in New York ~1858–59. Their children all carried Guirola Duke: Rafael, Adalberto (José Adalberto), Ángel, Eduardo, Julia, Lulú, Matilde (lists vary slightly; 10 born, 7–8 reached adulthood).

  • https://www.regionmagica.com/2026/03/quien-fue-cordelia-duke-alexander-la.html
  • https://ecumenico.org/los-patriarcas-de-la-oligarquia-salvadorena
4. Ángel Guirola Duke (the son) existed [Documented]

Listed as a son of Ángel Guirola and Cordelia Duke in multiple genealogies. He was a co-founder of the Instituto Moderno de Santa Tecla in the early 1900s (named alongside Rafael Guirola Duke among the founders), so he was an active adult in Santa Tecla society in the relevant era.

  • https://ecumenico.org/los-patriarcas-de-la-oligarquia-salvadorena
  • https://archive.org/download/santa-tecla-la-historia-y-el-futuro/Santa%20Tecla%2C%20la%20historia%20y%20el%20futuro%20-%20Alejandria_text.pdf (p. 88)
5. Timeline is plausible [Unverified lead, but coherent]
  • Ángel Guirola Duke (son): born ~1860s (brother Rafael b. 1864).
  • José Gavidia: married Carlota Castro ~1911; children born 1912+ (Juan José ~1912, the captain 1915) → José likely born ~1880–1885.
  • Ángel Jr. in his 20s fathering José out of wedlock ~1883 fits without strain.
6. "Helguerola" has a plausible explanation [Documented, with caveat]

Helguero/Helguera is a real Spanish surname of toponymic origin from Cantabria (Santander) — the towns Helguera de Reocín, Helguera de Molledo, Helguera de Rasines. "Helguerola" itself is not a standard documented surname; it may be a diminutive/variant form or a FamilySearch indexing/transcription error.

  • https://www.heraldicafamiliar.com/helguera/
  • https://significadodeapellidos.com/apellido-helguero/
  • Note the irony: whether the FamilySearch entry reads "Helguerola" (Cantabrian surname) or is a misreading of "Guirola," both point back to Santander — consistent with, not contradictory to, the Guirola story. Alternative hypothesis: the entry may record a genuinely different Cantabrian father, and the cousin's Guirola identification could be wrong. The actual entry needs to be inspected.
7. Guirola is a Basque surname [Documented]

From Basque "girola" (to surround/circle); spread to America via 19th-century Basque emigration; among the oldest respected families in El Salvador.

  • https://esp-apellidos.com/apellido-guirola-significado-origen-y-historia/

What does NOT check out / dead ends

  • Carmen Gaviria in El Salvador: no record found. "Gaviria" is overwhelmingly a Colombian (Antioquia) surname; all "Carmen Gaviria" hits are Colombian parish records. No Santa Ana / Salvadoran trace. [Unverified lead — no supporting evidence]
  • José Gavidia's birth/baptism record (~1880–1885): not found online. Salvadoran civil-registry and parish records for this period are not fully indexed on the public web. [Dead end online — needs parish/archive visit]
  • Ángel Guirola Duke's vital dates and biography: confirmed as a person, but no birth/death dates or personal biography found; he is the least-documented of the brothers (Rafael the minister and Adalberto the West Point colonel dominate the records). [Unverified lead]
  • Any record of an illegitimate child: none found. No document links Ángel Guirola Duke to a Carmen Gaviria or to José. [Unverified lead — the core of the claim]
  • "Helguerola" as a surname in Salvadoran records: not found anywhere; likely an indexing artifact. [Unverified lead]

Bottom-line assessment

The scaffolding is real; the core claim is unproven. Every verifiable peripheral detail the cousin mentioned checks out: the Guirolas were the eminent wealthy family; "Guirola Duque" is the exact compound surname of Ángel Guirola's children with Cordelia Duke; a son named Ángel Guirola Duke existed and was active in Santa Tecla in the right era; and — most strikingly — the family's Spanish patriarch Rafael María Guirola was indeed from Santander, a detail the cousin could not easily have invented. The timeline (Ángel Jr. b. ~1860s → José b. ~1880s) works, and the "unrecognized younger son makes his own fortune" motif even mirrors Rafael María's own story as a disinherited "segundón."

But: there is zero documentary evidence (yet) for the two load-bearing facts — (a) that Ángel Guirola Duke fathered a child with a Carmen Gaviria, and (b) that Carmen Gaviria existed in El Salvador at all. The "Helguerola" FamilySearch entry is the thread to pull, and it needs direct inspection before any conclusion.

Recommended next steps:

  • Get eyes on the actual FamilySearch "Helguerola" entry the cousin saw (what record type, what it really says, attached sources).
  • Santa Ana parish baptism records ~1880–1885 for José (gaviria/gavidia), naming parents — a trip task (Oct 12–Nov 12).
  • Any death record or biography for Ángel Guirola Duke (son) to pin his dates and residences.
  • AGN / Santa Ana civil registry: search "Gaviria" and "Gavidia" in Santa Ana indices.
  • Keep the alternative hypothesis open: "Helguerola" may be a real Cantabrian name, not a misread "Guirola."

Evidence-strength summary: family background [Documented] · Santander origin [Documented] · Ángel Guirola Duke's existence [Documented] · paternity of José [Unverified lead] · Carmen Gaviria [Unverified lead, no evidence found] · "Helguerola" reading [Unverified lead].

---

Round 2 — 2026-10-07 (evening)

Focus: Ángel Guirola Duke (son) biography; José Gavidia's death record; Carmen name variants; the "Helguerola" FamilySearch entry; Guirola footprint in Santa Ana; José as "hijo natural" in indexed records.

1. Ángel Guirola Duke was a major political figure, not an obscure son [Documented]

The single biggest round-2 find. The SIEP/ecuménico article "Tiburones y delfines de la oligarquía salvadoreña" (Roberto Pineda, 2011) profiles him directly:

  • "ANGEL GUIROLA DUKE — Hijo de Ángel Guirola de la Cotera y de Cordelia Duke. Participa en 1919 como candidato a la presidencia. Y en enero de 1932 se pone a la cabeza de la contrarrevolución para reprimir a los sectores populares. Durante buena parte del siglo XX represento los intereses de los sectores más reaccionarios de la oligarquía agroexportadora."
  • https://ecumenico.org/tiburones-y-delfines-de-la-oligarquia-salvadorena

A 2021 academic article ("El Martinato y la insurrección de 1932: mitos y verdades a medias," Revista Nuestro Tiempo, via academia.edu) places him at the founding of the repression apparatus:

  • "En la noche del 22 de enero de 1932 cuando se empezaban a realizar los asesinatos masivos, un pequeño pero importante grupo de hombres se reunió para constituir el Consejo de Orden Público. Estos eran don Rodolfo Duke (Carazo), don Ángel Guirola (Duke), don Mauricio L. Meardi (Scaffini), el doctor Francisco A. Lima y don J. Antonio Vilanova (Kreitz)…"
  • The same article calls him "uno de los más grandes latifundistas" who "se convirtió en dirigente del partido de Martínez el Pro Patria."
  • https://www.academia.edu/51018504/El_Martinato_y_la_insurrecci%C3%B3n_de_1932_mitos_y_verdades_a_medias

What this means for the investigation:

  • He was alive and politically active through at least January 1932. If born ~1860s, he was ~55 as a 1919 presidential candidate and ~68 in 1932 — timeline still coherent with fathering José ~1880–85 in his early 20s.
  • As a presidential candidate and 1932 figure, he left a paper trail: 1919 election records, Diario Oficial mentions, newspaper coverage, 1932 histories. His vital dates should be recoverable from these — a concrete next step.
  • Dramatic historical irony (story value, not evidence): if the paternity claim is true, the captain's biological grandfather was one of the oligarchs who helped consolidate the Martínez regime in 1932 — the same regime the captain died rebelling against in 1944.

2. Full sibling list and family dates refined [Documented]

The regionmagica.com Cordelia Duke Alexander article (March 2026, citing FamilySearch ID LJKZ-XVJ, Geni, MyHeritage, Wikipedia) lists all seven children of Ángel Guirola de la Cotera and Cordelia Duke Alexander: José Adalberto, Rafael, Ángel, Eduardo, Gertrudis, Cordelia Julia y Matilde — all carrying Guirola Duke.

  • Rafael Guirola Duke (1864–1919): minister, diplomat.
  • José Adalberto Guirola Duke (1864–1906): West Point graduate, died in the 1906 war with Guatemala. (Note: both listed as born 1864 — possibly twins, or a secondary-source error.)
  • Ángel, Eduardo, Gertrudis, Cordelia Julia, Matilde: integrated into elite society; Ángel co-founded the Instituto Moderno de Santa Tecla (1910s) per the Santa Tecla history volume (archive.org, p. 88).
  • https://www.regionmagica.com/2026/03/quien-fue-cordelia-duke-alexander-la.html

Ángel Sr. (from es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ángel_Guirola): born Zacatecoluca 15 Aug 1826, died Santa Tecla 27 Apr 1910; married Cordelia Duke Alexander (b. Kingston, Jamaica 31 Dec 1834, d. Santa Tecla 3 Jan 1920) in New York 1858. Cordelia's parents: Isaac Duke and Judith Alexander; her brother José Mauricio Duke Alexander became the US ex-consul figure in Salvadoran banking history.

3. Guirola presence in Santa Ana confirmed [Documented]

Two independent data points place Guirolas in Santa Ana (José's town):

  • Roberto Guirola (11 Dec 1919 – 30 Mar 2020), born in Santa Ana, El Salvador to Enrique and Maria Zelaya Guirola; died Houston, TX. A Guirola family was established in Santa Ana by the 1910s at the latest.
  • https://www.dignitymemorial.com/es-es/obituaries/houston-tx/roberto-guirola-9106035
  • Adán Molina Guirola served as Comandante (military commander) of the Department of Santa Ana in 1917 (per the academia.edu article on the Sociedad de Artesanos El Porvenir de Santa Tecla).
  • https://www.academia.edu/28294054/Sociabilidad_y_organizaciones_artesanales_obreras_la_Sociedad_de_Artesanos_El_Porvenir_de_Santa_Tecla_1902_1932

Neither is Ángel Jr. himself, but both show the Guirola clan had a Santa Ana footprint in the relevant era — the geographic objection ("Guirolas were Santa Tecla people, what would Ángel be doing in Santa Ana?") is weakened.

4. FamilySearch catalog: the exact records to pull [Documented]

The FamilySearch catalog confirms the following Salvadoran collections exist on microfilm/digital images (browse-only; requires free FS account):

  • Registros parroquiales: Bautismos 1861–1923 (DGS 4092378) — covers José's likely birth window ~1880–85.
  • Registros parroquiales: Matrimonios 1760–1965 (DGS 4092379) — covers José + Carlota's ~1911 marriage, which would name both sets of parents.
  • Registros civiles: Índice de Nacimientos 1879–1906 (DGS 8089578 / 8152767) — civil birth index.
  • https://www.familysearch.org/es/search/catalog/466193 (parish), https://www.familysearch.org/es/search/catalog/koha:687260 and /koha:704581 (civil)

These are the highest-value concrete next steps: José's baptism (~1880–85) and his 1911 marriage entry would each name his parents directly and could confirm or kill the claim in one record. Trip task for Oct 12–Nov 12, or a relative with a FamilySearch account.

5. Dead ends (round 2)

  • Ángel Guirola Duke's birth/death dates: still not pinned. No obituary, no standalone biography found online. (He was overshadowed in print by brothers Rafael the minister and Adalberto the West Point colonel, despite his own 1919 candidacy and 1932 role.)
  • José Gavidia's death record: not found. He was alive 1936-12-02 (25th anniversary photo). A Salvadoran death certificate would name his parents — still the second-highest-value record after the baptism/marriage.
  • Carmen (any variant) in El Salvador: "Carmen Gavidia," "Carmen Gabiria," "Carmen Gaviria" + Santa Ana/El Salvador — no hits. Unchanged from round 1.
  • The "Helguerola" FamilySearch entry: could not be located via web search. FamilySearch's person search requires login and isn't Google-indexed at the record level. Still needs direct inspection by someone with FS access (the cousin, or Carlos).
  • José as "hijo natural" in indexed records: no indexed Santa Ana baptism/marriage record for José found on the open web. The parish microfilms above are the path.
  • Guirola vital events in Santa Ana 1860–1900: no indexed births/marriages/deaths found online beyond the two data points in §3.

Updated bottom-line assessment

Round 2 strengthens the plausibility without touching the core gap. The biggest change: Ángel Guirola Duke is no longer "the least-documented brother" — he was a 1919 presidential candidate and a named founder of the January 1932 Consejo de Orden Público, described as one of the country's largest landowners. That makes him easier to trace (election records, Diario Oficial, 1932 histories should yield his vital dates), and it makes the cousin's story more, not less, coherent: a powerful, well-known oligarch is exactly the kind of father a family would whisper about but never document. The confirmed Guirola footprint in Santa Ana (a Guirola family there by 1919; a Molina Guirola as departmental commander in 1917) removes the geographic objection.

What would move the needle now, in order:

  • The actual FamilySearch "Helguerola" entry the cousin saw (quote it verbatim — record type, names, dates).
  • José's baptism (~1880–85) in Santa Ana parish books (DGS 4092378) — names parents.
  • José + Carlota's ~1911 marriage entry (DGS 4092379) — names parents.
  • Ángel Guirola Duke's vital dates from 1919 election coverage or Diario Oficial.
  • José's death certificate (post-1936) — names parents.
  • Who was Enrique Guirola of Santa Ana (father of Roberto b. 1919)? A Santa Ana Guirola branch could connect to Ángel Jr.

Evidence-strength summary (updated): family background [Documented] · Santander origin [Documented] · Ángel Guirola Duke's existence and public career [Documented] · Guirola presence in Santa Ana [Documented] · paternity of José [Unverified lead] · Carmen Gaviria [Unverified lead, no evidence found] · "Helguerola" reading [Unverified lead].

The Gutiérrez Family

The Gutiérrez family — three brothers executed April 10, 1944

Bottom line

The book Pugna a un dictador names three Gutiérrez brothers executed the morning of April 10, 1944: General Tomás Gutiérrez (called "the leader of the Revolution"), Colonel Lorenzo Gutiérrez (his brother), and Lieutenant Marcelino Gutiérrez ("another brother"). They were shot at 8 AM in the courtyard of the Police Barracks — not the cemetery — in front of all the political prisoners, "a Dantesque scene." With them died Major Pineda, who protested his innocence to the end. No independent source corroborates any of this — the documented April 10 execution lists name different men, and the Gutiérrez brothers have zero web trace. Treat the whole account as [Family record]/[Novel] until the trial dossier is checked.

What the book says [Family record]

  • The trial scene (pp. 95–98): the President of the Councils of War addresses "the general who led the revolution." The prosecutor accuses him of treason, of the deaths of troops and officers, of wanting to bring the country to chaos. Defense lawyers get one minute.
  • "The turn came to Colonel Gutiérrez, brother of the leader of the Revolution, and to another brother who was a lieutenant in the Army." Fifteen minutes for their defense — "a true pantomime."
  • Major Pineda's defense: he was loyal, took no part in the insurrection, was in his office when arrested; he had removed the fuses from the bombs so they would not explode; his witness was the Minister of Labor. The court silenced him. The prosecutor demanded death anyway.
  • The sentence, read aloud: "General Tomás Gutiérrez, Colonel Lorenzo Gutiérrez, Lieutenant Marcelino Gutiérrez, Major Pineda, Captain Roberto Flores Magaña, and seven lieutenants and sub-lieutenants will be shot at eight in the morning of this same day, for treason against their fatherland and their government."
  • Major Pineda went mad on hearing the sentence — undressing before the jury, repeating "I am innocent, I am loyal to my Government." The prison doctor declared him insane. The President of the Councils of War telephoned Martínez, who answered: "Shoot him; they are tricks."
  • Ten officers were shot at the General Cemetery at 8 AM (including Flores Magaña); the general, his brother the colonel, and Major Pineda were shot in the Police Barracks courtyard before the political prisoners "so that they would realize it and await their turn."
  • Earlier (p. 91): officers "led by the Colonel, brother of the General Líder," sought asylum at the American Embassy; the Ambassador refused and the Consul handed them over, citing the 1933 Montevideo treaty on asylum. This is almost certainly Colonel Lorenzo Gutiérrez.

The conflict — documented lists name different men [Documented]

The published April 10, 1944 execution lists (Wikipedia "Huelga de brazos caídos"; UES La Universidad article on the October 1944 coup, which reproduces the Diario Oficial notices) name:

  • General Alfonso Marroquín, Colonel Tito Tomás Calvo, Major Julio Faustino Sosa, Captain Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, Captain Antonio Gavidia Castro, Lieutenant Marcelino Calvo, Lieutenant Miguel Ángel Linares, Lieutenant Ricardo Mancía González, Lieutenant Eduardo Chacón, Lieutenant Óscar Armando Cristales.

No Gutiérrez. No Pineda. No Flores Magaña. Possible explanations:

  • The book (a novelized memoir written 40+ years later) conflates trial sessions or misremembers names.
  • The published lists are incomplete.
  • The Gutiérrez executions happened as described but were not in the Diario Oficial notices that survive.

One suggestive overlap: the documented list has "Teniente Marcelino Calvo" where the book has "Teniente Marcelino Gutiérrez." This could be a name confusion in either direction — do not assert an identification without the trial dossier.

Family relationship [Family record]

Per the book: three brothers — Tomás (general, the eldest and the leader), Lorenzo (colonel), Marcelino (lieutenant). No second surnames, no ages, no birthplace given in the book.

Not found [honest dead ends]

  • Zero web results for "Tomás Gutiérrez" general 1944 El Salvador, "Lorenzo Gutiérrez" coronel 1944, or "Marcelino Gutiérrez" teniente 1944. (The only "hermanos Gutiérrez" on the web are the 1872 Peruvian coup leaders — different people, different century.)
  • No photos, no descendants, no memorials, no obituaries.
  • No second surnames for any of the three.
  • Major Pineda's first name is not given in the book.

Why it matters anyway

If the book's account is accurate, the Gutiérrez brothers are the closest parallel to the Gavidia brothers in the whole 1944 story — an entire family that rebelled together and died together. General Tomás Gutiérrez is portrayed as the leader of the revolution, which would make him, not Marroquín, the uprising's central figure in the family's telling. Verifying or disproving this changes the historical picture.

Highest-value next documents (trip tasks)

  • The trial dossier (expediente del juicio militar) at the Archivo General de la Nación — the Cáceres Prendes article confirms a certified copy exists; it will name every accused and every condemned. This settles the Gutiérrez question definitively.
  • Diario Oficial, April 1944 — the execution announcements; check whether any Gutiérrez appears.
  • Cementerio General records, April 1944 — burial entries for the executed.
  • Diario Oficial officer appointments, 1935–1944 — a General Tomás Gutiérrez and Colonel Lorenzo Gutiérrez would appear in promotions lists if they existed.

Hacienda El Ángel

Hacienda El Ángel — Research Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: Location confirmed; ownership history incomplete

---

Bottom line

The family's Hacienda El Ángel is not in Santa Ana — it's in San Juan Opico, La Libertad, and it still exists today as Cooperativa Talcualuya. It was a very large property (~4,896 manzanas / ~3,427 hectares per a UES land-reform study). The captain administered it 1939–42, but family ownership is unproven. There are at least four different "Hacienda El Ángel" properties in El Salvador — the name alone proves nothing about the Guirola connection.

---

1. Location — [Documented]

  • Hacienda El Ángel (family) = now Cooperativa Talcualuya, San Juan Opico, La Libertad.
  • Coordinates: ~13.9365°N, -89.3316°W (Talcualuya); the "Centro Escolar Hacienda Talcualuya" school sits at ~13.9313°N, -89.3342°W — the place still carries the "Hacienda" name.
  • The family archive and research plan both place it at San Juan Opico / San Pablo Tacachico, La Libertad. The trip checklist already lists it: "Hacienda El Ángel, now Cooperativa Talcualuya (San Juan Opico)."
  • San Juan Opico is ~30 km northwest of San Salvador, in the lowland agricultural zone near Ciudad Arce.

2. Size — [Documented]

  • A UES academic study of land-reform properties ("Abordaje registral y catastral…", Revista Con-Secuencias) lists "El Ángel Talcualuya" in San Juan Opico at 3,427.20 hectares / 4,896 manzanas.
  • That is roughly 34 km² — a very large hacienda, consistent with the novel's depiction of 400+ colonos.

3. What the family records say — [Documented]

  • 1939: After being pushed out of military aviation amid regime suspicion, Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro administered Haciendas El Ángel and El Carmen (San Juan Opico / San Pablo Tacachico). The family archive explicitly flags: "Family ownership is UNPROVEN — he ran them." (Sources: Flotilla Aérea biography, family testimony.)
  • The novel (Pugna a un dictador, family = "Romo") depicts the hacienda in detail:
  • "Más de 400 colonos" (more than 400 tenant workers).
  • Payday Saturdays were "una verdadera feria," especially at harvest ("época de la recolección").
  • A "preciosa" two-story house with room for 10+ guest families, immense gardens.
  • Don José (great-grandfather José Gavidia) died at the hacienda — "una tarde Don José se puso mal… sufrió un colapso fulminante y murió."
  • The captain worked there after leaving the military; the family spent Christmases there.
  • April 1944: During the uprising, the captain "escapes through cane fields to Hacienda El Ángel" — placing it in a sugar-cane zone.
  • The family lived at Hacienda El Ángel 1939–42 (trip checklist notes San Juan Opico civil registrations for that period).

4. What it produced — [Unverified lead]

  • The novel never names the crop. The "cane fields" reference and the lowland Opico location point to sugar cane as the main crop (possibly with cattle/maize as secondary, typical of the zone).
  • San Juan Opico's neighbor haciendas in the same era grew coffee, maize, beans, and cane (per a UES local-history study of nearby Zapotitán properties).

5. From hacienda to cooperative — [Documented]

  • Properties over 500 hectares were expropriated under the 1980 agrarian reform; El Ángel Talcualuya (~3,427 ha) qualified and became Cooperativa Talcualuya.
  • The cooperative is still active today (appears in the national cooperative directory; the school retains the "Hacienda Talcualuya" name).
  • A YouTube video documents its history: "Recordar es volver a vivir! Así era antes la hacienda y cooperativa Talcualuya en San Juan Opico" (https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=redtoMjPrH4) — worth watching before the trip.

6. Critical distinction: there are MULTIPLE "Hacienda El Ángel" properties — [Documented]

Do not confuse the family's hacienda with these same-named properties:

| Property | Location | Notes |

|---|---|---|

| El Ángel Talcualuya | San Juan Opico, La Libertad | The family's. Now a cooperative. |

| Hacienda El Ángel (Meléndez) | Apopa, San Salvador | Owned by Francisco Meléndez since at least 1807 (Gutiérrez y Ulloa census: 34 caballerías; produced ganado, añil, azúcar, panela, maíz). Became Ingenio El Ángel — Grupo El Ángel, the sugar group founded 1882 by the Meléndez family. Still operating; second-largest sugar mill in the country. |

| Hacienda El Ángel | Cantón San Felipe, Concepción Batres, Usulután | Appears in 2026 crime news. |

| Hacienda El Ángel | Tapalhuaca, La Paz | Appears in a grenade-theft case. |

7. The "El Ángel" name vs. Ángel Guirola Duke — [Unverified lead]

  • The Apopa Hacienda El Ángel was Meléndez-owned since 1807 — the name long predates Ángel Guirola Duke (b. ~1860s) and has no Guirola connection there. "El Ángel" is simply a common religious dedication for haciendas.
  • No document links the San Juan Opico property to Ángel Guirola Duke or to the Guirola family.
  • Honest assessment: the shared name is intriguing given the paternity investigation, but it proves nothing. The Opico property could have been named by any previous owner, or the name could be pure coincidence. Resolving this requires the property's ownership chain (CNR / land-reform expropriation file), which would name the pre-1980 owner.

8. Hacienda El Carmen — [Unverified lead]

  • Mentioned only alongside El Ángel as the second property the captain administered 1939–42, located toward San Pablo Tacachico.
  • No independent online trace found. Likely also expropriated in the 1980 reform. The CNR and the land-reform archives are the way to identify it.

9. Current status / visitability — [Documented]

  • Cooperativa Talcualuya is an active agricultural cooperative — visitable, and cooperative members are the best living source for the property's pre-1980 history (who owned it, what it produced, whether any Gavidia connection is remembered).
  • The old hacienda "casco" (main house) may survive — the YouTube video shows the historic buildings.

10. Open questions (trip tasks)

  • Who owned El Ángel Talcualuya before the 1980 expropriation? → CNR (matrícula/folio real) + the land-reform expropriation file (ISTA/AGN).
  • Did the Gavidia family ever hold title, or only administer it? → Same sources; also ask cooperative elders.
  • Locate Hacienda El Carmen (San Pablo Tacachico) → CNR + local inquiry.
  • Photograph the casco, the cooperative, and the school; watch the YouTube history video first.
  • San Juan Opico civil registry 1939–42 — the family lived at the hacienda; births/deaths from those years may be registered there.

---

  • Gutiérrez y Ulloa (1807), via Con-Secuencias — Apopa Hacienda El Ángel, Francisco Meléndez, 34 caballerías.
