The Hacienda Years (1939–1942)

Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro — The Hacienda Years (1939–1942)

1. Why he was pushed out of aviation

[Family record] — Pugna a un dictador, primera parte, pp. 71–73. The book gives a detailed scene:

  • After a wedding-anniversary dinner at his house (organized by fellow officers who invited themselves and chose the menu), a colleague who had not been invited reported it to superiors as a clandestine political meeting.
  • His chief summoned him: "Capitán, con mucha pena lo llamo para notificarle su baja en la Aviación y queda agregado al Ministerio." The stated reason: "Según informes usted tiene reuniones clandestinas en su casa contra el Gobierno."
  • The chief added, revealingly: "su padre es adinerado y tiene muchas propiedades que usted puede administrar, así me lo han comunicado" — the regime was aware of the family's wealth.
  • Carlos requested his discharge (baja) the same day. At his farewell dinner with fellow pilots, he told them the informer was among the uninvited, warned them that "aquí entre nosotros hay espías," forgave the informer, and walked out.

[Documented] — Historical context anchoring the date to ~1939:

  • 1938: His uncle José Cipriano Castro Bernal ("Don Juan") resigned his post after confronting Martínez over reelection, and was exiled to Guatemala. The book places this just before: "Para entonces el tío de Carlos renunció de su cargo" after "La proclama de la tercera reelección del Presidente."
  • 1939: Martínez secured his third term (1939–1944) through a Constituent Assembly that amended Article 91 of the Constitution — the "reelección" the book references. Regime paranoia peaked; the Código Rojo made even contemplating revolt punishable by death.
  • The FAS official history (fas.gob.sv) lists "Carlos Gavidia" among pilot graduates of 1930–1940 but records no discharge — unsurprising for a politically motivated removal.

[Unverified lead]: Whether the 1939 discharge/baja appears in the Diario Oficial — trip task (AGN / Diario Oficial archive).

2. Who owned Hacienda El Ángel / El Carmen in 1939–42?

[Family record] — The book twice implies extensive family property:

  • The aviation chief's remark: "su padre es adinerado y tiene muchas propiedades."
  • After Don José's sudden death (a fulminating collapse, shortly after Carlos moved to the hacienda): "Después de sepultarlo se reunió toda la familia y fue Carlos el designado para administrar todas las propiedades." The family formally designated Carlos administrator of all the properties.

Critical nuance: The book says "many properties" but never names El Ángel or El Carmen specifically as owned (vs. administered). The family archive explicitly flags: "Family ownership is UNPROVEN — he ran them." (timeline tl-008).

[Unverified lead]: The CNR matrícula (folio real) for the Talcualuya/El Ángel property would show the pre-1980 ownership chain — trip task. Land-reform expropriation files (1980) may also name the pre-reform owner. Nothing on ownership is findable online; the UES land-reform study gives size (~4,896 manzanas) but not the 1939–42 owner.

Sequence per the book: discharge from aviation → move to the hacienda → Don José dies suddenly → Carlos designated administrator of all family properties → runs the haciendas 1939–42.

3. Life at the hacienda

[Family record] — The book's depiction is vivid and consistent across passages:

  • Scale: "Había más de 400 colonos" (400+ resident workers). Saturday paydays were "una verdadera feria sobre todo en la época de la recolección" — a true fair, especially at harvest. Vendors came from the capital; workers played guitars; "allí se vivía con tranquilidad."
  • Crops: Coffee ("recibía el café"), cotton ("revisaba las plantaciones de algodón"; later instructions on "siembras de algodón"), cattle ("vacunar el ganado"). Sugar cane is implied by the Opico lowland location and the 1944 flight "through cane fields" (family archive tl-012).
  • The big house: "La casa de la Hacienda era preciosa de dos plantas y en sus espaciosos corredores se veían los racimos de flores. En la planta alta estaban los dormitorios para los huéspedes y abajo los dormitorio principales de la familia, había espacio para alojar más de diez familias, los jardines eran inmensos y bellos."
  • Carlos at work: "Carlos se dedicaba de verdad a trabajar... era corto el día para tanto trabajo." He called María daily by telephone, "organizó sus trabajos perfectamente." Even while in San Salvador he sent planting and cattle-vaccination instructions through Betina to the administrator.
  • Family life: Christmas and New Year at the hacienda; brothers and friends visiting; piano, dancing, singing; María hosting "like a king." A portrait of a functioning patriarchal estate.

4. How the hacienda years connect to the 1944 uprising

[Family record]:

  • Capitán Flores was "de los pocos militares que visitaban a Carlos" — one of the few officers who visited him at the hacienda. Through Flores, Carlos learned "el movimiento de los cuarteles y la sucia política que el Gobierno llevaba a base de torturas e injusticias." The hacienda was an information node in the conspiracy network.
  • The book describes the conspiracy forming: "En esa época empezó la conspiración contra el Presidente. Un grupo de valientes, de jefes, oficiales y civiles sesionaban en diferentes lugares, para no ser descubiertos." The hacienda is not explicitly named as a meeting place, but Flores's regular visits made it a link.
  • Paco (José Antonio) came urgently to María's house asking for Carlos about "a business" — coded conspiracy contact during this period.

[Analysis]: The 1939–42 years transformed Carlos from a pilot into a landed administrator commanding 400+ workers — a rural power base with its own loyalty networks, plus ongoing (if discreet) contact with conspiring officers through Flores. When April 1944 came, he had military training, a hacienda refuge, and a direct line into the plot. The discharge meant to neutralize him politically instead gave him three years to build independent standing.

5. The April 1944 flight

[Family record] (book, segunda parte):

  • As the uprising collapsed, Betina instructed María: "Si alguien te pregunta por Carlos, diles que ignoras dónde está, que él salió para la Hacienda."
  • Carlos wrote to María: "pronto cruzaría la frontera de Honduras."
  • "Carlos fue capturado cuando llegaba ya casi a la frontera de un hermano país. Remitido como un criminal, era doloroso ver el sufrimiento que se reflejaba en su rostro."

[Family archive] (timeline tl-012): "escapes through cane fields to Hacienda El Ángel, and is betrayed by a guide near the Honduran border."

The accounts align: he fled west from San Salvador through the hacienda's cane fields toward Honduras, was betrayed by a guide, and captured near the border.

6. Correction to a prior report

[Family record] — During this research I verified a direct quote the flores-magana-puzzle.md report claimed did not exist. Pugna a un dictador, primera parte 2, line 1076:

"—Pero ¿Qué te pasa Manuel? dijo Marta"

Marta does call Flores "Manuel" in direct quoted speech. Per the book's own method (real names surface in quoted speech; pseudonyms in narrative), "Manuel" is likely his real first name — supporting "Manuel Roberto Flores Magaña" (or at minimum, Manuel as genuine). The puzzle report's claim that "that quote doesn't exist" is incorrect and should be revised. The hypothesis that "Flores Magaña" is a pure pseudonym is weakened (though "Magaña" as surname remains unconfirmed in any documented record).

Trip tasks arising

  • Diario Oficial 1939 — Carlos's baja/discharge notice (confirms date and official wording).
  • CNR San Juan Opico — matrícula/folio real for El Ángel/Talcualuya (pre-1980 ownership chain).
  • San Juan Opico civil registry 1939–42 — family births/deaths registered at the hacienda.
  • AGN — Carlos's military personnel file (discharge record, postings).
  • Cooperativa Talcualuya — ask elders about pre-1980 owners/administrators (was it the Gavidias'?).
  • Revise flores-magana-puzzle.md re: the "Manuel" quote (line 1076, primera-parte-2.md).

Merino in the Trial Record

Juan Ramón Merino — Research Report (Follow-up)

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: Identity CONFIRMED via the 1944 trial record. Post-trial fate unknown.

---

Bottom line

Subteniente Juan Ramón Merino was real. He appears in the actual April 1944 Council of War trial record (expediente): he was formally indicted, gave a sworn declaration at folio 40, and his defense was that he was following General Marroquín's orders. This is the first documentary confirmation of the man from Ricardo's tank story — independent of both the family oral history and the Flotilla Aérea biography.

---

The find

Jorge Cáceres Prendes, "PALABRAS. La memoria histórica del alzamiento militar salvadoreño del dos de abril de 1944 a la luz del expediente del juicio correspondiente," Diálogos: Revista Electrónica de Historia, Número especial 2008 (Universidad de Costa Rica). The author worked from a photocopy of the complete trial expediente.

The article cites: "declaración del SubTte. Juan Ramón Merino a fs.40" — as an example of junior officers who defended themselves by saying they obeyed Marroquín's orders (Marroquín being seconded by his brother Tito Calvo in everything Tito said/did).

What the expediente is
  • Two pieces. Piece 1: opened "a las siete horas del día cuatro de abril de mil novecientos cuarenta y cuatro," closed April 26 — 261 folios. Piece 2: 44 folios, last declaration taken May 3, 1944.
  • 47 indiciado (defendant) testimonies, 64 witness testimonies.
  • First Council of War (April 10): condemned 25 of 29 to death (23 military, 6 civilian formally indicted to that point). 13 military + 1 civilian were actually executed (those present).
  • Second Council (April 22–23): condemned 19 to death (15 military, 4 civilian). 3 military executed.
  • The author states he planned to deposit a copy of the expediente in a publicly accessible Salvadoran archive.
Council of War membership (confirmed)
  • Gral. Luis Andreu
  • Cnel. Santiago Ayala
  • Cnel. Joaquín Adolfo Fermán

Note: Santiago Ayala's presence on the council is now documentary — relevant to the (now superseded) Ayala second-husband hypothesis and to the family's account of who judged the executed.

Tank detail (corroborating context)

The article notes Tito Calvo rode out in a tank multiple times on April 3, the last time to request asylum at the American legation — captured on the way out. Tanks were central to the uprising's final hours, consistent with both the biography's "Merino's armored ground unit" and Ricardo's "famous tank at Ciudad Delgado."

---

What this resolves

| Question | Answer | Evidence |

|---|---|---|

| Full name | Subteniente Juan Ramón Merino | Trial expediente, fs.40, via Cáceres Prendes 2008 |

| Branch/rank in 1944 | Army subteniente (not navy, not colonel) | Same |

| Was he indicted? | Yes — formally indiciado | Same |

| His defense | Followed Marroquín's orders | Same |

What it does NOT resolve

  • His fate. One mention only. Was he condemned, acquitted, or amnestied (the Menéndez amnesty after May 9)? Unknown.
  • The tank at Ciudad Delgado. Not in the article. Lives in Relámpagos de Libertad + family memory only.
  • "De la marina" / "coronel." The cousin's rank/branch recollection contradicts the 1944 record. Possibilities: Merino transferred to the navy later (El Salvador created its navy in October 1952) and rose to colonel; or the cousin conflated details. His later career is the open thread.
  • The marriage to Lovo Castelar's sister. No public trace. Family knowledge only.

Discrepancy to note

The Flotilla Aérea biography (Mario Alfaro) says the captain "joined Subteniente Juan Ramón Merino's armored ground unit" — consistent with Merino commanding armor as a subteniente in April 1944. The trial record confirms the rank. Ricardo's "coronel de la marina" must refer to a later period, if accurate.

Next steps

  • The expediente itself — if Cáceres Prendes deposited the copy in a Salvadoran archive (he said he would), fs.40 gives Merino's full sworn declaration: unit, actions April 2–3, and possibly the tank.
  • Post-1944 military lists (escalafón) for "Juan Ramón Merino" — to trace the navy transfer and colonel rank.
  • Ricardo's Relámpagos pages — the decisive source for the Ciudad Delgado episode.
  • Marriage record: Merino × (sister of Lovo Castelar) — would confirm the brother-in-law link and give her name.

Sources

  • Jorge Cáceres Prendes, "PALABRAS. La memoria histórica del alzamiento militar salvadoreño del dos de abril de 1944 a la luz del expediente del juicio correspondiente," Diálogos (UCR), Número especial 2008. https://revistas.ucr.ac.cr/index.php/dialogos/article/download/31797/31485/93301
  • Family oral history (Ricardo Gavidia, voice note, 2026-10-07).
  • Mario Alfaro biography via Flotilla Aérea (armored unit reference).

Living Witnesses

Living witnesses of April 1944 — research map (NO CONTACT)

Research only. No outreach, no contact attempts. For Carlos to decide later.

Compiled 2026-10-07.

---

1. Merceditas Ros — niece of the Calvo Marroquín [Documented]

Who: Izalqueña, niece ("sobrina") of the executed Calvo Marroquín brothers. Was 7 years old in April 1944 (~89 now, if living).

Source: Quoted at length in Los Izalcos: Testimonio de un Indígena by Julio Leiva Masin, Editorial Universitaria (UES), Colección Bicentenario, 2011.

  • https://es.scribd.com/doc/245045607/Los-Izalcos-testimonio-de-Un-Indigena (passage at pp. 177–178)

What she said (summarized):

  • She visited her uncles' house daily in that era.
  • Martínez's 1966 assassination in Honduras was vengeance: the killer, Cipriano Morales, was the godson of one of the executed (Alfonso or Tito — she wasn't sure which), raised in the Calvo Marroquín house where one of his parents worked for years.
  • The family lived in silence and sought invisibility from fear of reprisals.
  • She remembers clearly the pain and fear in the family when the firing squad killed her uncles.
  • Emilia and Victoria, sisters of the executed, wore mourning for life.
  • Remains: Tito and Alfonso in San Salvador; Marcelino in Izalco.
  • Tito Tomás Calvo was among the most bloodthirsty officers crushing the 1932 peasant uprising, earned Martínez's trust — but in his last years opposed the dictator, joined the coup, and was shot by his "entrañable amigo y compadre" (Martínez himself).

Status: The single best-documented living witness of the 1944 executions' aftermath. Whether she is still alive is unknown — no obituary found; no contact info sought.

---

2. Sosa's son — son of Major Julio Faustino Sosa [Documented via memoir]

Who: Son of Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa (executed April 10, 1944), same name as his father, ~85 now (born ~1941).

Source: Memoir passage in El Independiente, Edición 1114 (March 28 – April 3, 2024), "La UES se niega a morir":

  • https://elindependiente.sv/wp-content/uploads/2024/04/EL-INDEPENDIENTE.-Edicion-1114.-28-marzo-3-abril-2024.-La-UES-se-niega-a-morir.pdf
  • The memoirist writes: a son of Major Sosa, "con su mismo nombre," was his primary-school classmate in his earliest school years; through him the writer learned details of the family's suffering. The writer was 3 years old in 1944 — the same age as Sosa's son.

Name discrepancy [flag]: The memoir calls the father "Julio Domingo Sosa"; official records say "Julio Faustino Sosa." Verify before asserting identity.

Status: Existence documented via the memoir; no independent trace of the son (no full name confirmed, no current whereabouts). The memoirist's identity (author of the El Independiente piece) is itself a lead — he knew the son as a child.

---

3. Flores-Pérez children [Unresolved — no trace]

Who: The children of "Capitán (Roberto) Flores Magaña" and "Marta Pérez" per Pugna a un dictador — including a newborn in April 1944 (~82 now).

Findings: Zero web trace of Marta Pérez, Flores Magaña, or any children under those names.

Complication: The leading hypothesis (see flores-magana-puzzle.md) is that "Flores Magaña" and possibly "Marta Pérez" are pseudonyms per the author's stated method. The real husband may be Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas (the only unmapped captain on the documented April 10 list; "Manuel" appears in the book's quoted speech — "¿Qué te pasa Manuel? dijo Marta" — and quoted speech uses real names).

Decisive test (trip task): A ~1930s marriage record for "Flores Magaña / Marta Pérez" — if it exists, the names are real and the children's birth records follow; if nothing turns up, the pseudonym hypothesis strengthens. Also: Manuel Sánchez Dueñas's marriage record.

---

4. Other 1944 descendants — sweep results

  • Calvo Marroquín extended family: Sisters Emilia and Victoria (lifelong mourning); the Ros family of Izalco (Merceditas's line). No other named living members found online.
  • Gavidia line: Francisco José Gavidia Ayala (Juan José's grandson, San Salvador) — documented via 2019 succession edicts; zero online footprint; contact path is the notary René Alirio Ayala (see francisco-jose-gavidia-ayala.md).
  • No other documented children/witnesses of 1944 participants surfaced in public sources (obituaries, histories, memorials). The 1944 generation's children would now be in their 80s; most left no online trace.

---

Trip tasks (San Salvador / Izalco)

  • Izalco: ask after the Ros family / Merceditas Ros (respectfully; she may still live there).
  • San Salvador civil registry: Sosa's son's birth record (~1941); Flores/Marta or Sánchez Dueñas marriage (~1930s).
  • Cementerio General: Calvo Marroquín plots (Tito, Alfonso); Sosa plot.
  • Izalco cemetery: Marcelino Calvo's remains.
  • AGN: trial dossier (names all 44 condemned + defenders — resolves Flores/Marta definitively).
