Labels: [Documented] = published source; [Unresolved] = no public trace. Nothing here authorizes contact.

Luis Lovo Castelar — the Second Husband (Family-Confirmed)

Coronel Luis Lovo Castelar — Verification as María Teresa's Second Husband

Date: 2026-10-07

Source of identification: Family — Carlos's cousin states "Roberto en realidad era el Cnel. Luis Lovo Castelar." Per family, María Teresa herself ("Mamá Tere") confessed this to the family when the book was newly published (1987).

Status: LIKELY — core identification strongly supported by family testimony; several book details do not match public records (see discrepancies).

Supersedes: The Santiago Ayala hypothesis (coronel-ayala.md). The Ayala theory's strongest dot ("voted for the executions") does not transfer — see below.

---

The man — documented biography [Documented]

Coronel Luis Lovo Castelar was a real, prominent Salvadoran military officer and intellectual:

  • Escuela Militar director, 1953 (listed as #14 in the "IV Época" directors).
  • Clash with President José María Lemus (1956–60): After a confrontation, Lemus sent him to Mexico on a scholarship ("beca"); when it was suspended, he stayed and studied economics at the Politécnico, defending his thesis in 1962: Un proyecto de reforma agraria de El Salvador — later published as a book and distributed among military, technicians, and politicians.
  • Returned to El Salvador in 1962 after Lemus fell and Julio Rivera took power.
  • Agrarian reform pioneer: One of the first military men to seriously push land reform; wrote for La Universidad's 1967 special issue on reforma agraria alongside Schafik Handal, Roberto Lara Velado, and others. Described as "un masón militar impulsor de la reforma agraria" (Freemason).
  • 1969 "Guerra de Legítima Defensa" (Football War): Reincorporated to active service, served as Ayudante de Campo (aide-de-camp) to the Director General of the Guardia Nacional. Wrote the book La Guardia Nacional en Campaña about it. His stated position: the war was a limited punitive operation, not a territorial conquest.
  • Diplomat: Represented El Salvador at the UN Conference on the Law of the Sea ("El Sr. LOVO-CASTELAR (El Salvador)").
  • Later career: By the 1980s, "Dr. Luis Lovo Castelar" represented the Minister of Planning (MIPLAN) in official matters — the "Dr." reflecting his economics doctorate.
  • Children (documented): Son José Luis Lovo Castelar — lawyer, law professor (Universidad José Matías Delgado), court magistrate (called to conform Sala, 2019). A Florencia Lovo Castelar also appears as a lawyer — likely another child. Both carry "Lovo Castelar," NOT "Ayala."

---

Fit with the book's "Roberto"

| Book detail | Lovo Castelar match | Verdict |

|---|---|---|

| Rising officer, ~1946 remarriage | Young officer in 1946 (estimated b. ~1922); plausible | [Plausible] |

| Rose to "Minister of War" under Osorio (1950–56) | No record of him as Defense Minister. Documented ministers: Bolaños (1950–53), Molina (1953–56). He was Escuela Militar director (1953). | [Contradicted — likely novelized] |

| "Voted for the executions" (1944 Council of War) | Too young — would have been ~22 in 1944, a junior officer. No record of him on any 1944 council. | [Contradicted — likely novelized] |

| Retired 1956 | His clash with Lemus was 1956–60; timeline roughly compatible with leaving active prominence | [Unverified] |

| First wife "Ana" (married Venezuelan diplomat) | No information found | [Unverified] |

| Mother "Doña Delia" | No information found | [Unverified] |

| Children "Ayala" (Gloria Gavidia Ayala, Herbert Ayala) | His documented children are "Lovo Castelar." No Ayala link found in any search. | [Open — unexplained] |

---

The "Ayala" problem [Unresolved]

This is the single biggest open question. Carlos's own notes say "María Teresa's second marriage (Ayala)" and the children carry "Ayala." But:

  • Luis Lovo Castelar's full name shows no "Ayala" component (Luis = first name, Lovo = paternal, Castelar = maternal).
  • His documented children are "Lovo Castelar," not "Ayala."
  • No search connects "Lovo Castelar" with "Ayala" in any context.

Possible explanations (all unverified):

  • Carlos's "(Ayala)" note is wrong or refers to something else.
  • The children were registered with "Ayala" for unknown reasons.
  • María Teresa had TWO post-Carlos relationships (Ayala AND Lovo Castelar), and the book fused them — the book is proven to fuse people (cf. General Tomás Gutiérrez = Marroquín + Calvo).
  • "Ayala" was a name Lovo Castelar used in some context (no evidence).

Resolution: The ~1946 San Salvador marriage record names the husband definitively. Also: ask the cousin directly — this is family knowledge, not a records problem.

---

Why "LIKELY" and not "CONFIRMED"

For: María Teresa herself told the family that "Roberto" was Luis Lovo Castelar. This is first-hand author testimony relayed by family — stronger than any of our documentary hypotheses. The cousin confirms it independently. Lovo Castelar fits the general profile: prominent colonel, right era, charismatic intellectual officer of exactly the type the book describes.

Against / unresolved: The "Ayala" children's surname has no explanation. The book's "Minister of War" and "voted for the executions" details do not fit Lovo Castelar's documented career — but the book is proven to novelize (the "Minister of War" claim was already contradicted under the Ayala hypothesis too).

What would confirm: The ~1946 marriage record (San Salvador civil registry — trip task, already on the checklist).

---

What this changes

  • The Santiago Ayala hypothesis is retired. Its strongest evidence ("voted for the executions" via council membership) belonged to Santiago Ayala the individual, not to "Roberto." If Roberto = Lovo Castelar, that book detail was novelized.
  • The "Minister of War" detail was novelized under both hypotheses — the book elevated its hero.
  • New research threads: Lovo Castelar's 1962 agrarian reform book (does the family's copy exist?); his UN diplomatic career; his 1969 war memoir (La Guardia Nacional en Campaña); contact with the Lovo Castelar family (José Luis Lovo Castelar is a public figure — lawyer/professor — but no outreach without Carlos's approval).
  • The "Ayala" question is now the #1 open thread on the second marriage.

---

Trip checklist additions

  • Registro Civil, San Salvador: Marriage of María Teresa González + [husband] ~1946 — names him definitively, resolves Ayala vs. Lovo Castelar.
  • Biblioteca Nacional / UES: Lovo Castelar's Un proyecto de reforma agraria de El Salvador (1962) and La Guardia Nacional en Campaña.
  • AGN: Military personnel file for Cnel. Luis Lovo Castelar (birth date, promotions, assignments 1940s–50s).
  • Diario Oficial 1950–56: Any Lovo Castelar appointments (check for vice-minister or other senior posts the book may have inflated to "Minister").

---

Evidence-strength summary

Existence and career of Luis Lovo Castelar [Documented] · Identification as "Roberto" [Family testimony — María Teresa herself, via cousin] · Marriage ~1946 [Unverified] · Minister of War claim [Contradicted — novelized] · "Voted for executions" [Contradicted — novelized] · Children "Ayala" vs. "Lovo Castelar" [Unresolved — open question] · First wife Ana / mother Delia [Unverified]

María Teresa's Later Life

María Teresa González — later life after 1944

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: Book's Third Part fully mined; web searches for death/obituary and second husband came up empty; one analytical breakthrough on the husband's identity.

Bottom line

The book's Third Part novelizes her life from ~1946 to ~1974: remarriage to a rising officer, his ascent to Minister of Defense under President Óscar Osorio, her charity work, two more children, a dramatic episode in which she saves two condemned officers' lives (mirroring 1944 with the roles reversed), a silver anniversary, and a quiet ending. The major new finding: the pseudonym key's "Confirmed" mapping of her second husband as "Coronel Roberto Ocampo" needs revision — Carlos's own notes say the second marriage was "(Ayala)" with children Gloria Gavidia Ayala and Herbert Ayala, and the author explicitly states that ALL protagonist names are changed. "Roberto Ocampo" is very likely itself a pseudonym for a real Coronel Ayala. Her death remains undocumented; a Miami 2016 obituary is an unverified lead only.

The second husband problem [Analysis — revises prior "Confirmed" mapping]

  • The book marries her to "Roberto Ocampo": a captain in 1944 (the narrative says he sat on the Councils of War), later coronel, then Minister of War and Aviation. [Novel]
  • The author's front-matter note states explicitly that the names of ALL protagonists are changed. [Documented — her own statement]
  • Carlos's own notes (2026-10-04) list "María Teresa's second marriage (Ayala): Gloria Gavidia Ayala and Herbert Ayala." [Family record]
  • Inference: "Ocampo" is a screen name; the real husband was a Coronel surnamed Ayala, very likely Osorio's defense minister or a senior officer of that era. The pseudonym key should downgrade "Coronel Roberto Ocampo" from Confirmed to "pseudonym — real identity probably Coronel ___ Ayala." [Unverified lead]
  • No web trace found for a "Roberto Ocampo" as Salvadoran colonel/minister, which is consistent with the name being invented. [Negative search]
  • Open: his first name, full identity, dates, and whether the marriage ended in divorce or his death. The book ends ~1974 with them together; she published as an independent author in 1987.

Timeline from the Third Part [Novel — her account, novelized]

  • ~1946 ("two years a widow"): Roberto courts her; she resists out of loyalty to Carlos. They marry in a small family ceremony (the Governor officiates; witnesses are her brother and Betina's husband). They move to a new house outside the city, "Quinta Santa María" — she refuses to live where either had lived before. [Novel]
  • Late 1940s: Roberto rises — Teniente Coronel, then Coronel. The three older sons go to study in the US/Canada. A daughter, Elizabeth, is born (cesarean); later a sixth child, a boy. [Novel]
  • ~1950–56 (Osorio presidency): Roberto becomes Minister of War and Aviation. María founds/supervises daycares (guarderías) alongside the President's wife and does charity work among the poor. [Novel]
  • The Muñiz/Velásquez episode: two colonels plot against the government, are condemned to death. Their wives beg María to intercede — the exact reversal of 1944, when no one helped her. Against Roberto's orders she goes to the President, argues that executions toppled Martínez, and wins a commutation to exile (signed 3 PM; deportation 8 PM the same day). At their 25th anniversary, the surviving Coronel Muñiz publicly thanks her for his life. [Novel]
  • ~1971: Silver wedding anniversary at the Círculo Militar. [Novel]
  • ~1974: The book closes on the 30th anniversary of Carlos's death — María at the cemetery, then tea with Roberto: "two beings born for each other." [Novel]
  • July 1987: She publishes Pugna a un dictador (1000 copies, Imprenta La Cruz del Sur), dedicating it to Carlos, José Antonio, the April 2 martyrs — and noting she remarried but "adoré a mi primer esposo y venero su recuerdo como una cosa sagrada." [Documented]

The Osorio identification [Analysis — strong]

  • The book's "nuevo Presidente": a young colonel, former classmate of Carlos, of humble but honorable birth, first in every class at the military school, who completes his term, refuses reelection, and respects the constitution. [Novel]
  • This matches Óscar Osorio (president 1950–1956) closely: humble origins, colonel, stepped down per the 1950 constitution, did not seek reelection. The 1948–50 period was a collective Junta, not a single president, so Osorio is the fit. [Documented biography vs. novel description]
  • If correct, the second husband was a senior officer under Osorio and the Muñiz/Velásquez plot dates to 1950–56. Both need Diario Oficial confirmation. [Unverified lead]

Children [Family record + Novel]

  • With Carlos Gavidia Castro: four sons — Memo, Mario, Tony, and Carlos's dad (the youngest, b. 1937). [Family record]
  • With the second husband: Elizabeth (daughter) and one son (the "sixth child"). [Novel]
  • Carlos's notes name the second-marriage children as Gloria Gavidia Ayala and Herbert Ayala. "Elizabeth" may be the book's pseudonym for Gloria — or a separate name. Unresolved. [Family record vs. Novel]
  • Naming puzzle: "Gloria Gavidia Ayala" does not follow the standard [father]-[mother] pattern for a daughter of an Ayala father and a González mother; the usage may be social/familial rather than legal. The civil registry would settle it. [Unverified lead]
  • "Robertito": the book's affectionate name for the youngest of the four Gavidia sons — i.e., Carlos's dad as a boy (~9–10 at the wedding), apparently nicknamed after his stepfather Roberto. A tender domestic detail. [Novel]

The epilogue's real names — Second Council of War [Documented — her authorial voice]

In the epilogue she drops the pseudonyms and names the real members of the council that condemned the last three executed (Gavidia Castro, Piche, Marín — i.e., the Second Council of War, April 23, 1944):

  • Auditor de Guerra: Doctor Roberto Paredes ("sanguinario y sin principios cristianos")
  • Fiscal Militar del Centro: Coronel Andrés Eulalio Castillo (opposed commuting the death sentences)
  • Procurador General Militar: Doctor y Capitán Héctor Muñoz Barillas
  • Juez Militar: Doctor Bernardo Reyes
  • Vocales propietarios: General Luis Andreu ("hombre intachable" who she notes must have acted from fear), Coronel Santiago Ayala, Coronel Joaquín Fernán
  • Suplentes: General Francisco Ponce, Coronel José Garay, Coronel Darío Flores

She also gives an eyewitness fragment of the execution morning: the condemned boarding the truck "amarrados como criminales." [Documented — firsthand]

Note: a "Coronel Santiago Ayala" sat on the 1944 council. Whether he connects to the later Ayala husband is unknown — flagged, not asserted. [Unverified lead]

The stolen dossiers [Documented — her account]

Months after the executions, the widows petitioned the Supreme Court for their husbands' case files: "Los famosos expedientes habían desaparecido, alguien se encargó de robarlos para que nadie pudiera probar tanto crimen e injusticia." This explains why the trial dossier is the great missing document — it may have been stolen in 1944, not merely lost. The AGN may hold copies or nothing at all. [Family record]

Her account of Martínez's fall and assassination [Family record]

  • The Third Council of War announcement triggered the general strike (students, doctors, lawyers, banks, commerce, schools, government employees); Martínez fled to Guatemala, then Honduras. [Documented history, consistent]
  • "Años más tarde, fue asesinado por su propio motorista. Según versiones dicen que recibió una puñalada por cada uno de los ejecutados por orden suya." Note this differs from the Calvo Marroquín family's version (the godson as assassin) — two circulating versions of the 1966 killing. [Family record — version A]
  • She closes by forgiving everyone who collaborated in the martyrs' deaths. [Documented]

Publication story (1987) [Documented, thin]

  • Pugna a un dictador, autora "Maria T. Gonzales" (cover spelling), primera edición, July 1987, 1000 copies, Imprenta La Cruz del Sur, San Salvador. Derechos reservados. [Documented — title page]
  • Dr. Salvador González Aguilar ("un buen escritor") advised the manuscript and gave her courage to publish; both he and Coronel Jorge Tenorio died before July 1987. [Documented — dedication]
  • No press coverage, reviews, or reception found online for the 1987 publication. The book survives via family copies and the UES bookstore. [Negative search]

Death — unresolved [Unverified lead]

  • No obituary or death record found online under any name variant (González, Gavidia, Ayala, Ocampo). [Negative search]
  • Born ~1917 → ~90 around 2007. Family knowledge needed: did she die in El Salvador or the US, and where is she buried? [Open]
  • Weak lead: a Dignity Memorial obituary for "Maria Teresa Gonzalez" (Nov 8, 1920 – Oct 19, 2016, Miami FL, Caballero Rivero Coral Gables). No family details listed; birth year is close but not matching; the name is extremely common. Do not assert this is her — it is a thread to check against family knowledge only. [Unverified lead]
  • Trip tasks: San Salvador civil registry death record (2000s–2010s under all surname variants); Cementerio General — look for a González/Ayala family plot near the 1954 martyrs' plaque.

Legacy [Assessment]

  • No public commemorations found: no streets, plaques, or institutional memory of her as an author in El Salvador's public record. [Negative search]
  • Her legacy is the book itself — a firsthand novelized testimony of April 1944, the only extended account by a participant's widow, preserving the execution lists, the council names, and the pseudonym-coded narrative that this research has been decoding. [Assessment]
  • The 1987 publication date matters: she wrote it as an old woman (~70), 43 years after the events, explicitly so "toda la familia querrá leerla" — it was a family memorial first, a historical document second. [Documented — her note]

Open questions for the trip / family

  • Second husband's real identity — marriage record (~1946, San Salvador) is the decisive document. [Trip]
  • "Ocampo" → Ayala: confirm via the marriage record and Diario Oficial (Osorio-era defense ministers). [Trip]
  • Her death record and burial location. [Trip / family]
  • Elizabeth vs. Gloria — one person or two? Birth records of the second-marriage children. [Trip]
  • The Muñiz/Velásquez commutation — Diario Oficial, 1950–56. [Trip]
  • Whether any of the 1000 copies reached libraries/press in 1987 — Biblioteca Nacional catalog. [Trip]

Evidence-strength summary

Second marriage to "Roberto Ocampo" [Novel] · Real husband probably Coronel ___ Ayala [Unverified lead, from Carlos's notes + author's pseudonym statement] · Osorio identification [Analysis, strong] · Muñiz/Velásquez episode [Novel] · Six children [Family record + Novel] · "Robertito" = Carlos's dad as a boy [Novel] · Second Council real names [Documented] · Stolen dossiers [Family record] · Publication facts [Documented] · Reception [Negative search] · Death [Unresolved; Miami 2016 = unverified lead only] · Public legacy [Negative search]

Mariano Castro Morán — the Book's Author

Mariano Castro Morán — research report

Researched 2026-10-06/07. Author of Relámpagos de libertad (the history book on the April 1944 uprising).

Bottom line

He was not the Gavidias' blood brother — he was a 23-year-old junior officer in 1944, a fellow conspirator of the same generation, and "mi hermano" is brother-in-arms language. But he survived, had a long distinguished career, and became the uprising's historian. His parents are unnamed in every biography found; his AGN personnel file (or his Mejicanos birth record) would settle the question of any distant Castro kinship. A likely daughter/granddaughter, Elena Castro Morán, appeared publicly in 2023 as editor of Relámpagos — a living family contact.

Biography [Documented]

  • Born: 7 October 1920, Mejicanos, San Salvador.
  • Died: 7 November 2005 (age 85).
  • Military: officer of Estado Mayor; graduated Escuela de Comando y Estado Mayor "Manuel Enrique Araujo"; Profesor Militar graduated from the Chilean Army's Escuela de Guerra.
  • January 1961: named Viceministro de Defensa Nacional.
  • 11 September 1961: named to the Directorio Cívico-Militar, replacing Coronel Julio Adalberto Rivera (resigned).
  • 1962: voluntary military retirement; began intellectual career.
  • 1977: presidential pre-candidate for the UNO (Unión Nacional Opositora).
  • Intellectual output: Función Política del Ejército Salvadoreño en el Presente Siglo (Premio Nacional de Ensayo UCA/Editores, 1983), La Guerra Prolongada que sufre El Salvador, El Impacto de las Políticas Estadounidenses en América Central, Subordinación del Poder Militar al Poder Civil en El Salvador; Relámpagos de Libertad, Tomo I (Editorial Lis, 2000); Reseña Histórica de las Administraciones Presidenciales y de la Policía Nacional de El Salvador desde 1867 hasta 1994 (2005).
  • Honors: Medalla Minerva (Chilean Army), Sable Toledano (Instituto de Cultura Hispánica), Medalla de Oro, Orden Salvador Mendieta, Honorary Academic of the Academia Salvadoreña de la Historia, Hijo Meritísimo of Mejicanos.
  • Memorialist Álvaro Darío Lara calls him "héroe de la gesta cívico-militar de 1944" — hero of the 1944 uprising.
  • Lectured at FLACSO, CENITEC, Centro DEMOS, Escuela Militar Capitán General Gerardo Barrios, Centro de Altos Estudios Estratégicos.

Sources: es.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariano_Castro_Morán; revistaculturel.com memorial by Álvaro Darío Lara; es-academic mirrors.

The "mi hermano" question [Analysis]

  • In April 1944 he was 23 — a junior officer (subteniente/teniente), the same generation as Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (b. 1915, age 29) and José Antonio (younger).
  • He survived 1944 and went on to a full military career, so he was not among the executed — though the memorial's "héroe de la gesta" label confirms he was aligned with the uprising.
  • "Mi hermano" twice applied to Antonio reads as revolutionary comradeship, the standard fraternal language of the 1944 conspirators — not a claim of blood kinship.
  • Both he and the Gavidias carry "Castro" as a maternal surname, but no source names his parents, and his origin is Mejicanos (San Salvador) while the family's Castro line is Santa Ana. A distant cousin link is possible but entirely unproven.

Elena Castro Morán [Documented — living contact, do not approach without authorization]

  • On 7 October 2023 (his 103rd birth anniversary), UCA's La Hora de Sofía (Departamento de Filosofía) aired a program on Relámpagos de libertad with guests Elena Castro Morán, editora, and writer Álvaro Darío Lara.
  • "Editora" of the book strongly suggests she is his daughter or granddaughter and the keeper of his papers.
  • Source: noticias.uca.edu.sv, "Relámpagos de libertad, de Mariano Castro Morán," 07/10/2023.

Not found / gaps

  • Parents' names: absent from all biographies. The AGN personnel file would name them — key document.
  • Exact 1944 role: rank, unit, what he did during April 2–4, whether he was detained or tried. The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier study may mention him.
  • Mejicanos birth record (7 Oct 1920): would name parents and godparents in one document.
  • Whether Relámpagos vol. 2 was ever published (only Tomo I is cited, 2000).

Trip / next-step tasks

  • AGN: his military personnel file (names parents; details 1944 service).
  • Mejicanos civil registry: birth entry 7 Oct 1920.
  • UES Librería Universitaria: buy Relámpagos de libertad vols. 1–2 (~$14.40 each) — read the "mi hermano" passages in full context.
  • UCA: contact via the La Hora de Sofía program about Elena Castro Morán (only with Carlos's approval).
  • Diario Oficial 1943–44: his rank/unit appointments.
  • Check the 1944 court-martial dossier for his name (detained? witness?).

The Marín Brothers

The Marín Cluster — April 1944

Bottom line

There were two Marín brothers in the uprising, and the book confirms it explicitly: Teniente Alfonso Marín (executed April 26, 1944) and the civilian Don Víctor Marín (captured April 11, tortured, executed). The narrative calls them "Marinelo" (pseudonym); the front matter and quoted speech use the real surname "Marín." Alfonso was Capitán Erazo's brother-in-law — married to Erazo's sister, father of a young child in 1944. A third name, "Víctor Manuel Marín," appears in documented civilian lists and is most likely Don Víctor's full name.

The three names

1. Teniente Alfonso Marín [Documented]
  • Sentenced to death April 23, 1944 by the Second Council of War, alongside Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro and Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche (author's front-matter real-name list).
  • Executed April 26, 1944.
  • Capitán Erazo's brother-in-law: Erazo's quoted words — "El Teniente Marín, es mi cuñado, esposo de mi hermana" (quoted speech → real surname per the book's method).
  • Married to Erazo's sister; they had a young child in 1944 ("también al niño, está muy grande y gracioso").
  • Full name beyond "Alfonso Marín" unknown (no second surname found).
2. Don Víctor Marín (civilian) [Documented]
  • In the author's front-matter April 11 real-name list: "Don Víctor Marín, ésta última persona fue capturada el 11 de Abril y después de ser torturado cruelmente, fue pasado por las armas cuando ya era casi un cadáver."
  • The most-tortured civilian in the narrative.
  • "Víctor Manuel Marín" appears in documented civilian uprising lists — most likely the same man's full name [Probable].
3. "Teniente Marinelo" / "Víctor Marinelo" [Family record — pseudonym]
  • The narrative's pseudonym form of "Marín" (the author adds the "-elo" suffix, consistent with her method of lightly disguising real surnames).
  • "Víctor Marinelo" (narrative, one occurrence) = Don Víctor Marín (real).
  • "Teniente Marinelo" (narrative) = Teniente Alfonso Marín (real).

Are they related? YES — brothers [Family record, stated explicitly]

The narrative states it directly (segunda-parte-1.md:601):

"el Teniente Marinelo, hermano del civil que había sido el más torturado de los civiles"

— Teniente Marinelo, brother of the civilian who had been the most tortured of the civilians.

The most-tortured civilian = Víctor Marinelo = Don Víctor Marín (real).

Therefore: Teniente Alfonso Marín and Don Víctor Marín were brothers.

The full relationship map


Marín parents (unknown)
├── Don Víctor (Manuel?) Marín — civilian, captured Apr 11, tortured, executed
└── Tte. Alfonso Marín — executed Apr 26
    └── married to Erazo's sister (name unknown)
        └── their child ("el niño," young in 1944, ~82-85 today)

Capitán Erazo (Guardia Nacional)
└── his sister = Alfonso Marín's wife
    → Erazo and Alfonso are brothers-in-law (cuñados)

Narrative arc of Teniente Marinelo (= Alfonso Marín)

From the transcription, his story in the book:

  • April 2–3: At the Cuartel Central during the fighting; the President himself phones ordering surrender; Marinelo refuses and orders heavier fire.
  • Captured and horribly tortured — arm dislocated/destroyed.
  • María visits him in detention; his wife (tall, young, dark, beautiful — unknown to María) brings their child.
  • At trial: refuses to defend himself; denounces the jury as "una jauría de lobos que quieren sangre."
  • Before execution: asks to marry by the Church ("El Teniente Marinelo también pidió casarse por la Iglesia") — parallel to Carlos's in articulo mortis wedding.
  • Executed: "Queremos morir como verdaderos hombres y militares de honor."

Note: the narrative compresses his trial/execution with the first group, but documented records place his sentencing April 23 and execution April 26 (Second Council). The book is not strictly chronological here.

Erazo's sister (Alfonso's wife)

  • Name: unknown. María did not know her; the book gives no name.
  • Described only as tall, young, dark, very beautiful.
  • She visited Alfonso in detention with their child.
  • Her fate after April 1944 is unknown.
  • Key document: Alfonso Marín's marriage record (~1930s, San Salvador civil registry) — names her, her parents, and witnesses. Trip task.

Living descendants

  • Alfonso's child ("el niño," described as big and charming in 1944) would be ~82–85 today. Possible living descendants through this line — no web trace found.
  • Víctor Marín's family status unknown — no mention of wife/children in the book.
  • No web trace of any Marín descendants from these lines. The marriage record (Alfonso) and any birth records (the child) are trip tasks at the San Salvador civil registry.

Unresolved

  • Alfonso Marín's second surname, birth date/place, military unit.
  • Víctor Marín's full name confirmation ("Víctor Manuel"?), birth details, family.
  • Erazo's sister's name and fate.
  • Erazo's own first name (still unknown).
  • Whether Víctor had a wife/children.
  • The trial dossier (AGN) would give full names, testimonies, and family details for both brothers.

Evidence labels

  • Alfonso Marín's execution (Apr 26), Víctor Marín's capture/execution (Apr 11): [Documented] (author's front-matter newspaper lists; Wikipedia; trial-dossier scholarship).
  • Erazo–Alfonso brother-in-law link: [Family record] (Erazo's quoted speech; quotes use real names per the book's method).
  • Alfonso–Víctor brothers: [Family record] (narrative states it explicitly).
  • "Marinelo" = pseudonym for "Marín": [Probable] (consistent with the book's disguise method; front matter gives real "Marín" forms).
  • "Víctor Manuel Marín" = Don Víctor Marín: [Probable] (documented civilian-list name matching the front-matter entry).
  • Living descendants: [Unverified lead] (no records found; inferred from the 1944 child's age).

Marroquín and Sosa

General Alfonso Marroquín & Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa

Research: October 7, 2026. Evidence labels: [Documented] / [Scholarship] / [Family record] / [Unverified lead].

Bottom line

Marroquín and Calvo were maternal brothers — the family is the Calvo Marroquín clan, and they rebelled as a family. Both men had also been Martínez's instruments in the 1932 Matanza repression, making their 1944 executions a devastating full circle. Sosa was the Air Force's second-in-command and a pioneer pilot; a possible living son (~85) is the best descendant lead.

General Alfonso Marroquín [Documented/Scholarship]

Family — the key discovery. Per the Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier study ("Mitos y palabras," Realidad No. 154, 2019), Marroquín's maternal brothers were Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo, Teniente Marcelino Calvo, and doctors Mario and Tomás Calvo Marroquín. The brothers carried the maternal surname Calvo; Marroquín carried the paternal one. Same mother, one clan, one rebellion — the closest parallel to the Gavidia brothers.

Role in the uprising. Marroquín was comandante primero (first commander) of the 1st Infantry Regiment — the uprising's key unit. The plan depended on his brothers persuading him to join. The prevailing account (via Castro Morán's Relámpagos) says his participation was limited to letting his brother Tito into the regiment — Tito being the actual military commander of the insurrection — after which Marroquín stayed passive. That passivity, plus his name appearing on the supposed post-coup Military Directory list, earned him the death sentence.

But the testimony complicates it. Analysis of the trial depositions shows Marroquín did far more than play prisoner: he issued key orders to subordinates throughout the first day, moved freely, and held conversations with both rebel and "neutral" officers while his own junior officers were genuinely detained. The "passive victim of his brothers" story was partly his own courtroom strategy.

The 1932 connection. Marroquín and Calvo had led the military operation in Izalco during the 1932 Matanza — they were Martínez's own instruments of repression. Twelve years later, the same man who ordered them to crush the indigenous uprising had them shot. [Scholarship — slideshare "Ama 1932" citing this history]

Execution. April 10, 1944, in the Policía Nacional courtyard, before 800+ political prisoners — alongside Calvo and Sosa. [Documented]

In the book. Fused into "General Tomás Gutiérrez, líder de la Revolución" (merged with Tito Tomás Calvo). [Family record — see pseudonym-key.md]

Mayor (Capitán Mayor) Julio Faustino Sosa [Documented]

Role. Second-in-command (segundo jefe) of the Air Force (Aviación). The plot counted on winning him over during the absence of the first chief, Mayor Juan Ramón Munés. [Scholarship — Cáceres Prendes]

Pilot. Listed among El Salvador's pioneering fighter pilots (Wikipedia, Fuerza Aérea Salvadoreña) — one of the early aviators who built the country's air arm. The Air Force's first fighter squadron organized 1936; the Caproni AP.1 fleet arrived 1939. Sosa flew in that era — the same skies as Capitán Gavidia Castro. [Documented]

Execution. April 10, 1944, Policía Nacional courtyard, with Marroquín and Calvo. [Documented]

In the book. Mapped to "Mayor Pineda" in the pseudonym key [Probable] — the officer who protested his innocence at trial, claimed he defused the bombs, was declared insane by the prison doctor, and whom Martínez ordered shot anyway ("Shoot him; they are tricks"). If the mapping holds, his final hours were among the book's most harrowing passages. [Family record]

Descendants & legacy

Sosa — living lead. A memoir in El Independiente (2024) recalls: a son of the executed major — same name, "Julio [Domingo] Sosa" — was the writer's primary-school classmate; the writer was 3 in 1944, putting the son's birth ~1941, making him ~85 today. Note the discrepancy: the memoir says "Julio Domingo Sosa" while military records say "Julio Faustino Sosa" — possibly the writer's memory error on the middle name, or a different person. Worth verifying via the son's birth record. [Unverified lead]

Marroquín/Calvo. No descendants traced online. The Calvo Marroquín brothers' story survives mainly in scholarship and the book. [Dead end]

Legacy. Marroquín and Calvo are named in Wikipedia and Salvadoran histories as the uprising's ringleaders ("los cabecillas Alfonso Marroquín y Tomás Calvo"). No memorial traced online. [Documented]

Trip tasks

  • AGN trial dossier — Marroquín's and Sosa's full depositions and the defenders' names.
  • Sosa's birth/marriage records; verify the "Julio Domingo Sosa" son lead (~1941 birth).
  • AGN personnel files for both men (parents' names, careers).
  • Diario Oficial 1932 — Marroquín's and Calvo's Izalco operation appointments.
  • Cementerio General burial records, April 10, 1944.

Marta / Coralia de Lemus

Marta = Coralia de Lemus? — Verification Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Source of claim: Ricardo Ernesto Gavidia (family historian), via Carlos, 2026-10-07.

Claim: The book's "Marta" — Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro's girlfriend before María Teresa — was really Coralia de Lemus. After breaking up with Carlos, she married his military-school classmate José María Lemus, who became president of El Salvador (1956–1960); she became First Lady.

Status: Core identifications DOCUMENTED; the Marta = Coralia equation is FAMILY TESTIMONY with a serious age problem (see below).

---

What is documented

Lemus's wife: Débora Coralia Párraga de Lemus [Documented]
  • Wikipedia (es): Lemus "contrajo nupcias con Débora Coralia Parraga de Lemus, con el cual procreó 7 hijos."
  • "Coralia de Lemus" matches Ricardo's name exactly.
  • Life dates per Joaquín Rivera Larios: 1927–2011.
Lemus: IV promoción 1933, primera antigüedad [Documented]
  • Joaquín Rivera Larios, "El intelectual que llegó a ser presidente" (joaquinlarioslibros.blogspot.com, Aug 2023): from the Escuela Militar's Cuadro de Honor histórico — of ten military presidents since 1931, only two graduated primera antigüedad: José María Lemus in 1933 and Arturo Armando Molina in 1949.
  • "Egresó cuando tenía 22 años como Primera Antiguedad de la IV Promoción de la Escuela Militar (1933)."
  • Promotions: Subteniente, 20 de octubre de 1933; Teniente 1936; Capitán 1940; Capitán Mayor 1945; Teniente Coronel 1952. Military career 1933–1952.
Carlos Gavidia Castro: same IV promoción, same graduation day [Documented — family source]
  • The Flotilla Aérea article (family-sourced via Ricardo): Carlos entered Escuela Militar Feb 13, 1931, graduated subteniente in the IV Promotion on October 20, 1933, alongside José María Lemus.
  • So: Carlos and the future president were classmates for the full 1931–33 course. Ricardo's "compañero de Carlos" is confirmed.
Lemus presidency and fall [Documented]
  • President: 14 September 1956 – 26 October 1960. Succeeded Óscar Osorio (PRUD).
  • Overthrown in the "golpe de los compadres" (Oct 26, 1960) — dragged from his home in pajamas, beaten, flown to Costa Rica from Ilopango the same morning.
  • Exile in Heredia, Costa Rica; died 31 March 1993, San José. Buried in Heredia with ceremony of a head of state — after the family refused the Cristiani government's offer of state burial honors in El Salvador.
  • Tried once to return (during Rivera's government); forced out via Frontera El Amatillo; never returned.
Lemus exiled Luis Lovo Castelar [Documented — earlier research]
  • Per the Lovo Castelar report: after a confrontation with President Lemus, Lemus sent him to Mexico on a scholarship ("beca"); when it was suspended, Lovo Castelar stayed, earned his economics doctorate (thesis 1962), and returned in 1962 after Lemus fell.
  • The circle: Lemus (Carlos's classmate) → exiled Lovo Castelar → Lovo Castelar later married Carlos's widow María Teresa (~1946). And per Ricardo, Lemus also married Carlos's ex-girlfriend.
Coralia after 1960 [Documented]
  • Exiled with Lemus to Costa Rica, October 1960.
  • Gave the interview "La verdad del golpe de los compadres" to Mayor Ernesto García — her firsthand account of the coup night (agents bursting in at dawn, Lemus beaten in pajamas, she herself grabbed by the hair and thrown to the floor by a "mayor Escamilla").
  • Donated many of Lemus's personal objects to the Museo Militar (San Salvador).
  • Died 2011 (per Rivera Larios dates 1927–2011).

---

The age problem [Unresolved — serious]

If Coralia was born in 1927:

  • Carlos courted "Marta" before his ~1937 civil marriage to María Teresa — i.e., ~1935–36, when Coralia would have been 8–9 years old.
  • The book's Marta is the "novia elegida" — the adult bride the Gavidia family wanted Carlos to marry instead of María Teresa. An 8-year-old cannot be that person.

Possible resolutions (all unverified):

  • The 1927 birth year is wrong. If Coralia was born ~1915–1920, the whole timeline works: dates Carlos ~1935, marries Lemus ~1940s, 7 children, First Lady at ~40, dies 2011 at ~95. The Rivera Larios dates may come from a misread source.
  • Ricardo's identification is wrong — "Marta" is someone else entirely.
  • The book's Marta is a composite — the name attached to a fused story.

Resolution: Coralia Párraga's birth record. Also: the Lemus–Párraga marriage record (date would show whether she was previously married — relevant to the Flores Magaña question below).

---

Tension with the book's Marta Pérez [Unresolved]

The book's established "Marta" is Marta Pérez — daughter of Don Pedro Pérez (Almacén de los Pérez), the family's chosen bride, who married Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña and was widowed April 10, 1944 (see marta-perez.md).

If Marta = Coralia Párraga:

  • The Pérez family and the Almacén would be novelized disguise (consistent with the book's proven method — but note: the book usually disguises the surname, and "Pérez" → "Párraga" is a bigger rewrite than usual).
  • The Flores Magaña marriage is either (a) novelized cover, or (b) real — Coralia widowed in 1944, remarried to Lemus. If born 1927, she was 17 in 1944 with (per the book) a newborn — biologically possible, and remarriage to Lemus (b. 1911) afterward is plausible. If born ~1917, even more comfortable.
  • No public record confirms or denies a Flores–Párraga marriage.

---

What this changes if confirmed

The circle becomes extraordinary — four lives braided:

  • Carlos Gavidia Castro (1915–1944) — dated Coralia, married María Teresa, executed.
  • Coralia Párraga — dated Carlos, married Lemus, First Lady 1956–60.
  • José María Lemus — Carlos's classmate (IV promoción 1933), president, exiled Lovo Castelar.
  • Luis Lovo Castelar — exiled by Lemus, married Carlos's widow María Teresa.

Everyone in the story knew everyone else. The book's "Marta" would be the third hidden identity cracked by family testimony (after "Roberto" = Lovo Castelar).

---

Trip checklist additions

  • Coralia Párraga birth record — settles the 1927 question definitively.
  • Lemus–Párraga marriage record — date; shows whether she was previously married (widow of Flores?).
  • Flores Magaña–[bride] marriage record (~1930s) — names the bride; the single document that decides between Marta Pérez and Coralia Párraga.
  • Museo Militar, San Salvador — the Coralia-donated Lemus objects; staff may hold the García interview or family contacts.
  • Find the full "La verdad del golpe de los compadres" interview (Mayor Ernesto García) — it may mention her early life.

---

Evidence-strength summary

Wife = Coralia Párraga, 7 children [Documented] · Lemus IV promoción 1933, primera antigüedad, graduated Oct 20 1933 [Documented — Cuadro de Honor] · Carlos same promotion and graduation day [Documented — family Flotilla article] · Lemus president 1956–60, overthrown, exiled Costa Rica, d. 1993 [Documented] · Lemus exiled Lovo Castelar to Mexico [Documented — earlier research] · Coralia's post-1960 life and 2011 death [Documented — interview + donations] · Marta = Coralia [Family testimony ONLY — age discrepancy unresolved] · Any Carlos–Coralia relationship record [Unverified]

Verdict: The scaffolding is all DOCUMENTED; the identification itself is LIKELY at best and has a real age problem. Do not publish "Marta = Coralia" as fact until the birth record lands.

Marta Pérez — the Chosen Bride

Marta Pérez — Research Report

Date: October 7, 2026

Status: Identity established from the novel; no independent documentation found yet.

---

Bottom line

[Novel] Marta Pérez was the daughter of Don Pedro Pérez (owner of "el Almacén de los Pérez," where María Teresa worked) and Doña Conchita Pérez. She was the Gavidia family's approved match for Carlos — "la novia elegida" — whom he rejected for María Teresa. She later married Capitán (Manuel) Roberto Flores Magaña, Carlos's military-school classmate, who was executed April 10, 1944 for the uprising. She vanishes from the narrative after his execution. No web trace of her, her parents, or her children has been found.

---

What the book says [Novel]

Family background
  • Don Pedro Pérez: father of Marta, owner of "el Almacén de los Pérez" — the store/warehouse where María Teresa (María) worked as a young woman.
  • Doña Conchita (Concepción) Pérez: Marta's mother; dies during the story (a blow to the family; the Gavidias are first to offer condolences).
  • The Pérez family were social peers of the Gavidias — invited to the Gavidia country outings, close enough that Carlos's mother assumed he'd marry Marta.
"La novia elegida" — the chosen bride
  • When Carlos came home radiant after María told him she was pregnant, his mother's first assumption was: "¿Te comprometes con Marta?" (Are you getting engaged to Marta?)
  • Carlos: "¡Dios me libre, esa locura no la cometería jamás!" (God forbid, I'd never commit such madness!)
  • The parents' position: marry Marta or lose the family — "tendrás que decidir, o tu familia o María."
Rivalry with María Teresa
  • At the first party, Marta danced with Carlos; María watched, convinced Marta had a boyfriend and that Carlos could never notice someone like her.
  • The New Year's Eve turning point: in the dark, Carlos kissed María believing she was Marta. When the lights came up, Marta was furious — she had "despreciado a todos los amigos, hasta a su novio para ser su pareja" (spurned all her friends, even her boyfriend, to be his partner that night).
  • Carlos's confession to María: "yo te quise desde siempre, pero yo mismo me engañaba, pensando que era a Marta a la que quería" (I loved you always, but I deceived myself thinking it was Marta I wanted).
  • Marta actively poisoned the Gavidia parents against María: "Marta Pérez se encarga de alimentarlo [el odio] al grado de que Carlos casi no llega a casa" (Marta Pérez feeds the hatred to the point Carlos barely comes home).
  • Her intrigues contributed to María being fired from the Almacén.
Marriage to Capitán Flores
  • "Por esa época Marta se casó con un militar que conoció en la última fiesta de graduación del Círculo" — she married a military man, Carlos's classmate from the first year of military school.
  • His name: Capitán (Manuel) Roberto Flores Magaña. Marta calls him "Manuel" ("¿Qué te pasa Manuel? dijo Marta") — his first name is Manuel.
  • They had children: at various points "Marta esperaba otro niño" (expecting another child); by April 1944 "tenía un niño recién nacido" (a newborn).
  • The book describes them as happy: "El esposo de Marta era magnífica persona, se veían muy felices."
  • Flores was "un militar sin tacha" (a spotless officer) who kept Carlos informed about the regime's abuses.
April 1944
  • Flores was "uno de los jefes del complot" (one of the plot's leaders), under strict government surveillance.
  • Arrested, subjected to "las torturas más crueles" alongside Paco, without betraying anyone.
  • At the trial he stood with dignity; sentenced to death with the others.
  • Executed the morning of April 10, 1944, at the Cementerio General.
  • Betina went to Marta's house that morning to prepare her; Marta "no sabía nada... estaba rezando y pidiendo a Dios, por él" — she knew nothing and was praying for him. When told, "enloqueció de dolor y angustia" (went mad with grief and anguish).
  • This is Marta's last appearance in the book. Her fate and her children's fate are not recorded.

---

What can be documented

  • [Novel] Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña is named in the book's trial/execution account (April 10, 1944, Cementerio General).
  • [Scholarship] The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier study confirms 44 death sentences from the Consejo de Guerra, 14 carried out — Flores Magaña would be among the executed, but the individual name list was not accessible.
  • [Unverified lead] No independent web record found for: Marta Pérez, Don Pedro Pérez / Almacén de los Pérez, Doña Conchita Pérez, or the Flores-Pérez children.

---

Dots connected

  • Marta is the hinge between the book's romance plot and its tragedy: the "acceptable" bride Carlos rejected became the widow of his executed comrade. Both women — María Teresa and Marta — lost their husbands to the same firing squads in April 1944.
  • Flores Magaña and Carlos were classmates from year one; Flores fed Carlos inside information about the regime. His execution alongside the Gavidia brothers binds the two families' fates.
  • The Pérez family (Almacén) employed María Teresa — meaning the "rival" and the heroine knew each other as employer/employee before becoming romantic rivals.

---

Open questions / trip tasks

  • Marta Pérez + Roberto Flores Magaña marriage record (~early 1930s?) — would give her full name, parents' confirmation, witnesses.
  • Birth records of the Flores-Pérez children (San Salvador civil registry) — the newborn of 1944 would be ~82 today; possible living descendants.
  • Flores Magaña's military personnel file at the Archivo General de la Nación.
  • The 1944 trial dossier — his testimony and full name.
  • The Pérez family / Almacén de los Pérez — business or property records in San Salvador.
  • Whether "Manuel" was his legal first name (Manuel Roberto Flores Magaña?) or a nickname — the marriage record would settle it.

The Muñiz/Velásquez Coup

The Muñiz/Velásquez Coup — Identification Attempt

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: No positive identification found after exhaustive public-web searching. Honest negative result with analysis.

What the book says [Novel]

From Part 3 (tercera-parte-1.md, pp. 161–166):

  • During the presidency matching Óscar Osorio (1950–56), while "Roberto" (Ocampo/Ayala) serves as Minister of War, a small group of discontented officers — some retired, some transferred to remote posts — conspires against the government.
  • The plot is discovered and suppressed; arrests are made in time.
  • Two Colonels, Muñiz and Velásquez, are identified as the ringleaders ("los cabecillas"). An extraordinary Council of War condemns both to death; ten other officers are to be deported.
  • Their wives beg María Teresa to intercede. Against her husband's explicit prohibition, she goes to the Presidential House, speaks directly with the President, and argues that executions destroyed Hernández Martínez — the same would happen to this government.
  • The President promises to reconsider; the death sentences are commuted to exile ("destierro"). The two colonels are deported that same night. A newspaper extra announces the pardon; the city praises the President's kindness.
  • Decades later, Coronel Muñiz returns and speaks at the Ocampos' silver anniversary (~1971), publicly thanking María Teresa for his life.

Search results: negative

Exhaustive web searching found no record of:

  • Any failed officers' coup against Óscar Osorio (1950–56) in the general histories. The documented failed coups of the century are: 1927 (Romero Bosque), 1932, April 1944 (Martínez), June 1945 (Castaneda Castro), 1972 (Sánchez Hernández), 1980, 1981, 1989. No failed attempt against Osorio appears in the standard lists (hispanopedia "Golpes de Estado en El Salvador," Wikipedia "Dictadura militar en El Salvador").
  • Any Salvadoran military officer named Muñiz (or Muñoz) involved in a 1950s conspiracy or coup.
  • Any Salvadoran Coronel Velásquez involved in a 1950s conspiracy or coup.
  • The pair "Muñiz" + "Velásquez" together in any military/political context.

What IS documented: the Osorio commutation pattern [Documented]

Revista Elementos ("Dos mujeres contra la pena de muerte," citing historian Carlos Wilfredo Moreno's thesis Criminalidad y pena de muerte en El Salvador del siglo XX):

  • During Osorio's presidency (1950–56), every death sentence was saved at the last hour — officially attributed to "technical problems" (e.g., the firing squad failing to assemble). Judges denounced executive interference in judicial matters.
  • Between 1953 and 1983, of 33 condemned to death, only 3 were actually executed — an abolitionist phase in practice.

This makes the book's commutation episode plausible in its setting: Osorio demonstrably refused to carry out executions. The author may have taken a real pattern (or a real small incident) and dramatized it.

Why the specific event may not be findable

  • Scale. The book itself calls it "un pequeño grupo" — a small conspiracy discovered before it acted. Minor foiled plots often leave no trace in general histories; they surface only in Diario Oficial decrees, contemporary newspapers, or military archives.
  • Novelization. Part 3 is already known to novelize: the "Minister of War" claim is contradicted (documented Osorio defense ministers were Óscar Bolaños 1950–53 and José María Lemus's predecessor structure — no Ayala). The author wrote 40 years after the fact and freely elevated positions and compressed timelines.
  • The quoted-speech rule may not hold for Part 3. The "real names in quoted speech" method was derived from Part 1 (1944), where it could be cross-checked against printed execution lists. Part 3 has no such Rosetta Stone. "Muñiz" and "Velásquez" may be pseudonyms despite appearing in quoted speech — or lightly altered versions of real names.
  • Displacement. The episode's moral structure (María reversing 1944 by saving men from the firing squad) is the thematic climax of Part 3. The author may have displaced or composited events for narrative effect.
  • Wrong president. If the "President" is a composite, the real event could belong to another era entirely (Lemus 1956–60, or later). Note the 1972 parallel: Coronel Benjamín Mejía's failed coup against Sánchez Hernández ended with José Napoleón Duarte condemned to death for treason and then exiled to Venezuela — the same death-to-exile pattern. But the book's chronology (silver anniversary ~1971, closing ~1974) rules out 1972 as the depicted event.

What would resolve it

  • Diario Oficial 1950–56 — search for deportation/exile decrees naming military officers; the book says ten officers were deported the same night.
  • Contemporary newspapers (El Diario de Hoy, La Prensa Gráfica, Diario Latino) — the book mentions a newspaper "extra" announcing the pardon; the event would have been covered.
  • AGN military files — personnel records for any Coronel Muñiz/Muñoz or Coronel Velásquez active 1950–56.
  • Family knowledge — Ricardo Gavidia or the cousins may know whether María Teresa ever spoke of this episode as real.

Bottom line

The Muñiz/Velásquez coup, as described, has no documented correlate in the public historical record. The most likely explanations, in order: (a) a real but minor foiled conspiracy, too small for the general histories, recoverable only from Diario Oficial/newspapers/AGN; (b) a novelized composite built on Osorio's documented refusal to carry out executions; (c) names changed despite the quoted-speech rule. The episode should be treated as [Novel] with a plausible historical setting, not as a documented event, until archival evidence surfaces.

Evidence-strength summary

Episode narrative [Novel] · No failed Osorio coup in general histories [Documented absence] · No Muñiz/Velásquez officers traceable [Documented absence] · Osorio-era death sentences systematically commuted [Documented] · Part 3 novelization (Minister claim contradicted) [Documented] · 1972 death-to-exile parallel [Documented, chronology excludes it]

Paco — the 'Teniente Romo' Identification

Paco (Teniente Romo) & Doctor Velásquez — Identity Research

TARGET 1: "Teniente Romo," called Paco

What the book says [Family record / Novel]

Identity markers:

  • A Gavidia brother: "el hermano de José," son of Doña Carmela (Carlota), brother of Carlos and Betina. (primera-parte-1.md:282; primera-parte-2.md:562, 681)
  • Young: "a pesar de su juventud," "el niño bueno, dulce y comprensivo de la familia Romo." (primera-parte-2.md:1218; segunda-parte-1.md:182)
  • Entered the Escuela Militar; graduated alongside Roberto Flores Magaña. María was to place Roberto's officer star at graduation. (primera-parte-1.md:660; primera-parte-2.md:526–565)
  • Doña Carmela told President Martínez he "no trae para militar" — the family planned to send him to Europe for Engineering, Architecture, or Agriculture. (primera-parte-2.md:641–642)
  • He was studying Engineering; gave up the Europe trip after his mother's death to stay near his father and brothers. (primera-parte-2.md:695)
  • Dark-haired ("moreno"), quiet, very kind, cultured, educated, few words. (primera-parte-1.md:290; primera-parte-2.md:960)

In the plot:

  • Among the first to enlist when he learned of the conspiracy; assigned to Group 1 with Capitán Flores despite his youth. (primera-parte-2.md:1070, 1218)
  • At the Cuartel Central during the fighting; designated with Flores and Teniente López as parliamentary envoys to Cuartel Zunzal; captured and jailed (López freed to report). The book then calls him "el Teniente Romo." (primera-parte-2.md:1267, 1278)
  • Tortured; brought bound into the Council of War. (segunda-parte-1.md:142–147)

His trial testimony (key passage):

> "No me considero militar por que nunca ejercí la carrera ni estuve de alta en ningún Cuartel... mis padres ayudaron con dinero para que el General lanzara su candidatura; cuando estuvo de baja fueron mis padres los que le brindaron casa y alimentación a él y a su familia... acuso al Presidente de la República de la muerte de mi madre, de la muerte de mi padre, del destierro de mi tío y de la persecución de mi hermano."

>

> ("I don't consider myself military because I never practiced the career nor was I on active duty in any barracks... my parents helped with money so the General could launch his candidacy; when he was out [of power] my parents gave him house and food, for him and his family... I accuse the President of the Republic of the death of my mother, the death of my father, the exile of my uncle, and the persecution of my brother.")

> (segunda-parte-1.md:182–188)

The four accusations map exactly: mother's death = Doña Carmela (died calling for her exiled brother, denied a final visit); father's death = Don José; uncle's exile = Don Juan (José Cipriano Castro Bernal, exiled 1938); brother's persecution = Carlos (discharged from aviation, 1939–42).

Death: Sentenced to death; executed April 10, 1944, at the Cementerio General firing wall ("entre ellos Paco Romo, el joven bueno, gallardo y leal"). A novenario (ninth-day memorial mass) was held for him. (segunda-parte-1.md:188–311)

Cross-reference against the brothers

| Brother | Rank | Death | Fits Paco? |

|---|---|---|---|

| José Antonio ("Rey Antonio") | Teniente | Executed April 10, 1944 | YES — date, rank, age all match |

| Juan Carlos | (rank unconfirmed) | Executed April 26, 1944 | NO — wrong date |

| Ricardo | — | Survived (doctor, UES dean) | NO |

| Rodolfo Eduardo | — | No trace; no death record | Unlikely — a 1944 execution would leave a record |

The author's front-matter real-name list gives "Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro" executed April 10 at the cemetery — the official form of José Antonio's name (family: "José Antonio" / "Rey Antonio").

Reconciling "nunca ejercí la carrera"

Paco's claim that he "never practiced the career nor was on active duty" is consistent with José Antonio if he graduated from the Escuela Militar but never took an active posting — instead studying Engineering (as the novel states) and planning a European career. He held the rank of Teniente by graduation but was never "de alta" (on the active rolls). The "Teniente Romo" title in the narrative may reflect his graduation rank or a revolutionary brevet.

Conclusion

Paco / "Teniente Romo" = José Antonio ("Rey Antonio") Gavidia Castro. Confidence: [Probable], borderline [Confirmed].

The April 10 execution date is the decisive discriminator — it eliminates Juan Carlos (April 26) and Ricardo (survived). The rank (Teniente), youth, and family position all align. The only soft point is the "never served" testimony, which is reconcilable as above but deserves a check against his AGN personnel file or the trial dossier.

Trip task: José Antonio's AGN military personnel file (would confirm commission status and active-duty record); his baptism record (full legal name — "José Antonio" vs "Antonio").

---

TARGET 2: Doctor Velásquez

What the book says [Family record / Novel]
  • "El Doctor Velásquez que era el Abogado más brillante de la época empezó la defensa, con énfasis e indignación." ("Doctor Velásquez, who was the most brilliant lawyer of the era, began the defense, with emphasis and indignation.") (segunda-parte-1.md:576)
  • He led the defense across multiple councils — for the enlisted men, the officers, and the civilians. His speeches are quoted at length: principled, emotional, directly confronting the Fiscal and the jury. (segunda-parte-1.md:576–692)
  • Described as "persona de gran talento y conocida honorabilidad" ("a person of great talent and recognized honor"). (segunda-parte-1.md:692)
  • The author's dedication honors "los honorables Abogados, que desafiando la cólera del Presidente, con sus argumentos de defensa, lucharon hasta el último momento." (front-matter.md:30)
Complication: two Velásquezes?
  • Doctor Velásquez — the defense lawyer (above).
  • Coronel Velásquez — condemned to death: María says "me acabo de enterar que condenaron a muerte al Coronel Muñiz y al Coronel Velásquez" (tercera-parte-1.md:875); "Las señoras de Muñiz y de Velásquez se despidieron de María" (tercera-parte-1.md:824).
  • Line segunda-parte-1.md:690 reads "Por último el Doctor Velásquez y Velásquez comprendió..." — most likely a transcription duplication, but it raises the possibility the author distinguished two men.

Relationship between the Doctor and the Coronel (brothers? father/son? coincidence?) is unknown.

Is "Velásquez" real or a pseudonym?

Per the book's method, quoted speech preserves real names. "Coronel Velásquez" appears in María's direct quoted speech, which suggests Velásquez is a real surname [Probable]. The defense lawyers are neither Council members nor protagonists, so they fall outside the disclaimer — but the author may still have protected them. No first name is given anywhere in the book.

Web search results

No identification found. Searches for prominent Salvadoran lawyers named Velásquez (1940s), "Coronel Velásquez" executed 1944, and the defense counsel of the April 1944 councils returned no match. The Cáceres Prendes trial-dossier article ("Mitos y palabras," Realidad No. 154, 2019) analyzes the expediente and may name the appointed defenders, but its PDF was not accessible online.

Conclusion

Doctor Velásquez: [Unresolved]. The surname is likely real; the first name, biography, and fate are unknown. He may be related to the condemned Coronel Velásquez.

Best next documents (trip tasks):

  • The trial dossier at the AGN (certified copy confirmed to exist) — names all appointed defense counsel.
  • Diario Oficial, April–May 1944 — council appointments and defender designations.
  • Identify "Coronel Velásquez" first (military records) — may lead to the Doctor through family linkage.
  • UES law faculty archives — "the most brilliant lawyer of the era" likely taught or graduated there.

The Pseudonym Key — Full Research File

Pseudonym Key — Pugna a un dictador (María Teresa González, 1987)

The author's disclaimer (quoted verbatim from NOTA DE LA AUTORA)

> "En este relato, todos los nombres de los que integraron el fatídico Consejo de Guerra, están cambiados, lo mismo que el de todos los protagonistas de mi Historia; pues aunque los lectores no lo crean y aunque después de viuda me haya vuelto a casar, deseo explicar que adoré a mi primer esposo y que venero su recuerdo, como una cosa sagrada."

Translation: "In this account, all the names of those who made up the fateful Council of War are changed, as are those of all the protagonists of my Story."

Methodology notes

  • Two name regimes. The front matter (NOTA DE LA AUTORA) uses REAL names — it prints newspaper execution lists verbatim. The narrative uses pseudonyms. Direct quoted speech sometimes preserves real names (e.g., Erazo's quoted words say "Teniente Marín" and "Capitán Gavidia Castro") while the surrounding narrative says "Teniente Marinelo" and "Capitán Romo."
  • "Romo" = Gavidia. Confirmed textually (p. 77: Mayor Ordóñez addresses "Capitán Romo"; the Romo family hosts the New Year dinner; Betina presents Carlos as "mi hermano").
  • Rank changes. The book sometimes promotes/demotes: "General Tomás Gutiérrez" fuses a real General (Marroquín) with a real Coronel named Tomás (Calvo). Ranks in the book are unreliable; first names + dates are the anchors.
  • Confidence levels: [Confirmed] = textual proof or family knowledge; [Probable] = strong cross-reference; [Unresolved] = cannot determine.

Names from the front-matter newspaper lists (REAL — printed verbatim)

Executed April 10, 1944 — Policía Nacional: General Alfonso Marroquín, Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo, Mayor Julio Faustino Sosa.

Executed April 10, 1944 — Cementerio General: Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro, Teniente Marcelino Calvo, Teniente Miguel Angel Linares, Teniente Ricardo Mancía González, Teniente Edgardo Chacón, Teniente Oscar Armando Cristales.

Sentenced April 11, 1944: Coronel Alfredo Aguilar, Capitán Héctor Montalvo, Capitán Guillermo Castellanos Fuentes, Capitán Fidel Issusi, Teniente Benjamín Rodríguez, Teniente Daniel Cañas Infante, Teniente Ricardo Lemus Rivas, Teniente Héctor Castaneda Dueñas, Alumno Aviador Enrique Aberle, Doctor Arturo Romero, Don Agustín Alfaro Morán, Doctor Francisco Guillermo Pérez, Doctor Mario Calvo, Doctor Tomás Calvo, Don Víctor Marín.

Second Council of War, April 23, 1944 (executed Apr 24/26): Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro, Capitán Carlos Francisco Piche, Teniente Alfonso Marín.

Saved (first Council, escaped): Capitán Fernando Carmona Dárdano, Capitán Darío Vega Duarte, Teniente Belisario Peña, Teniente Salvador Crespo, Teniente Julio Adalberto Rivera, Teniente Daniel Guevara Paiz, Teniente Salguero.

Notable internal puzzles (flagged, not resolved)

  • Flores Magaña's absence from her own newspaper list. The book narrative says he was executed the morning of April 10, but the front-matter list (which she says came "from the newspaper") does not name him. Possible: incomplete newspaper list, or he was listed under a different name/rank.
  • "Capitán Roberto Romo" (1 mention). Likely a slip conflating Roberto (Flores) with Romo (Gavidia) — or a real composite. Flagged.
  • The "three brothers" device. The book presents Marroquín/Calvo/Sosa as "three brothers" (the Gutiérrezes). The real men were not brothers. This appears to be a literary compression — treat the fraternity as fictional, the executions as real.
  • "Mi querido cuñado" (dedication). "My dear brother-in-law, to whom I kept special affection" — past tense, deceased. Likely José Antonio or Juan Carlos Gavidia Castro (both executed 1944). Unresolved which.

Top unresolved names (future dig targets)

  • Doctor Velásquez (the brilliant defense lawyer)
  • Coronel Palma (penitentiary director)
  • Coronel Muñiz (captured ringleader)
  • Doctor Córdova ("líder de la revolución")
  • Doña Delia's real name (Flores Magaña's mother)
  • Which Gavidia brother is "Paco"; which sister is "Betina"
  • Capitán Rodríguez / Doña Angela
  • Teniente López

Remaining Names

Remaining Names — Pseudonym Key Resolutions

Research into six unidentified names from "Pugna a un dictador" (1987), using the pseudonym-key method (quoted speech = real names; narrative = changed names; author's front-matter lists = real execution names).

Results Table

| Book name | Real person (or Unresolved) | Evidence | Confidence |

|---|---|---|---|

| "Betina" | Alba Marina Gavidia Castro | Paco's (José Antonio's) sister per "Betina ignoraba la condena de su hermano." Husband named "Mario" in the book; two children (boy and girl). Estela married a Grabowski, so by elimination Betina = Alba Marina. Brave, take-charge personality; comforted Marta after Flores's execution. | [Probable] |

| "Doña Delia" | Unresolved — Flores Magaña's mother | Consistently "la madre de Roberto" (Roberto Flores). No surname ever given in the book. She is a protagonist, so "Delia" is likely a changed first name. Needs Flores Magaña's birth record (~1910s) or his marriage record (~early 1930s). | [Unresolved] |

| "Coronel Palma" | Unresolved — Director of the Penitenciaría Central, 1944 | Sympathetic figure who allowed the in articulo mortis weddings without asking superiors. Described as "persona de muy buenos sentimientos y reconocida honorabilidad." No web trace of a 1944 prison director by any name. Protagonist → name likely changed. Needs Diario Oficial 1944 appointments or AGN records. | [Unresolved] |

| "Coronel Muñiz" | Unresolved — later-period figure, NOT 1944 | Critical correction: Muñiz appears in the THIRD part (María's later life with Roberto Ocampo), not the 1944 narrative. His wife begs María to intercede; María gets his death sentence commuted to exile. He survives and reappears at celebrations. The "President" María addresses is post-Martínez ("la caída del Presidente Hernández Martínez fue por los fusilamientos"). Likely a 1960s figure with changed name. | [Unresolved] |

| "Doctor Córdova" | Doctor Arturo Romero | Book: "El Doctor Córdova, líder de la revolución era persona querida y estimada por la gente pobre, pues en ese sector él hacía caridades como Médico." Documented: Dr. Arturo Romero, physician at Hospital Nacional Rosales, "dirigente opositor... de gran arraigo popular," LED the April 2, 1944 uprising alongside Tito Calvo and Julio Faustino Sosa. Sentenced to death April 11 (named in the author's own front-matter real-name list). Doctor + revolutionary leader + beloved by the poor = exact match. | [Confirmed] |

| "Ana Mercedes" | Unresolved — Flores Magaña's cousin | "Acaba de salir graduada" (just graduated), introduced by Doña Delia as Roberto's cousin ("tu prima"). Single mention at a pre-1944 social gathering. No surname given. Needs Flores family records. | [Unresolved] |

Key Correction: Coronel Muñiz Is Not a 1944 Figure

The task brief described Muñiz as "a captured ringleader" of 1944, but the text places him firmly in the third part — María's second marriage era. The timeline: his wife begs María for help, María intercedes with a post-Martínez president (addressed as "Coronel"), the sentence is commuted to deportation, and Muñiz later speaks at a celebration. This is a different political crisis, likely 1960s. Do not conflate with 1944.

Betina Identification — Reasoning

  • "Betina ignoraba la condena de su hermano" (re Paco) → she is José Antonio's sister.
  • Husband called "Mario"; two children.
  • Real sisters: Alba Marina (husband unknown) and Estela (married a Grabowski).
  • "Mario" ≠ Grabowski → Betina is not Estela → Betina = Alba Marina.
  • Caveat: "Mario" could itself be a changed name, but the elimination logic holds unless Estela's husband was named Mario Grabowski (unlikely).

Doctor Córdova = Arturo Romero — Why Confirmed

Four independent points converge:

  • Profession: both physicians.
  • Role: both described as leader of the revolution/uprising.
  • Popular base: book says "querida y estimada por la gente pobre... hacía caridades como Médico"; documented sources say "de gran arraigo popular."
  • The author's front matter names the REAL "Doctor Arturo Romero" in the April 11 death-sentence list — while the narrative calls him "Córdova." This is exactly the pseudonym pattern.

Trip Tasks

  • Flores Magaña's birth record (~1910s) → mother's real name (Doña Delia).
  • Flores Magaña's marriage record (~early 1930s) → confirms mother's name, Marta's identity.
  • Diario Oficial 1944 → Penitenciaría Central director (Coronel Palma's real identity).
  • Alba Marina Gavidia Castro's marriage record → husband's name (confirms Betina = Alba Marina).
  • AGN personnel files → Coronel Palma, Doctor Velásquez.

Research Report

Gavidia Family History — website content brief

Compiled 2026-10-04 by Muse for the family website build. Every fact below is

verified from family notes (Ricardo Gavidia via Carlos) and live source checks

done 2026-10-04. Do NOT invent additional facts, dates, or people. Anything not

listed here must be shown on the site as a blank / open question ("Can you help?").

Privacy rule: living persons appear by name and relationship ONLY — no birth

dates, no places, no addresses, no ID numbers.

Family tree (dad's side)

  • José Gavidia + Carlota Castro de Gavidia — great-grandparents, Santa Ana.

Ten children; the second was Carlos.

  • Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (1915–1944) + María Teresa González (grandmother).
  • Their four children: Carlos Gavidia II, Memo Gavidia, Mario Gavidia,

Tony Gavidia (4th child; Ricardo Gavidia's father).

  • María Teresa González's second marriage (husband's first name unknown, surname Ayala):
  • Gloria Gavidia Ayala — sister of Carlos Gavidia II, i.e. Carlos's aunt.

(She is NOT Carlos's mother; the shared first name is a coincidence.)

  • Herbert Ayala.
  • Carlos Gavidia II + Gloria Gavidia (Carlos's mom): their children Carlos Gavidia III

and Richard Gavidia. (Show names + relationships only.)

Timeline — Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro

  • Nov 24, 1915 — born in Santa Ana, second of ten children.
  • Feb 13, 1931 — enters the Escuela Militar as a cadet at age 15.
  • Oct 20, 1933 — graduates subteniente (IV Promoción).
  • Mid-1936 — selected for the Aviation School at Ilopango; promoted teniente Sep 29, 1936.
  • Jan 5, 1937 — earns his military pilot title; marries María Teresa González (1937).
  • Aircraft flown: WACO CTO, Curtiss-Wright CW-14 "Osprey", Caproni AP-1

(later also Fairchild PT-19 and Vultee BT-13A).

  • Second half of 1939 — pushed out of military aviation amid regime suspicion;

manages Haciendas El Ángel and El Carmen (San Juan Opico / San Pablo Tacachico,

La Libertad).

  • Late 1942 — rejoins military aviation.
  • Nov 12, 1943 — promoted to captain.
  • Apr 2, 1944 — joins the uprising against President Maximiliano Hernández Martínez

after his brother José Antonio convinces him; blocked from reaching Ilopango,

fights with rebel troops at Candelaria and Cuartel San Carlos, retreats west

toward Santa Ana.

  • Apr 10, 1944 — his younger brother Tte. José Antonio Gavidia Castro is executed

by firing squad with ten others.

  • Apr 22, 1944 — Consejo de Guerra Extraordinario sentences Carlos to death.
  • Apr 23, 1944 — writes his farewell letter to María Teresa ("cuida a nuestros

hijos, los tres y el que viene" — she is three months pregnant with their fourth child).

  • Apr 24, 1944, 06:30 — executed by firing squad at the Cementerio General.
  • May 9, 1944 — the Hernández Martínez regime ("Martinato") falls.
  • His personal effects are on permanent display at the Museo Nacional de Aviación

in Ilopango, donated by the Gavidia family.

  • Uncle: José Cipriano Castro Bernal (1895–1958), Santa Ana conservative, exiled to

Guatemala in 1938 after protesting Martínez's re-election.

María Teresa González (grandmother)

  • Author of the book "Pugna a un dictador" (1987). No online or audio copy has been found.
  • Parents, birth date, and birthplace: UNKNOWN (blank).
  • Second husband's first name: UNKNOWN (blank).

The Gavidia surname

  • Possibly Valencian Spanish, an adaptation of the Basque name Gabiria (a place in

Gipuzkoa) — uncertain.

  • Spanish marriage records with the name go back to the 1600s (e.g., Granada area).
  • Only ~435 bearers in Spain today; El Salvador has the highest density worldwide.
  • The poet Francisco Gavidia (1863–1955, San Miguel) is the best-known Salvadoran

Gavidia; no family link found.

Blanks — show these on the site as "Can you help?" open questions

  • José Antonio's surname order: "Castro Gavidia" vs "Gavidia Castro" (the Flotilla

Aérea article uses both).

  • Carlos's birthplace: Santa Ana (article) vs San Salvador (family memory).
  • María Teresa: parents, birth date, birthplace; second husband's first name.
  • José Gavidia + Carlota Castro: no records found yet for them or any property they owned.
  • A copy of "Pugna a un dictador" — ask Ricardo, Gloria, or Herbert.

Properties section

  • Lot near Residencial Atami, La Libertad: sold by Gloria Gavidia (mom). The family

is reviewing the transaction. Keep this entry FACTUAL AND MINIMAL on the public

page — no accusations, no buyer names, no prices. ("Family reviewing the sale.")

  • Unclaimed assets: placeholder section. Weekly research will check unclaimed-property

registries (US states and El Salvador) for family names and report here.
