The captain's siblings and their descendant lines

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: Several major finds; some siblings still blank

Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro (1915–1944) was one of 10 children of José Gavidia and Carlota Castro de Gavidia of Santa Ana. The 1936 25th-anniversary photo caption names 8 of 10: Juan José (eldest), Rey Antonio (= José Antonio), Alba Marina, Juan Carlos, Estela (Stella Antonia), Dagoberto, Rodolfo Eduardo, Ricardo — plus the captain himself. One sibling remains unnamed.

---

1. Juan José Gavidia Castro (eldest brother) — BEST-DOCUMENTED LINE

Born ~1912 (104 at death), died September 19, 2016. [Documented]

Diario Co Latino inheritance edicts (notary René Alirio Ayala, published Aug 24 and Oct 4, 2019) establish:

  • Died September 19, 2016, at home: Colonia Miramonte, Calle Cerro Verde #34, San Salvador
  • Age 104; DUI 01870574-5; native of Santa Ana, resident of San Salvador
  • His grandson Francisco José Gavidia Ayala accepted the intestate inheritance as "nieto sobreviviente" (surviving grandson), first interim (Aug 2019) then definitive (Oct 2019)

Living descendant identified: Francisco José Gavidia Ayala — presumably in San Salvador. A man who lived to 104 in the internet era may also have obituaries/social traces under his own name.

Sources:

  • https://www.yumpu.com/es/document/view/62792881/edicion-24-de-agosto-2019/11
  • https://www.yumpu.com/es/document/view/62854460/edicion-04-de-octubre-2019/17

2. José Antonio ("Rey Antonio") Gavidia Castro — executed April 10, 1944

Teniente, executed with the April 1944 conspirators. Multiple histories confirm the date and rank (rank still contested: teniente vs subteniente — see open questions). No record found of a wife or children; he was young (~20s) in 1944. [Documented death; no descendants found]

3. Juan Carlos Gavidia Castro — executed April 26, 1944

Executed alongside the captain. No record found of a wife or children. [Documented death; no descendants found]

4. Estela (Stella Antonia) Gavidia Castro de Grabowski — PIONEERING PHYSICIAN

In 1945 she became the first woman to earn a doctorate from the Universidad de El Salvador. [Documented]

Reported in an academic survey of women in Latin American medicine (EnFoco, FFyB-UBA): "Recién en 1945 se doctoró la primera mujer en la Universidad de El Salvador, Stela Gavidia Castro de Grabowski."

She married a Grabowski (first name unknown). As a pioneering woman doctor she likely has a professional footprint: UES archives, medical college records, possibly a named ward/prize. Her descendants (if any) would carry the Grabowski surname.

Source: https://enfoco.ffyb.uba.ar/mujeres-medicas-desde-el-rio-bravo-mas-abajo-y-tambien-a-la-derecha-del-mapa/

5. Dagoberto Gavidia Castro — DIPLOMAT

Consul General of El Salvador in San Francisco, May 1944. [Documented]

San Bernardino Sun, May 17, 1944 (via Geneanet newspaper index): "Dagoberto Gavidia said today he had withdrawn his resignation as consul general of El Salvador."

Timing is poignant: his brothers José Antonio and Juan Carlos were executed April 10 and 26, 1944 — his resignation (then withdrawal) came weeks later. Worth pulling the full article.

Son identified: Carlos Dagoberto Gavidia Ventura — "licenciado," appears as legal counsel (apoderado) in Salvadoran court records in the 2000s (Supreme Court civil chamber, 2000; criminal court testimony, 2004). The maternal surname Ventura. Likely born ~1940s–50s; may still be practicing.

Possible second trace: "Arq. Dagoberto Gavidia" credited as the architect for the Farmacia Principal historic-building reconstruction in San Salvador (1994–1997, per HMdb marker). If the consul was born ~1915, he'd be ~80 in 1994 — so this is more likely a son or nephew namesake, another living-line lead.

Sources:

  • https://es.geneanet.org/fonds/bibliotheque/?go=1&nom=GAVIDIA&size=50 (San Bernardino Sun 1944/05/17)
  • https://www.jurisprudencia.gob.sv/DocumentosBoveda/D/1/2000-2009/2000/03/1F330.PDF
  • https://www.hmdb.org/m.asp?m=94701

6. Ricardo Gavidia Castro — SCIENTIST, UES DEAN

Well-documented public figure, very likely the sibling ("the boy Ricardo" in the 1936 photo — a 1957 doctorate fits a ~1930 birth): [Documented]

  • Doctor in Biological Chemistry (Química Biológica), UES, 1957 — thesis: "La electrofóresis sobre papel como método para la identificación de las variantes hemoglobínicas" (with Max Bloch). UES library catalog: https://catalogosiidca.csuca.org/Record/UES.68255/Details
  • Dean (decano) of the UES — in September 1969 he personally handed Armando Bukele Kattán his chemistry doctorate diploma (El Diario de Hoy photo, via revistaelementos.net)
  • UES Normalization Commission, 1972–73 — one of five members named after the 1972 military intervention (with Mario Alberto Fernández, Efraín Amaya, Francisco José Retana, Juan José Fernández)

As a dean and public academic, his personnel file, obituary, and descendants should be traceable via UES archives.

7. Alba Marina Gavidia Castro — NO TRACE FOUND

No obituaries, records, or mentions located online. [Dead end online]

8. Rodolfo Eduardo Gavidia Castro — NO TRACE FOUND

No obituaries, records, or mentions located online. [Dead end online]

9. Tenth sibling — UNNAMED, UNKNOWN

The 1936 caption names only 8 of 10 children (+ the captain = 9 accounted for). One sibling's name is missing from family records. [Open]

10. The captain's own line (for the tree)

Four sons: Carlos (dad), Memo, Mario, Tony. Tony's son Ricardo Ernesto Gavidia holds the family albums and the captain's original farewell letter. [Family record — confirm details with Carlos]

---

The Xalapa connection: Castro Guevara family

Carlo Antonio Castro Guevara (1926–2010) — major find, connection to our Castros still unproven: [Documented person; family link unverified]

  • Born July 18, 1926, in Santa Ana, El Salvador
  • Father: "político cercano al revolucionario nicaragüense Augusto César Sandino"; seven children with classical names (Numa Pompilio, Marco Tulio, Alejandro Magno…)
  • With Hernández Martínez's rise, the family fled El Salvador when Carlo was 12 (~1938); Mexico took them in
  • Became a renowned anthropologist, writer, translator, professor at the Universidad Veracruzana in Xalapa; Premio Chiapas 1988; honorary doctorate UV 2004; died April 11, 2010, in Xalapa; family donated part of his library to the UV in 2015
  • Wife: Carmen Vargas Delgadillo (a teacher)

The José Cipriano question: José Cipriano Castro Bernal (1895–1958) was exiled in 1938 — the same year the Castro Guevara family fled. Suggestive timing, BUT: (a) José Cipriano was exiled to Guatemala, the Castro Guevaras went to Mexico; (b) no document names Carlo's father; (c) "Castro Guevara" implies a mother surnamed Guevara, and nothing ties her to José Cipriano. The two Castros could be brothers/cousins rather than father/son — or unrelated. Do not assert a link.

Sources:

  • https://12horasdeveracruz.com/carlo-antonio-castro-guevara-1926-2010-un-centenario-entre-la-memoria-y-el-olvido/
  • http://www.antropologuitosuv.com/article-el-ultimo-banquete-con-carlo-antonio-castro-guevara-48740094.html

---

Bottom line

Most worth pursuing (living contacts / photo holders):

  • Francisco José Gavidia Ayala (Juan José's grandson, San Salvador) — the single most promising living contact; his grandfather lived to 104 and the family likely holds photos and stories.
  • Carlos Dagoberto Gavidia Ventura (lawyer, San Salvador) — Dagoberto's line; findable via the bar association/court records.
  • Ricardo Ernesto Gavidia (already known to Carlos) — holds the albums and the farewell letter; the known photo source.
  • Grabowski descendants (Estela's line) — via UES/medical college records.
  • UES archives for Ricardo (dean 1969, commission 1972–73) — personnel file may name spouse/children.
  • Castro Guevara family in Xalapa — via the Universidad Veracruzana (they donated his library in 2015); the link to José Cipriano needs the father's name first.

For the October El Salvador trip checklist:

  • Cementerio General: look for Juan José (d. 2016), Ricardo, Estela, Dagoberto graves — gravestones name spouses/children.
  • UES archives: Ricardo's dean file; Stela's 1945 doctorate record (first woman doctorate — there will be a file).
  • Diario Co Latino / Diario Oficial archives: full text of the Juan José inheritance edicts; Dagoberto's 1944 consul appointment.
  • Santa Ana parish: baptism records for all 10 siblings (~1910–1930) — names parents and godparents, and may reveal the tenth sibling's name.
  • Registro Nacional: death certificates for Dagoberto, Ricardo, Estela (name spouses/children).

Dead ends (online): Alba Marina, Rodolfo Eduardo — no traces; likely need parish/cemetery records in person. José Antonio and Juan Carlos — no evidence of descendants (both died young in 1944).

Tenorio's Maternal Surname

Coronel Jorge Tenorio — maternal surname and family link

Research date: 2026-10-07. Task: find his full name (maternal surname) and exact family connection to María Teresa González.

[Documented] What is established

The cousin relationship. María Teresa González's dedication in Pugna a un dictador (July 1987) names "mis estimados primos Doctor Salvador González Aguilar y al Coronel Jorge Tenorio, los dos ya fallecidos" — both cousins, both dead by July 1987. She writes of Tenorio: "no tengo palabras para agradecer todo lo que hizo por mis hijos y por mí, cuando yo suplicaba por la vida de mi esposo" — firsthand confirmation of his 1944 support. His wife comforted her ("siempre recibí consuelo de él y de su esposa"), but the wife is never named.

Military career (fully documented in coronel-jorge-tenorio-findings.md): 4th promotion Escuela Militar (~1932–33) alongside José María Lemus; captain 1944 (aligned with the post-Martínez junta, not a high official — matching the book's "no tenía ningún alto puesto"); major in the 14 Dec 1948 coup; colonel by ~1950; Director del Departamento del Censo; Director de la Policía Nacional (Aug 1957, the "ola difamatoria" scandal).

María Teresa's parents (1944 prison registry): Santiago González and Susana Jirón, of San Salvador.

[Unverified lead] The family-link hypotheses

Every public source — military histories, the 1948 coup rosters, the 1957 press, the book itself — gives only "Jorge Tenorio." No second surname exists online.

Hypothesis A (stronger): González line — Jorge Tenorio González. If his mother was a González, sister of Santiago González, he is María Teresa's first cousin through her father. Supporting logic: (1) Dr. Salvador González Aguilar — the other "primo" named in the same dedication breath — carries González paternally, placing him on Santiago's side; naming both cousins together suggests one family branch. (2) If both cousins descend from the González grandparents, Tenorio and González Aguilar are also cousins to each other, which the joint dedication fits naturally.

Hypothesis B (weaker): Jirón line — Jorge Tenorio Jirón. If his mother was a Jirón, sister of Susana Jirón. Weaker because Jirón is vanishingly rare in El Salvador (1 person per forebears.io) — a Tenorio-Jirón marriage would be a remarkable coincidence, though not impossible. Note the earlier research flagged the reverse: if the mother were Jirón, Tenorio would more likely be paternal-side; the logic cuts both ways without the marriage record.

Caveat: "Primo" in Salvadoran usage can stretch to second cousins or cousins by marriage. First cousin is the working hypothesis, not a fact.

[Documented] Negative findings (honest dead ends)

  • No source anywhere gives a maternal surname: not the 1948 coup histories, not the 1957 press coverage, not the book, not military rosters.
  • No obituary found (died before July 1987; exact date unknown).
  • Wife's name unknown; children unknown; no web trace of descendants.
  • The Diario Oficial digital archive (Imprenta Nacional) exposes only the current issue online; the 1950s tomos are not on Wikisource (which stops far earlier).
  • Mexican "Jorge Tenorio" hits (Campa, Tapia, Chávez, Salomón) are different people.

Documents that would resolve it (trip / in-person tasks)

  • Diario Oficial, 1957 — his appointment as Director de la Policía Nacional (executive agreement naming him in full). At the AGN or Imprenta Nacional archive.
  • AGN military personnel file — would give full name, birth date/place, parents' names.
  • Civil registry, San Salvador — his birth record (~1910–1912, inferred from ~1932–33 graduation; NOT confirmed) names both parents; his marriage record names his wife; his death record (before July 1987).
  • Marriage record of his parents — a Tenorio-González (or Tenorio-Jirón) marriage ~1900–1912 is the direct proof of Hypothesis A or B.
  • Dr. Salvador González Aguilar's papers — if his descendants hold correspondence with María Teresa, the cousin network may be mapped there.

Confidence summary

  • Cousin relationship to María Teresa: [Confirmed] (her own dedication).
  • 1944 support role: [Confirmed] (her firsthand account).
  • Military career outline: [Documented].
  • Maternal surname: [Unresolved] — no public source gives it.
  • Family link via González line: [Unverified lead] — Hypothesis A favored on parsimony, unproven.
  • Family link via Jirón line: [Unverified lead] — Hypothesis B, weaker.

The Tenth Sibling

> CORRECTION — 2026-10-07 (dad's identification, in-chat): The 1936 photo's "Juan Carlos" (back row, top middle) is the captain — they are the same man, so the double-count collapses. What follows below was written before this correction; treat the sibling-count arithmetic as under review. Dad disputes the earlier count entirely ("there weren't even that many"); the Flotilla Aérea article (written with Ricardo Gavidia, the family collaborator) says the captain was "el segundo de una numerosa generación de 10 hijos." Conflict now live: Flotilla=10 vs dad=fewer — pending dad's list.

The tenth sibling & Rodolfo Eduardo — gap report

Date: 2026-10-07

Status: 10th sibling still unidentified; Rodolfo Eduardo still traceless online

1. The "10 children" figure — confirmed source

The Flotilla Aérea article on the captain (written with Ricardo Gavidia, the family collaborator) states: the captain "fue el segundo de una numerosa generación de 10 hijos" — the second of ten children. [Documented — family-sourced]

Source: https://flotilla-aerea.com/2021/09/01/capitan-carlos-gavidia-castro-1915-1944/

2. Photo accounting (Dec 2, 1936)

The anniversary photo shows exactly 10 people, all named in the caption:

  • Back row (6): Rey Antonio, Alba Marina, Juan Carlos, Estela, Juan José, Dagoberto
  • Front row (4): Rodolfo Eduardo, José Gavidia (father), Carlota de Gavidia (mother), Ricardo

8 children pictured + 2 parents. NOT pictured: the captain (age 21, in military aviation service) and the 10th sibling.

Deduction: The 10th sibling was either (a) deceased before Dec 1936 — most likely, given infant/child mortality of the era, and the family still counts 10; (b) born after the photo; or (c) absent for another reason. No online record distinguishes these.

3. The 10th sibling — searches conducted, all negative

  • Full-text web search for sibling enumerations: none found beyond "10 hijos."
  • The book (Pugna a un dictador) does not enumerate the Gavidia siblings.
  • Family tree PDFs (Arbol_Genealogico_Gavidia_ES.pdf, Gavidia_Family_Tree.pdf) name only José/Juan José, José Antonio, and the captain among siblings.
  • No baptism, marriage, or death record found online for an unidentified Gavidia Castro child.
  • Definitive source: Santa Ana parish baptism index, 1911–1940, for children of José Gavidia + Carlota Castro — children would appear roughly every 2 years from ~1912. The FamilySearch Santa Ana collections (DGS 4092378 baptisms 1861–1923; later volumes to be located) are the trip task. A civil birth index 1879–1906 exists but predates the children.

4. Rodolfo Eduardo Gavidia Castro — searches conducted, all negative

  • "Rodolfo Eduardo Gavidia" / "Rodolfo Gavidia Castro": zero relevant hits.
  • "Rodolfo Gavidia" El Salvador Santa Ana: one hit — a "Rodolfo Gavidia" from San Salvador with a video résumé on CazVid (posted July 3, 2025), tourism/customer-service worker. [Unverified lead — age does not fit the sibling (born ~1931–32, would be 93+ in 2025); could be a younger namesake relative or unrelated. No link established. Do NOT assert identity.]
  • https://cazvid.com/cazume/rodolfo-gavidia-404149
  • No obituary, profession, marriage, or death record found.
  • In the 1936 photo he appears as a small boy (~4–6), suggesting birth ~1930–32.

5. Juan José obituary — none found

Searched Spanish-language obituary terms; only the two Diario Co Latino inheritance edicts (2019) document his death. No newspaper obituary located online. At 104, a published obituary may exist in print archives (El Diario de Hoy / La Prensa Gráfica, Sept 2016) — trip/library task.

6. Name discrepancy noted

The Flotilla Aérea article and the family tree call the eldest brother "José Gavidia Castro"; the Diario Co Latino legal edicts give "JUAN JOSÉ GAVIDIA CASTRO" (DUI 01870574-5). Full legal name is Juan José; "José" is the familiar form. The article also gives his age at death as 105 vs. the edict's 104 — minor discrepancy, use the edict.

7. Alba Marina — still no trace

"Alba Marina Gavidia" / "Alba Gavidia Castro": zero relevant hits (all noise). The remaining-names research identified her as "Betina" in the book, married to a "Mario" with two children — married surname unknown, which is why she vanishes from records.

Bottom line

| Gap | Online result | Resolution path |

|---|---|---|

| 10th sibling identity | Not found | Santa Ana parish baptisms 1911–1940 (trip) |

| Rodolfo Eduardo trace | Not found (one unverified younger namesake) | Parish/civil records; family ask |

| Juan José obituary | Not found | Print newspaper archives Sept 2016 (trip) |

| Alba Marina trace | Not found | Marriage record (unknown married surname) |

Best hypothesis for the 10th: a child who died young before Dec 1936 — counted in the family ten, absent from the photo, leaving only a parish baptism/burial record. The alternative (born after 1936) is less likely given the caption-writer's silence, but not ruled out.

The Book's Third Part — Decoded

The Third Part of Pugna a un dictador — mined for history

Date: 2026-10-07

Source: Full Spanish transcription, pugna-a-un-dictador/transcripcion/tercera-parte-1.md (pp. 137–176 + unnumbered epilogue)

Method: Complete read-through; cross-checked against Parts 1–2 for name continuity.

---

1. Historical scope

The Third Part covers roughly 1944 → 1974 — thirty years, from the aftermath of Carlos's execution to the 30th anniversary of his death ("cumplía treinta años de muerto," p. 176), which is the book's narrative present. The 1987 publication epilogue is appended.

It is a domestic-political chronicle, not a battle narrative: María's widowhood, her remarriage, her husband's rise under a reformist president, a later failed coup she helps defuse, and the family's long aftermath. The eras it touches:

  • ~1944–1947: Widowhood, courtship, remarriage.
  • 1950–1956: The reformist presidency (identified below as Óscar Osorio); Roberto Ocampo as Minister of War; a failed officers' coup (Muñiz/Velásquez).
  • 1956: The reformist president leaves office refusing re-election; the official-party candidate wins; Ocampo resigns and retires.
  • 1960s–1970s: Family life, silver anniversary (~1971–72), children grown.
  • 1974: 30th anniversary of Carlos's death.
  • Epilogue (1987 frame): Returns to 1944 — the general strike, Martínez's fall and assassination — and names the Council of War members with real names.

---

2. Reconstructed timeline [Family record + Analysis]

| Approx. date | Event |

|---|---|

| Apr 1944 | Carlos executed |

| ~1946 | Betina's wedding; María "dos años de viudez" (two years a widow) |

| ~1946–47 (Christmas season) | María–Roberto wedding (civil; the Governor officiates) |

| ~1948–49 | Elizabeth born (daughter with Roberto; cesarean) |

| 1950 | New President takes power; Roberto promoted Colonel, designated Minister of War, Navy and Aviation |

| Early 1950s | Sixth child (a son) born; three eldest sons sent to study in the US/Canada |

| During 1950–56 presidency | Muñiz/Velásquez coup attempt → death sentences → María intercedes → commuted to deportation |

| ~mid-1950s | María spends a year in the US (Carlitos's graduation) |

| 1956 | President finishes term, refuses re-election; official party wins; Roberto resigns as Minister, retires from the Army |

| Later | Roberto's father dies |

| ~1971–72 | Silver wedding anniversary (25 years); Coronel Muñiz — pardoned/returned — gives the tribute speech |

| 1974 | 30th anniversary of Carlos's death; book's present |

| 1987 | Book published |

---

3. The President — identification [Analysis, probable]

The unnamed President is almost certainly Coronel Óscar Osorio (president 1950–1956):

  • A young Colonel, "ex-compañero de Carlos" (Carlos's former classmate) — Osorio was a 1944-era officer.
  • "De cuna humilde pero honrada" (of humble but honorable birth); always first in his class from military school to his last promotion — matches Osorio's biography.
  • His wife founds daycares/charities ("guarderías infantiles," "Sociedad del Buen Corazón") — matches Osorio's social-reform presidency.
  • "Terminó su período con justicia y acierto, el pueblo deseaba la reelección, él... no aceptó... Se ciñó a la Constitución, sin permitir que la Asamblea la cambiara" — Osorio famously declined to extend his term and handed power to Lemus in 1956.
  • He tells María: "A Carlos lo aprecié, fue un gran compañero" — consistent with a fellow officer of Carlos's generation.

Confidence: probable. Needs one archival confirmation (e.g., the 1950 cabinet list naming the Minister of War).

---

4. Named figures

The Ocampo household
  • Coronel Roberto Ocampo — María's second husband. Doña Delia's son; Ana's ex-husband. Young officer in Parts 1–2 (already in love with María, married to Ana, rumored a government informant — María defends him). Courts María ~1946, marries her at Christmas, rises to Colonel and Minister of War under Osorio, resigns 1956, retires to administer properties. Dies after the book's present (not depicted).
  • Doña Delia — Roberto's mother. Present from Part 1 ("Doña Delia, la madre de Roberto," primera-parte-1.md:739) through Part 3. See §7 correction.
  • Ana — Roberto's first wife; they divorce; she marries a high Venezuelan diplomatic official who becomes Ambassador of Venezuela. Two sons with him. Beautiful, vindictive, torments María for decades (has the "Alejandra" waltz played to wound her). Her daughter with Roberto debuts in society engaged to the son of the Spanish Ambassador.
  • Roberto and Ana's daughter — unnamed; debutante; the Venezuelan Ambassador is her adoptive father figure.
María's family
  • Betina = Alba Marina Gavidia Castro (per the pseudonym key). Marries Mario, a surgeon ("tuvo que operar de urgencia"). Two children (a boy and a girl). Mario is "materialista" with tasteless jokes; María dislikes him.
  • Raúl — young engineer and painter, relative of Mario's, just returned from studying in the US. Paints María's famous portrait (blue dress, white hair). Never marries ("vivo de ilusiones, de imposibles"). A gentle unrequited-love thread.
  • Isabel — 20-year-old society beauty, green eyes, obsessively pursues Roberto.
  • José (y su esposa) — appears at Betina's wedding with his wife and two daughters ("dos niñas de José llevaban el ramo y el velo"). Identity unresolved — possibly a Gavidia relative or family friend. [Unresolved]
  • Rodolfo González — María's brother; witness at her wedding to Roberto. María's second son is named Rodolfo after him. [Family record]
María's children
  • Four with Carlos: Carlitos (eldest), Rodolfo (second), two more including "Robertito" (the youngest, "el chiquitín"). Three eldest study in the US/Canada.
  • Two with Roberto: Elizabeth (daughter, cesarean birth ~1948–49; marries an Army Captain) and a son (sixth child; "el retrato de su padre"; allowed at last to pursue a military career).
The later coup — Muñiz and Velásquez
  • Coronel Muñiz — co-leader ("cabecilla") of the failed officers' coup during the Osorio-era presidency. Sentenced to death by Extraordinary Council of War; María intercedes with the President; sentence commuted to deportation (same night, 8 PM). Survives, returns, and gives the tribute speech at Roberto and María's silver anniversary — "El Coronel Muñiz tomó la palabra... dió gracias a Dios y a ella por estar con vida." The arc closes: the man whose life she saved honors her 25 years later.
  • Coronel Velásquez — the other "cabecilla," same sentence, same commutation. This is NOT Doctor Velásquez (the 1944 defense lawyer, segunda-parte-1.md) — different man, different era (~1950s vs 1944). Named in María's quoted speech to the President ("condenaron a muerte al Coronel Muñiz y al Coronel Velásquez"), so per the book's method the surname is likely real. [Analysis]
  • The wife of Coronel Muñiz and the wife of Coronel Velásquez — come to María begging intercession; the Muñiz wife brings fruit baskets for years after.
  • Ten other officers deported.
Other named figures
  • Coronel Rodríguez — his wife heads the Sociedad de Señoras de Militares that honors María.
  • Doctor Vides — society figure at the reception.
  • The President's wife — charity leader (see §3).
  • The Japanese Ambassador (hosts a reception), the Spanish Ambassador (his son engaged to Roberto's daughter), the Venezuelan Ambassador (Ana's husband).
  • General Valdéz — recalled: "como Magdalena, regué con mis lágrimas los pies del General Valdéz" (her 1944 plea).
  • "Alejandra" — the waltz associated with Carlos and María; Ana weaponizes it.
Epilogue — the Council of War, real names [Documented — author's own list]

María Teresa prints the full membership of "el fatídico Consejo de Guerra":

  • Auditor de Guerra: Dr. Roberto Paredes ("hombre sanguinario")
  • Fiscal Militar del Centro: Coronel Andrés Eulalio Castillo (opposed commuting the last three death sentences)
  • Procurador General Militar: Dr. y Capitán Héctor Muñoz Barillas
  • Juez Militar: Dr. Bernardo Reyes
  • Vocales propietarios: General Luis Andreu ("hombre intachable" — she notes the puzzle of an honorable man signing), Coronel Santiago Ayala, Coronel Joaquín Fernán
  • Suplentes: General Francisco Ponce, Coronel José Garay, Coronel Darío Flores

Note Coronel Darío Flores among the suplentes — a Flores in the 1944 military apparatus; possible thread for the Flores Magaña puzzle. [Unverified lead]

The epilogue also gives her account of Martínez's end: fled to Guatemala then Honduras; assassinated by his own driver ("su propio motorista"); "una puñalada por cada uno de los ejecutados" — consistent with the Calvo Marroquín family's vengeance account (Cipriano Morales). [Family record / corroborated]

---

5. Coronel Velásquez and Coronel Muñiz — eras and roles

Both belong to the post-Martínez era, during the Osorio presidency (1950–1956) — NOT 1944:

  • They are "Jefes y Oficiales descontentos, unos retirados y otros trasladados" — officers purged or sidelined by Roberto's army reforms.
  • Tried the night of the attempt by Extraordinary Council of War; the two colonels sentenced to death ("serían pasados por las armas veinticuatro horas después"), ten officers deported.
  • María's intercession (direct audience with the President, over Roberto's objections — he voted for execution in council) gets both death sentences commuted to destierro (exile/deportation) at 3 PM the same day.
  • Muñiz reappears decades later, pardoned/returned, as honored speaker at the silver anniversary.

What real event is this? Unverified. There were conspiracy rumblings under Osorio, but no famous "Muñiz-Velásquez coup" is documented in the standard histories. Either a real minor plot or a dramatized composite. Deserves its own dig — Diario Oficial 1950–56, deportation decrees, and the names Muñiz/Velásquez in officer lists.

Do not conflate: Doctor Velásquez (1944, defense lawyer, probably a pseudonym) vs. Coronel Velásquez (1950s, coup leader, surname likely real per quoted speech).

---

6. María Teresa's later life as depicted

  • Widowhood (~1944–47): Lives for her children and Carlos's memory; "su única salida era al cementerio." Roberto — in love with her since youth ("desde niño supe tus medidas") — waits.
  • The proposal and conditions: She accepts on terms: a new house with no memories ("Quinta Santa María"), no official parties or military casinos ("siento repugnancia por los militares"), and that he be a father to her children. Civil wedding, Governor officiating; witnesses her brother Rodolfo and Mario.
  • Marriage: Genuinely happy ("el hombre más feliz del mundo"); two children; she tells Carlos's grave asking forgiveness ("yo imploro tu perdón por no haberte sido fiel hasta el fin").
  • The moral climax: She defies her own husband the Minister to save Muñiz and Velásquez from the firing squad — reliving 1944 from the other side of power. "Tu espíritu me ayudó a salvar dos vidas" (to Carlos at his grave).
  • The breach and repair: Roberto votes for the executions in council; María goes to the US for a year; they reconcile. He resigns in 1956 and retires; she is "contentísima."
  • Old age: Grandchildren, the silver anniversary, the 30th anniversary Mass at Carlos's tomb. The book closes: "hogar, dulce hogar, al lado de mi mujercita adorada."
  • 1987 present: Widow (presumably) of Ocampo, publishing at last with Dr. González Aguilar's posthumous encouragement.

---

7. CORRECTION to prior research: the two Robertos

Prior claim (remaining-names.md) was wrong. It listed "Doña Delia" as "Flores Magaña's mother" and glossed "(Roberto Flores)."

The book actually contains TWO different Robertos:

  • Roberto Ocampo — Doña Delia's son; Ana's husband/ex-husband; María's second husband. Called "Roberto" throughout Parts 1–3; surname revealed via "Capitán Ocampo" (segunda-parte-1.md:240, 336, 374) and "el Coronel Ocampo" / "los esposos Ocampo" (Part 3). Doña Delia is HIS mother — stated explicitly in Part 1 ("Doña Delia, la madre de Roberto").
  • Capitán Roberto Flores Magaña — Marta Pérez's boyfriend/husband; named once in the quoted death sentence (segunda-parte-1.md:198). Per the quoted-speech rule, "Roberto Flores Magaña" is likely his real name. He is executed; Marta is widowed.

Evidence they are distinct: Part 1 establishes Marta has her own "novio" (boyfriend) separate from Roberto (primera-parte-1.md:784: "lo mismo debe haber hecho con su novio"). Roberto is married to Ana; Marta's man is Flores.

Downstream corrections needed:

  • remaining-names.md — "Doña Delia" entry: she is Roberto Ocampo's mother, not Flores Magaña's. Her real identity = the mother of Coronel Roberto Ocampo (a documented officer — findable).
  • remaining-names.md — "Ana Mercedes" entry: introduced by Doña Delia as Roberto's cousin ("tu prima") — that is Ocampo's cousin, not Flores's.
  • pseudonym-key.md — should note the two-Roberto distinction explicitly; it strengthens the key (Ocampo confirmed real via quoted speech; Flores Magaña real via quoted verdict).

---

8. Figures/events deserving their own digs

  • Confirm the Osorio identification — 1950 cabinet list; was Ocampo actually Minister of War under Osorio? (Diario Oficial 1950–56.)
  • The Muñiz/Velásquez coup — what real event, if any? Search officer lists and deportation decrees 1950–56.
  • The Council of War members — ten real names María Teresa printed. Who were Paredes, Castillo, Muñoz Barillas, Reyes, Andreu, Ayala, Fernán, Ponce, Garay, and Darío Flores? (The Flores thread.)
  • Doña Delia Ocampo — mother of a documented colonel/minister; find her full name via Roberto Ocampo's records.
  • "José" with wife and two daughters — wedding party figure; possibly a Gavidia relative.
  • Ana's Venezuelan ambassador husband — identifies the family Ana married into.
  • "Alejandra" the waltz — Carlos and María's waltz; already linked in the audiobook notes.

---

Evidence-strength summary

Two Robertos (Ocampo vs. Flores Magaña) [Documented — book text] · Doña Delia = Ocampo's mother [Documented — book text] · President = Óscar Osorio [Analysis, probable] · Muñiz/Velásquez coup [Family record; historical correlate unverified] · Council of War roster [Documented — author's list] · Martínez assassination account [Family record, corroborated] · Timeline dates [Analysis from internal book evidence] · "José" identity [Unresolved]

Tirana Memoria — Literary Corroboration

Tirana memoria — mining Castellanos Moya's novel for Gavidia connections

Date: 2026-10-07

Task: Investigate Horacio Castellanos Moya's novel Tirana memoria for connections to the Gavidia family story.

Status: Research complete from published fragments, scholarly analyses, and reviews. The full novel text was consulted only via a publicly posted excerpt (epdlp.com) and search-result passages; no pirated full text was relied on as a source to cite.

1. The novel — publication facts [Documented]

  • Tirana memoria, Horacio Castellanos Moya (b. 1957, Honduran-Salvadoran), first published 2008 (Tusquets; 360 pp.). Reissued by Random House (2021/2024). English translation Tyrant Memory.
  • Third panel of the Aragón family saga, after Donde no estén ustedes (2003) and Desmoronamiento (2006). Characters (El Vikingo, María Elena) carry across novels.
  • Narrated as the diary of Haydée, wife of imprisoned communist journalist Pericles Aragón, covering the April 1944 coup and the May "huelga de brazos caídos."
  • The dictator is never named — only "el presidente," "el brujo nazi," "el hombre." [Documented — Revista de Libros review, 2009]
  • The author's own epigraph to the project, quoted in scholarship: "No se busque aquí, pues, la 'verdad histórica'." [Scholarship — Mackenbach, Ayer 97/2015]

2. What the novel says about Sánchez Dueñas and the Gavidia brothers

Source: the novel's published excerpt (Haydée's diary entry dated Friday, April 14, 1944 — four days after the April 10 executions), summarized here in paraphrase:

  • Haydée meets "Merceditas," 23, mother of two, wife of "el capitán Gavidia." Antonio's execution ("el fusilamiento de Antonio") has devastated her; she hopes American pressure may still save her husband, citing his top marks in a US military training course.
  • Merceditas tells Haydée that the Gavidia brothers were convinced to join the coup by Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, who was executed together with Antonio.
  • Sánchez Dueñas is presented as "el verdadero organizador del golpe militar": a promotion leader, discharged for insubordination about two years earlier, under an arrest warrant, exiled — then returned secretly at Christmas to organize the conspiracy.
  • His hideout: a finca north of the city called "la Layco," property of "Mariíta Loucel," where he met with fellow captains of his promotion, including Merceditas's husband.
  • Haydée is left wondering whether Mariíta Loucel, not Sánchez Dueñas, was the real organizer.

[Source: published excerpt of the novel. Treat as literary depiction, not testimony.]

3. Character-by-character analysis

"El capitán Gavidia" [Novel — probable = Capitán Carlos Gavidia Castro]
  • Never given a first name in the excerpt. The identification rests on: rank (captain), brother Antonio executed April 10, and the documented fact that Carlos was the captain among the Gavidia brothers in the plot. Probable, not certain — the novel may composite.
"Antonio" [Novel — maps to Teniente Antonio Gavidia Castro]
  • Executed ("fusilado") before the April 14 diary entry; executed together with Sánchez Dueñas. Matches the documented April 10, 1944 executions at the Cementerio General, where the author's own front-matter roster names Sánchez Dueñas first and Antonio Gavidia Castro among the tenientes. [Documented alignment]
"Merceditas," the captain's wife [Novel — fictional/composite]
  • 23 years old, mother of two children. The real María Teresa González was ~27 in 1944 with four sons. The mismatch is significant — Merceditas is not a portrait of María Teresa.
  • Possible source of the name: Merceditas Ros, the documented niece of the Calvo Marroquín (7 years old in 1944, the living witness of the vengeance story). Castellanos Moya may have borrowed a resonant period name. [Unverified lead — speculative]
  • Function in the novel: the military wife as information conduit — she "knows details only the military know," mirroring how officers' wives actually circulated news in April 1944.
"Mariíta Loucel" and finca "la Layco" [Novel — new thread]
  • María Loucel is a documented historical figure: the poet who seized the YSP radio microphones alongside Matilde Elena López on April 2, 1944 (see civilian-network.md). Castellanos Moya takes this real person and elevates her to the conspiracy's host — and Haydée speculates she may have been its true organizer.
  • "Layco" is a real place: Colonia Layco exists in San Salvador (near the UES/national university zone, north of the historic center) — consistent with "una finca al norte de la ciudad." [Documented — map/cartographic sources]
  • Neither the Loucel-as-host nor the Layco finca appears in María Teresa's account or in the family research. This is either Castellanos Moya's invention or a thread from his own sources. Flagged as a new research thread, not a fact.
The US training detail [Novel — unverified]
  • The novel says the captain earned top marks in a US training course and that American officers took a liking to him — Merceditas's hope for US intercession rests on it.
  • No corroboration found in any family record, the book transcription, or documented sources. Salvadoran officers did train in the US in this era, so it is plausible, but it is currently novel-only. [Unverified lead]

4. Comparison with María Teresa's account (Pugna a un dictador)

Agreements:

  • A single officer-recruiter as the plot's true organizer who personally drew the Gavidias in. María Teresa calls him "Capitán Flores (Magaña)"; Castellanos Moya calls him Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas, by his real name. The profiles match: promotion leader, discharged ~2 years before (~1942), exiled, returned secretly at Christmas 1943.
  • The Gavidia brothers as recruited participants, not originators.
  • Antonio executed April 10 alongside the organizer.
  • The April 1944 atmosphere: arrests, prison visits, wives networking for information and pardons.

Contradictions / divergences:

  • María Teresa shields the organizer behind the pseudonym "Flores Magaña"; Castellanos Moya, writing fiction 64 years later with no living to protect, uses the real name. This is the single most important convergence in the whole investigation: two independent literary tracks, 21 years apart, identifying the same man as the organizer.
  • The novel's captain's wife (Merceditas, 23, two children) vs. the real María Teresa (~27, four sons).
  • The novel's Loucel/Layco conspiracy hub has no counterpart in María Teresa's account.
  • María Teresa's "Manuel" (from Marta's quoted speech, "¿Qué te pasa Manuel?") vs. the novel's full "Manuel Sánchez Dueñas" — consistent, mutually reinforcing.

Weight of the convergence: Castellanos Moya's documented scholarly sources (see §5) do not include Pugna a un dictador. If he reached Sánchez Dueñas independently through Parkman/Castellanos historiography, the organizer identification is doubly attested. If he knew María Teresa's book (possible — he is Salvadoran, deeply researched, and the book existed since 1987), it is singly attested but still significant as literary corroboration. Either way it strengthens, but does not prove, the Flores Magaña = Manuel Sánchez Dueñas identification. [Analysis]

5. Castellanos Moya's sources [Scholarship]

From Werner Mackenbach's peer-reviewed study "Historia, memoria y ficción. Tirana Memoria" (Ayer 97/2015, pp. 83–111):

  • Patricia Parkman's book on the 1944 insurrection (the "Nota introductoria" by Gilberto Aguilar Avilés is cited for its chronology) — i.e., Parkman's Nonviolent Insurrection in El Salvador (2004), the standard English-language history of the May huelga.
  • Juan Mario Castellanos, El Salvador, 1930–1960. Antecedentes históricos de la guerra civil — specifically its subchapter on the April 2, 1944 insurrection and the May general strike.
  • No citation of Pugna a un dictador found in the scholarship surveyed. Whether Castellanos Moya knew María Teresa's book is unknown. [Unverified]

Additional scholarly reception: Alberto Fonseca's "Tirana memoria de Horacio Castellanos Moya" (Istmo, Denison); Revista de Libros review "Lápidas y corolarios" (2009).

6. Other literary or historical works depicting the Gavidia brothers

  • None found. No other novel, memoir, or history located in this research names or depicts Carlos or José Antonio Gavidia Castro as characters. Tirana memoria appears to be the only literary work in which the Gavidia brothers appear.
  • The brothers appear in documented sources only: the author's front-matter execution lists, the April 1944 rosters, and the family book itself.

7. New research threads opened by this dig

  • Finca "la Layco" / Mariíta Loucel — real place (Colonia Layco, San Salvador), real poet (María Loucel, YSP radio). Did the Loucel family host conspirators? Was there a finca? Check against 1944 sources; ask whether the family knows the Loucels.
  • US training — did Capitán Carlos train in the United States? His AGN personnel file would list courses. If true, it explains the novel's "American pressure" hope and adds a biographical detail.
  • "Merceditas" — confirm she is purely fictional; check whether any Gavidia-adjacent woman of 1944 matches (23, two children, captain's wife). If none, close the thread.
  • Castellanos Moya's archive — he is living (b. 1957). His papers/notes for the novel might name his sources on Sánchez Dueñas. No contact without Carlos's approval — flagged only.

8. Bottom line

Tirana memoria (2008) independently casts Capitán Manuel Sánchez Dueñas as the April 1944 plot's true organizer — promotion leader, discharged ~1942, exiled, back at Christmas 1943 — who personally recruited the Gavidia brothers, and has him executed alongside Antonio on April 10. This mirrors María Teresa's "Capitán Flores" point for point, except it uses the real name. The novel's scholarly sources (Parkman, Juan Mario Castellanos) do not include her book, making this either independent corroboration or, at minimum, serious literary corroboration of the Flores Magaña = Manuel Sánchez Dueñas identification. The novel also plants two new threads — the Loucel/Layco conspiracy hub and the captain's US training — both currently unverified.

Evidence-strength summary

Novel's 2008 publication, saga placement, diary structure [Documented] · Sánchez Dueñas as organizer/recruiter in the novel [Novel — from published excerpt] · "El capitán Gavidia" = Carlos [Probable — rank and brother match] · "Antonio" = Antonio Gavidia Castro [Documented alignment — April 10 roster] · "Merceditas" as fictional/composite wife [Analysis — age/children mismatch] · Mariíta Loucel ≈ María Loucel the poet [Probable — name and period fit] · Layco as real San Salvador place [Documented] · Loucel/Layco as conspiracy hub [Novel — unverified] · US training [Novel — unverified] · Castellanos Moya's sources (Parkman, J.M. Castellanos) [Scholarship] · No Pugna citation in his sources [Unverified] · Convergence strengthening the Sánchez Dueñas identification [Analysis] · No other literary depictions of the brothers [Negative search result]

Tito Tomás Calvo

Tito Tomás Calvo — Deep Dig Report

Bottom line

Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo was not just a ringleader of the April 1944 uprising — he was Martínez's compadre, one of the most bloodthirsty officers of the 1932 Matanza, and a member of the Calvo Marroquín family of Izalco that lost at least five members to Martínez's firing squads and death sentences in April 1944. The family's surviving niece says Martínez's own assassination in Honduras was vengeance for what he did to them.

Documented biography

  • Name: Tito Tomás Calvo (all sources use "Tito" — no source clarifies whether it is a given name or nickname) [Documented]
  • Rank trajectory: "Capitán mayor" in January 1932 (moved a regiment with machine guns and 66 guards to the western zone during the uprising — UNAM/Diario del Salvador account); Coronel by 1944 [Documented]
  • Position: Commander and captain of the port of Acajutla; compadre of General Hernández Martínez [Documented — "Los Izalcos. Testimonio de un indígena," Julio Leiva Masín, Editorial Universitaria UES]
  • 1932 role: One of the military officers Martínez entrusted with crushing the western uprising. Brought soldiers from Chalatenango and San Miguel to Izalco (Ama family testimony). 1932 survivors call him one of the most bloodthirsty in the repression; his loyalty earned him a position of trust in the Armed Forces [Documented]
  • 1944 role: The regime's own Diario Oficial (No. 78, April 4, 1944) names him alongside Dr. Arturo Romero as an organizer: "El Dr Arturo Romero y el oficial Coronel Tito Tomás Calvo organizaron una rebelión contra las autoridades legalmente constituidas." A later account says the failed plan was drawn up by Coronels Tito Calvo and Alfredo Aguilar, accepted by civilian leaders Romero and Agustín Alfaro Morán [Documented]
  • The turn: "En sus últimos años se opuso al dictador, participó en el golpe de Estado y fue fusilado por su entrañable amigo y compadre" [Documented — Leiva Masín]
  • Arrest: Sought US Embassy asylum; it was denied. Arrested with Marroquín [Documented]
  • Execution: April 10, 1944, in the Policía Nacional courtyard, after prior degradation, before 800+ political prisoners [Documented]

The Calvo Marroquín family

The family compound surname is Calvo Marroquín, from Izalco (house on Avenida Morazán, between Tercera and Quinta Calle Oriente). [Documented — Leiva Masín]

| Member | Fate |

|---|---|

| Tito Tomás Calvo Marroquín (Coronel) | Executed April 10, 1944, Policía Nacional; remains in San Salvador |

| Francisco Alfonso Marroquín (General) | Executed April 10, 1944, Policía Nacional; remains in San Salvador; maternal half-brother of Tito and Marcelino; compadre of the president |

| Marcelino Calvo (Teniente) | Executed April 10, 1944, Cementerio General; remains in Izalco |

| Mario Calvo Marroquín (Doctor) | Death sentence (in absentia list, April 11) |

| Tomás Calvo (Doctor) | Death sentence (in absentia list, April 11); brother of Mario |

| Emilia and Victoria | Sisters; wore mourning for life in honor of their brothers |

| Merceditas Ros | Niece (sobrina); 7 years old in 1944; visited the uncles' house daily; the living witness behind the family testimony |

Correction to earlier research: the book's "three brothers" device (Gutiérrez brothers = Marroquín/Calvo/Sosa) was judged fictional — but the reality is stranger: Marroquín really was the half-brother of Tito and Marcelino Calvo. The book got the fraternity half-right and the membership wrong (Sosa was not kin).

The vengeance coda

Martínez fled to Honduras after May 1944 and was assassinated there as an 84-year-old by a worker named Cipriano Morales. Per Merceditas Ros: Morales was the godson of one of the executed Calvo Marroquín (she wasn't sure whether Alfonso's or Tito's), raised in their house, where one of his parents had worked for years. "La muerte de Martínez fue una venganza por lo que el dictador hizo contra los Calvo Marroquín." The family interpreted Martínez's fall as vindication for their murdered members. [Family testimony via Leiva Masín — treat as the family's account, not independently corroborated]

In "Pugna a un dictador"

Tito appears fused into "General Tomás Gutiérrez, líder de la Revolución" — the book merged him with General Marroquín and promoted Tomás to General. "Coronel Lorenzo Gutiérrez" is also Tito. The fusion obscures the real history: two maternal half-brothers, both compadres of the president they tried to overthrow. [Probable — per pseudonym key]

The Gavidia parallel

The Calvo Marroquín family is the closest parallel to the Gavidias: multiple brothers/half-brothers executed the same morning (April 10), a family that rose together. The difference: the Calvos had been the regime's enforcers in 1932 before turning on it; the Gavidias were never regime insiders.

Unresolved

  • Whether "Tito" is a given name or nickname (no source clarifies)
  • Birth date/place (likely Izalco; unconfirmed)
  • Full names with second surnames for all five condemned/sentenced members
  • Living descendants: Merceditas Ros was the witness; no current trace found. The Ros family of Izalco is the thread.
  • Memorials: no documented street/plaque found; burial locations known (San Salvador for Tito and Alfonso; Izalco for Marcelino)

Best next documents (trip tasks)

  • Izalco civil registry — Calvo Marroquín births (~1890–1910), Tito's full name and birth date
  • Cementerio General (San Salvador) — Tito and Alfonso burial records
  • Izalco cemetery — Marcelino's burial
  • AGN — Tito's personnel file; the trial dossier (his testimony)
  • Diario Oficial 1932 — his Matanza-era appointments and rank
  • Izalco — the family house on Avenida Morazán; ask after the Ros family

Velasquez

The Two Velásquez Figures in "Pugna a un dictador"

Critical framing: they are from DIFFERENT eras

The book's two Velásquez figures do not belong to the same time period. This is the single most important finding.

---

1. DOCTOR VELÁSQUEZ — the 1944 defense lawyer

Role in the book [Family record]: Lead defense counsel for the accused in the April 1944 Councils of War. He defends the soldiers (tropa) first, then the civilians and officers. His speeches are quoted at length (Segunda Parte, pp. 112–118 of the book).

What the book says about him:

  • "El Doctor Velásquez que era el Abogado más brillante de la época empezó la defensa, con énfasis e indignación." (The most brilliant lawyer of the era.)
  • "El Abogado, persona de gran talento y conocida honorabilidad" (a person of great talent and known honorability).
  • His quoted defense speeches run for pages — he argues the soldiers are "del pueblo," pleads with the jury as "hermanos de armas," invokes the doctors among the accused (notably "el Doctor Funes, especialista en niños").
  • He leads "los defensores" (plural) — there was a team, he was its head.

The "Velásquez y Velásquez" question [Probable typo]: At one point the book reads "Por último el Doctor Velásquez y Velásquez comprendió que sus compañeros habían perdido toda esperanza." This is the ONLY occurrence of the doubled surname in the entire book (~94,000 words). Everywhere else he is simply "Doctor Velásquez." Most likely a typographical duplication, not his real double surname. Do not treat "Velásquez y Velásquez" as his name without corroboration.

Is "Velásquez" his real surname? [Unresolved, leaning pseudonym]:

  • He appears only in NARRATIVE, never in quoted speech. Per the book's established method (real names in quotes, pseudonyms in narrative), his name may be changed.
  • The author's note states she changed the names of Council of War members AND all protagonists. The Doctor is a heroic protagonist.
  • Counterpoint: she may have kept the surname of a hero while changing the first name, or kept it whole as an honor. Cannot determine from the book alone.
  • No first name appears anywhere in the book.

Web trace: None. No Salvadoran lawyer named Velásquez from the 1940s identified online. The October 1944 trial defender list (UES journal, "El golpe del 21 de octubre de 1944") names eight lawyers — no Velásquez — but that is a different trial (October, not April).

Best next documents (trip tasks):

  • The April 1944 trial dossier at the AGN (names all defense counsel — definitive).
  • Diario Oficial, April–May 1944 (Council of War proceedings).
  • UES Facultad de Jurisprudencia archives — "the most brilliant lawyer of the era" should appear in faculty history or bar records.

---

2. CORONEL VELÁSQUEZ — condemned in the Tercera Parte (post-1944)

Role in the book [Family record]: A colonel condemned to death as one of "los dos Coroneles, Jefes del Complot" (the two colonels, leaders of the plot), alongside Coronel Muñiz, in a failed military coup described in the Tercera Parte.

When is the Tercera Parte set? [Analysis]: Clearly AFTER 1944. Key markers:

  • María is married to Roberto (Ocampo), who is a minister ("después del Presidente soy yo el que tiene el mando").
  • "El nuevo Presidente era un Coronel joven, ex-compañero de Carlos" (the new president was a young colonel, Carlos's former classmate).
  • María references "la caída del Presidente Hernández Martínez" as past history.
  • This is a different government, a different coup, years after 1944. The exact historical coup is not identified — possibly 1950s–60s, or fictionalized.

Is "Coronel Velásquez" his real name? [Probable real surname]: He is named in María's DIRECT QUOTED SPEECH: "me acabo de enterar que condenaron a muerte al Coronel Muñiz y al Coronel Velásquez, es cierto Coronel?" Per the book's method (real names in quotes), the surname Velásquez is likely genuine. His wife ("la señora de Velásquez") visits María with the wife of Muñiz.

Web trace: None. No Coronel Velásquez identified as executed/condemned in post-1944 El Salvador.

Best next documents: Identify which historical coup the Tercera Parte describes, then check its trial records.

---

3. Are they related?

[Unverified lead / speculation]: Both carry the surname Velásquez, but they belong to different eras (1944 vs. post-1944, possibly decades apart). Possibilities:

  • Father and son (the lawyer's son becomes a colonel who rebels a generation later) — dramatically fitting, zero evidence.
  • Coincidence of a common surname.
  • The Doctor's "Velásquez" is a pseudonym and there is no connection at all.

Do not assert a relationship without documentation.

---

4. Bottom line

| Figure | Era | Status | Real name? |

|---|---|---|---|

| Doctor Velásquez | April 1944 | Defense lawyer, "most brilliant of the era" | [Unresolved] — narrative-only, may be pseudonym; no first name |

| Coronel Velásquez | Post-1944 (Tercera Parte) | Condemned coup ringleader | [Probable] real surname — named in quoted speech |

Neither is identified. The April 1944 trial dossier at the AGN is the single document that names the Doctor. The Coronel requires first identifying the Tercera Parte's historical coup.
